Parallel ( 1 + ϵ ) (1+\epsilon) -Approximate Multi-Commodity Mincost Flow in Almost Optimal Depth and Work
Bernhard Haeupler, Yonggang Jiang, Yaowei Long, Thatchaphol Saranurak, Shengzhe Wang

Abstract
We present a parallel algorithm for computing ( 1 + ϵ ) (1+\epsilon) -approximate mincost flow on an undirected graph with m m edges, where capacities and costs are assigned to both edges and vertices. Our algorithm achieves O ^ ​ ( m ) \hat{O}(m) work and O ^ ​ ( 1 ) \hat{O}(1) depth when ϵ > 1 / polylog ⁡ ( m ) \epsilon>1/\mathrm{polylog}(m) , making both the work and depth almost optimal, up to a subpolynomial factor. Previous algorithms with O ^ ​ ( m ) \hat{O}(m) work required Ω ⁡ ( m ) \Omega(m) depth, even for special cases of mincost flow with only edge capacities or max flow with vertex capacities. Our result generalizes prior almost-optimal parallel ( 1 + ϵ ) (1+\epsilon) -approximation algorithms for these special cases, including shortest paths [ Li20 , ASZ20 , RGH+22 ] and max flow with only edge capacities [ LNPS23 , AKL+24 ] . Our key technical contribution is the first construction of length-constrained flow shortcuts with ( 1 + ϵ ) (1+\epsilon) length slack, O ^ ​ ( 1 ) \hat{O}(1) congestion slack, and O ^ ​ ( 1 ) \hat{O}(1) step bound. This provides a strict generalization of the influential concept of ( O ^ ​ ( 1 ) , ϵ ) (\hat{O}(1),\epsilon) -hopsets [ Coh00 ] , allowing for additional control over congestion. Previous length-constrained flow shortcuts [ HHL+24 ] incur a large constant in the length slack, which would lead to a large approximation factor. To enable our flow algorithms to work under vertex capacities, we also develop a close-to-linear time algorithm for computing length-constrained vertex expander decomposition. Building on Cohen’s idea of path-count flows [ Coh95 ] , we further extend our algorithm to solve ( 1 + ϵ ) (1+\epsilon) -approximate k k -commodity mincost flow problems with almost-optimal O ^ ​ ( m ​ k ) \hat{O}(mk) work and O ^ ​ ( 1 ) \hat{O}(1) depth, independent of the number of commodities k k .



1
Introduction

The
mincost flow
problem is one of the most fundamental combinatorial optimization problems and has been extensively studied since the 60s
[
Ful61
,
EK72
,
Tar85
,
Orl84
,
GT88
,
GT90
,
AGOT92
,
Orl93
,
Orl97
,
DS08
,
vdBLL+21
,
AMV21
,
vdBGJ+22
]
, which recently culminates in the breakthrough almost-linear-time algorithm
[
CKL+22
]
. However, the aforementioned algorithms were mainly designed in the
sequential
setting, and generally cannot be parallelized in an efficient way.

This motivates the next natural goal of obtaining
parallel
algorithms with almost-optimal
1
1

1

By convention, the term
almost
means a subpolynomial overhead, e.g. almost-linear in
n
means
n^{1+o(1)}
. Moreover,
nearly
means a polylogarithmic overhead, and
close-to
means an
n^{\epsilon}
overhead. We use
\tilde{O}(\cdot)
and
\hat{O}(\cdot)
to hide polylogarithmic and subpolynomial factors.
work and depth. Previously, there have been exciting achievements for two special cases of the mincost flow problem, the
s
-
t
shortest path
and
maximum flow
problems, in undirected graphs. For shortest paths, the 25-year-old breakthrough by
[
Coh00
]
showed the first
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate algorithm with almost-linear work and subpolynomial depth, and recently,
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate algorithms with nearly-linear work and polylogarithmic depth were developed
[
Li20
,
ASZ20
,
RGH+22
]
.
For maximum flows, the state-of-the-art
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate algorithm also attained nearly-linear work and polylogarithmic depth
[
AKL+24
]
.
However, there is still no comparable result for either

1.

the more generalized
mincost flow
problem in undirected graphs (with edge capacities), or

2.

still the maximum flow problem but in undirected graphs with
vertex capacities
.

Solving either of the above problems will be challenging, and in particular, the techniques in the aforementioned works cannot be directly transferred to either of these two problems. The mincost flow problem requires a joint
\ell_{1}
(lengths/costs) and
\ell_{\infty}
(congestion) optimization, while previous techniques including hopsets
[
Coh00
]
,
\ell_{1}
-oblivious routings
[
Li20
,
ASZ20
,
RGH+22
]
and congestion approximators based on
\ell_{\infty}
-oblivious routings
[
LNPS23
,
AKL+24
]
only capture either
\ell_{1}
or
\ell_{\infty}
information. Also, in vertex-capacitated undirected graphs, it is known that good
\ell_{\infty}
-oblivious routing for vertex-capacitated graphs does
not
exist
[
HKRL07
]
. This gives a fundamental barrier to previous oblivious-routing-based techniques
[
AKL+24
]
. To summarize, the following natural question is still open, and answering it may require new approaches.

Are there parallel
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate algorithms for either problems
1
or
2

with almost-linear work and subpolynomial depth?

1.1
Our Results

We answer the above open question affirmatively. In particular, we present the first parallel
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate mincost flow algorithm with almost-linear work and subpolynomial depth for undirected graphs with vertex capacities and costs, which solves problems
1
and
2
simultaneously.

Formally, consider an
n
-vertex
m
-edge graph
G=(V,E)
with non-negative polynomially-bounded integral capacities and costs on both edges
and vertices
. Let
B
be a given number representing the cost budget and
s,t\in V
be two given vertices. The
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate mincost flow problem asks for a
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate maximum flow among all flows that have total cost at most
B
and respect the capacity on each edge and vertex. Our result is formally stated in
Theorem

1.1
.

1.1
.

Given a graph
G
, cost budget
B
, two vertices
s,t\in V
, and a precision parameter
1/\mathrm{polylog}(n)<\epsilon<1
, there exists a parallel
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate mincost flow algorithm with
\hat{O}(m)
work and
\hat{O}(1)
depth.

Barriers towards Exact Algorithms or Directed Graphs.

It is noteworthy that simultaneously working in undirected graphs (with vertex capacities and costs) and allowing
(1+\epsilon)
-approximation is the strongest setting in which efficient parallel mincost flow algorithms may be achievable based on current knowledge. Considering either
directed
graphs or
exact
solutions will run up against the following long-standing barriers.

•

The parallel (approximate) mincost flow problem in
directed
graphs subsumes the basic
parallel

s
-
t
reachability
problem. Parallel
s
-
t
reachability is a big open problem studied over decades and we are still far from knowing whether algorithms with almost-linear work and subpolynomial depth exist. The state-of-the-art almost-linear-work algorithm has
O(n^{0.5+o(1)})
depth
[
JLS19
]
.

•

An efficient parallel
exact
mincost flow in undirected graphs faces the barrier even for two special cases: the
maximum flow
and
s
-
t
shortest path
problems. Computing an exact maximum flow in undirected graphs is as hard as in directed graphs
[
Mąd11
]
, so it again suffers from the aforementioned parallel reachability barrier. For the
s
-
t
shortest path problem, even in the simplest undirected, unweighted graphs, the state-of-the-art almost-linear-work algorithms still have
O(n^{0.5+o(1)})
depth
[
RHM+23
,
CF23
]
.

Indeed, there have been attempts to design parallel exact mincost flow algorithms in directed graphs, but they either suffer from a large polynomial work
[
OS93
]
or get stuck in roughly
\sqrt{n}
depth
[
LS14
,
vdBGJdV25
]
.

Multi-Commodity Mincost Flows.

There has been a long line of work
[
SM90
,
LSM+91
,
Rad96
,
GK96
,
Nes09
,
Mad10
,
KMP12
,
She17
,
CLS21
,
CS21
,
CY24
,
CGS25
]
devoted to fast algorithms for computing
multi-commodity flow
. See
Section

7
for the formal problem statements.

Quite recently, algorithms for multi-commodity flow with near-optimal work have been discovered. In the discussion below, we assume that
\epsilon>1/\mathrm{polylog}(m)
. The first algorithm, presented by Sherman
[
She17
]
, is a
(1+\epsilon)
-approximation algorithm for computing
k
-commodity concurrent flow, achieving near-optimal
\tilde{O}(mk)
work. An alternative approach is to apply known reductions from
[
Rad96
,
GK96
]
, which reduce the problem of
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate
k
-commodity concurrent (mincost) flow to
\tilde{O}(k/\epsilon^{2})
calls of
single-commodity

(1+\epsilon)
-approximate mincost flow. Plugging in the recent exact
\hat{O}(m)
-time mincost flow algorithm
[
CKL+22
]
, this also yields an algorithm with
\hat{O}(mk)
work. Unfortunately, none of these approaches provide parallel algorithms with non-trivial sublinear depth.

Notably, by applying
Theorem

1.1
to the above reduction, we immediately obtain a
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate
k
-commodity concurrent (mincost) flow algorithm with
\hat{O}(mk)
work and
\hat{O}(k)
depth. To our knowledge, this already gives the first parallel multi-commodity flow algorithm with almost-optimal work and non-trivial depth, motivating us to further investigate whether
\hat{O}(k)
depth is inherent.

Is there a parallel
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate multi-commodity flow algorithm with almost-optimal work and depth that is sublinear in the number of commodities
k
?

Conceptually, this question asks how much different commodities in a near-optimal flow must “depend” on each other. To our knowledge, the only related previous work is
[
HHL+24
]
, which shows a parallel
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}
-approximation algorithm with
O((m+k)^{1+\epsilon})
work
2
2

2

In fact, when the algorithm is required to output a flow solution explicitly, there is an
\Omega(mk)
work lower bound due to the output size. Their algorithm bypasses this by outputting a flow solution
implicitly
via a flow oracle.
and
O((m+k)^{\epsilon})
depth for
k
commodities and arbitrary constant
\epsilon>0
. The significant drawback of
[
HHL+24
]
is that its approximation factor is a larger constant, significantly above
1+\epsilon
, and thus unsatisfactory in our context.

We provide a strong affirmative answer to the above question by showing an almost optimal algorithm with depth
\hat{O}(1)
:

1.2
.

Let
G
be an undirected graph with non-negative polynomially-bounded integral costs and capacities. Given a precision parameter
1/\mathrm{poly}\log n<\epsilon<1
, there are
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate algorithms for both the
k
-commodity concurrent and non-concurrent mincost flow problems with work
\hat{O}(mk)
and depth
\hat{O}(1)
. The flow solution is outputted explicitly in its edge representation.

Theorem

1.2
gives a parallel
(1+\epsilon)
-approximation multi-commodity flow algorithm with almost optimal depth and work.

We point out that our algorithms are randomized, but they have good potential to be deterministic since the only randomized ingredients are parallel algorithms related to sparse neighborhood covers. We refer to
Remark

7.4
for a discussion.

1.2
Our Techniques

We now highlight our technical components that may be of independent interest. An overview of how everything fits together will be given in
Section

2
.

Length-Constrained Flow Shortcuts with
(1+\epsilon)
Length Slack.

The notion of
length-constrained flow shortcuts
3
3

3

They are called
low-step flow emulator
in
[
HHL+24
]
.
(LC-flow shortcuts) are introduced recently by
[
HHL+24
]
, which is a new kind of graph augmentation capturing both length and congestion information. Roughly speaking, an LC-flow shortcut augments a graph with additional edges and vertices, enabling all multi-commodity flow demands to be routed with approximately the same length and congestion as before, but only along flow paths each of which has a small number (called
step
) of edges.

[
HHL+24
]
showed the existence of an LC-flow shortcut of size
O(n^{1+O(\epsilon)})
with length slack
O(1/\epsilon^{3})
, congestion slack
n^{O(\epsilon)}
and
O(1/\epsilon)
step
4
4

4

In fact, the shortcut in
[
HHL+24
]
has length slack
O(1/\epsilon^{4})
and step
O(1/\epsilon^{2})
, but they ensure that all shortcut edges have endpoints inside
V
. When allowing endpoints outside
V
, one can shave a factor of
1/\epsilon
from both length slack and step by replacing their routers with star graphs.
, but for undirected graphs with only edge lengths and capacities. They also presented shortcuts that can be constructed in parallel and fast but with worse quality such as
2^{\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)}
length slack. For undirected graphs with vertex lengths and capacities, very recently,
[
HLSW25
]
showed that there exists an LC-flow shortcut of size
O(n^{1+O(\epsilon)})
with length slack
O(1/\epsilon^{3})
, congestion slack
n^{O(\epsilon)}
and step
2^{O(1/\epsilon)}
, but without an efficient construction.

The approaches behind both the aforementioned LC-flow shortcuts are based on tools related to
length-constrained (vertex) expanders

[
HRG22
,
HHT24
,
HLSW25
]
. However, these approaches fail to go beyond the inherent large length slack of these tools, and therefore do not result in LC-flow shortcuts with
1+\epsilon
length slack.

We give the first LC-flow shortcut with
1+\epsilon
length slack. Precisely, our LC-flow shortcut works for undirected graphs with vertex lengths and capacities, and it has size
\hat{O}(n)
, length slack
1+\epsilon
, congestion slack
\hat{O}(1)
and step
\hat{O}(1)
for any
1/\mathrm{polylog}(n)<\epsilon<1
. Furthermore, our shortcut can be constructed in
\hat{O}(m)
work and
\hat{O}(1)
depth. We refer to
Theorem

5.2
for a detailed statement of our result (or
Theorem

2.1
in the overview for an informal version).

Our new LC-flow shortcut, thus, improves upon previous length slack from a large constant to
(1+\epsilon)
, at an expense of increasing the step from a large constant to
\hat{O}(1)
. However, the step will only affect the work and depth of the final algorithms, not the approximation. The construction of our LC-flow shortcuts can be viewed as a generalization of Cohen’s hopset construction
[
Coh00
]
which provides shortcuts with
(1+\epsilon)
length slack but no guarantee on congestion slack.

Close-to-Optimal LC-Vertex Expander Decomposition Algorithm.

Our second technical contribution is the first parallel length-constrained vertex expander decomposition algorithm in close-to-linear work and close-to-constant depth. We refer to
Section

3.3
for related preliminaries and
Theorem

4.1
for the formal statement of our results.

A fast LC-vertex-expander decomposition algorithm is required when we want our LC-flow shortcuts to work on undirected graphs with vertex lengths and capacities. However, previously
[
HLSW25
]
only showed the existence of good LC-vertex-expander decompositions without an efficient algorithm for them.

Our approach mainly follows the fast LC-(edge)-expander decomposition algorithm, developed recently in
[
HHT24
]
. Given that algorithmic LC-(edge)-expander decompositions have already led to several applications
[
HHL+24
,
HLS24
]
, our fast algorithm for LC-vertex-expander decomposition will likely have more future applications.

Length-Constrained Multi-Commodity Maxflows in Low Depths.

Our last intermediate result which may be of independent interest is a
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate length-constrained multi-commodity maxflow algorithm
with
\tilde{O}(mk)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h,\epsilon^{-1})
work and
\tilde{O}(1)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h,\epsilon^{-1})
depth, for
k
commodities and a given
h
representing the length constraint. We refer to
Section

6
for the formal definition and
Theorem

6.1
for the formal result statement.

Previously,
[
HHS23
]
studied this problem in the single-commodity setting, and showed a single-commodity version with
\tilde{O}(m)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h,\epsilon^{-1})
work and
\tilde{O}(1)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h,\epsilon^{-1})
. They also generalized their result to the multi-commodity setting in an (almost) black-box way, but with an additional
k
factor in depth compared to ours.

Our approach towards improvement (i.e. the depth is now independent from
k
) is a white-box generalization of
[
HHS23
]
combining Cohen’s idea of
path-count flows

[
Coh95
]
.

1.3
Organization

In
Section

2
, we give an overview of our techniques.
Section

3
introduces basic concepts and definitions we will use in the main body. In
Section

4
, we formally state the result on our fast parallel length-constrained vertex expander decomposition algorithm (with proof deferred to the appendix). In
Section

5
, we discuss our LC-flow shortcuts with
(1+\epsilon)
length slack, which will use the decomposition subroutine in
Section

4
. In
Section

6
we talk about our

(1+\epsilon)
-approximate length-constrained multi-commodity maxflow algorithm. Lastly, in
Section

7
we complete the proof of our parallel
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate multi-commodity mincost flow result, using our new tools in
Sections

5
and
6
.

2
Overview

In this section, we give an overview of our parallel
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate multi-commodity mincost flow algorithm.

At a high-level, there are three steps towards our final result. The first and most important step is to build
length-constrained flow (LC-flow) shortcuts
with
1+\epsilon
length slack, subpolynomial congestion slack and subpolynomial step (
Section

5
) and towards this we need a fast parallel length-constrained vertex expander decomposition algorithm (
Section

4
). The second step is to develop a low-step multi-commodity mincost flow algorithm, but we allow a bicriteria approximation with
1+\epsilon
cost slack and subpolynomial congestion slack (
Section

7.3
), which requires an approximate length-constrained multi-commodity maxflow algorithm (
Section

6
). Lastly in the third step, combining the shortcuts and the low-step flow, we can compute a multi-commodity mincost flow in the original graph, also with a bicriteria
1+\epsilon
cost slack and subpolynomial congestion slack (
Section

7.4
). Further using the flow boosting template
[
GK07
,
HHL+24
]
based on multiplicative weight updates, the subpolynomial congestion slack can be boosted down to
1+\epsilon
, at the cost of an additional subpolynomial factor in depth, and this gives our final result (
Section

7.5
).

A large fraction of this overview will focus on the first step, i.e. LC-flow shortcuts with
(1+\epsilon)
length slack. Although the formal argument is somewhat complicated, the key idea is intuitive. Therefore, in
Section

2.1
, we will present a relatively succinct argument, which is informal but (almost) precise and self-contained, by simplifying the setting and omitting the proofs of some less important properties. For example, we will mainly focus on undirected graphs with only edge capacities and costs/lengths, and we will only argue the
existence
of such shortcuts without providing a fast construction algorithm (only in
Section

2.1.3
, we will talk about undirected graphs with vertex costs and capacities, and briefly discuss the fast construction).

In
Section

2.2
, we will briefly discuss the idea behind the second step without going into the technical details. We will not talk about the third step in this overview, because
[
HHL+24
]
already presented a formal template of the flow boosting and we only need to plug in our new algorithm as a subroutine.

2.1
LC-Flow Shortcuts with
(1+\epsilon)
Length Slack

Before introducing our LC-flow shortcuts, we need to set up some basic notions on flows. Consider an undirected graph
G=(V,E)
with edge lengths
\ell
and capacities
U
. A
(multi-commodity) flow

F
is a collection of weighted flow paths
P
. The weight of each path
P
is called its value, denoted by
F(P)
, and the value of the flow
F
is
\mathrm{value}(F)=\sum_{P\in F}F(P)
. A
demand

D
is a function that assigns each (ordered) vertex pair
(u,v)
a non-negative number
D(u,v)
. We further have the following concepts about a flow
F
.
The
demand routed by
F
, denoted by
D_{F}
, has
D_{F}(u,v)
equals to the total value of
u
-
v
flow paths for any vertex pair
(u,v)
.
The
length
of
F
, denoted by
\mathrm{leng}(F)
, is the maximum length of flow paths.
The
congestion
of
F
, denoted by
\mathrm{cong}(F)
, is the maximum
F(e)/U(e)
over
e\in E
, where
F(e)
is the total value of flow paths going through
e
.
The
step
of
F
, denoted by
\mathrm{step}(F)
, is the maximum number of edges on a flow path.
Our result about low-step LC-flow shortcuts is given in
Theorem

2.1
.

2.1
.

Given an undirected graph
G
and a parameter
1/\mathrm{polylog}(n)<\epsilon<1
, we can compute an additional edge set
H
(possibly with endpoints outside
G
) satisfying the following.

•

(Forward Mapping) For any flow
F^{G}
in
G
with congestion
1
, there is a flow
F^{H}
in
G\cup H
routing the same demand with length
(1+\epsilon)\mathrm{leng}(F^{G})
, congestion
1
and step
\hat{O}(1)
.

•

(Backward Mapping) For any flow
F^{H}
in
G\cup H
routing a demand between
V(G)
with congestion
1
, there is a flow
F^{G}
in
G
routing the same demand with length
\mathrm{leng}(F^{H})
and congestion
\hat{O}(1)
.

The size of
H
is
\hat{O}(n)
, and
H
can be computed in
\hat{O}(m)
work and
\hat{O}(1)
depth.

The additional edge set
H
in
Theorem

2.1
is what we called an LC-flow shortcut. The length slack and step of the LC-flow shortcut are those in the forward mapping, while the congestion slack is from the backward mapping. In other words,
Theorem

2.1
shows an LC-flow shortcut with length slack
1+\epsilon
, congestion slack
\hat{O}(1)
and step
\hat{O}(1)
.

To achieve LC-flow shortcuts with
1+\epsilon
length slack, we show a new construction inspired by Cohen’s
hopsets

[
Coh00
]
. Roughly speaking, a hopset is an LC-flow shortcut without guarantees on the congestion slack, i.e. it

(1+\epsilon)
-approximately preserves pairwise distances. Cohen showed that one can construct hopsets with
1+\epsilon
length slack using
neighborhood covers
that have a relatively large length slack (i.e., the ratio between the diameter and the covering radius). To further encode congestion information, we hope to integrate the recently developed
LC-expander decompositions
into Cohen’s approach, as LC-expander decompositions strengthen neighborhood covers in the sense that they capture both length and congestion information. Moreover, this time we can hope to overcome the large-length-slack barrier in
[
HHL+24
]
since Cohen’s hopset construction shows the possibility of obtaining
1+\epsilon
length slack using tools with much larger length slack.

We now start discussing the existence of such LC-flow shortcuts in
Theorem

2.1
. Our full argument is a
two-dimensional
induction/recursion. In the first dimension, we perform induction on the graph size (this corresponds to Cohen’s hopset construction), and the second-dimensional induction is on the levels of the
LC-expander hierarchy
. For better understanding, in
Section

2.1.1
, we first consider a warm-up case in which the input graph is already an LC-expander, in which the argument only involves the first-dimensional induction. Next, in
Section

2.1.2
we turn to general graphs, introduce the LC-expander hierarchy, and discuss the two-dimensional induction (based on a two-level hierarchy or more).

Before we move on, we emphasize that we will actually aim at shortcuts with
(1+o(\log n)\epsilon)
length slack instead. Scaling down
\epsilon
by
\log n
gives a
1+\epsilon
(for the original
\epsilon
) length slack. Furthermore, we point out that it suffices to show the existence of a weaker version of LC-flow shortcuts, called
h
-LC-flow shortcuts
. That is, the forward mapping only needs to work for
h
-length flows
F^{G}
in
G
(i.e.
\mathrm{leng}(F^{G})\leq h
). To obtain a (general) LC-flow shortcut, just take the union of
h
-LC-flow shortcuts for
h
being different powers of
1+\epsilon
. This only multiplicatively increases the length slack by another factor of
1+\epsilon
, and the congestion slack by a factor of
O(\log n/\epsilon)
, assuming polynomially-bounded lengths.

2.1.1
Warm Up:
h
-LC-Flow Shortcuts for LC-Expanders

First we set up the scenario by introducing LC-expanders. A
node-weighting

A
is a function that assigns each vertex
v\in V
a non-negative number
A(v)
. In particular, the
degree node-weighting
of
G
, denoted by
A_{\deg}
, has
A_{\deg}(v)=\sum_{e\in E\text{
incident to }v}U(e)
for each vertex
v
. A demand
D
is
A
-respecting
if for each vertex
v
, the total demand units involving
v
, i.e.
D(v):=\sum_{v^{\prime}}D(v,v^{\prime})+D(v^{\prime},v)
, is at most
A(v)
, and
D
is
h
-length
if any pair of vertices
(u,v)
s.t.
D(u,v)>0
has
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)\leq h
. Finally, for a length parameter
h_{\mathrm{ed}}
, a length slack
s
and a congestion parameter
\phi
, a node-weighting
A
is
(h_{\mathrm{ed}},s)
-length
\phi
-expanding
in
G
if any
h_{\mathrm{ed}}
-length
A
-respecting demand
D
can be routed (by a flow) with length
h_{\mathrm{ed}}s
and congestion
O(\log n/\phi)
. Particularly, we say
G
is an
(h_{\mathrm{ed}},s)
-length
\phi
-expander if its degree node-weighting
A_{\deg}
is expanding (for
h_{\mathrm{ed}},s,\phi
).

In this warm-up scenario, we assume the input graph
G
is an
(h_{\mathrm{ed}},s)
-length
\phi
-expander for

s=\log n,h_{\mathrm{ed}}=\epsilon h/s,\text{
and }\phi=1/2^{\log^{0.75}n}.

We choose such
s
and
\phi
because we want them to fit our argument in
Section

2.1.2
later.

Second, we will need the aforementioned tool of neighborhood covers. A neighborhood cover
{\cal N}
is a collection of vertex subsets
S
called
clusters
, which can be partitioned into
\omega
(called the
width
)
clusterings
, where each clustering is a collection of disjoint clusters. The
diameter

h_{\mathrm{diam}}
of
{\cal N}
is the maximum diameter of a cluster, and
{\cal N}
has
covering radius

h_{\mathrm{cov}}
if for each vertex
v
, there is a cluster
S
containing all vertices
v^{\prime}
within distance
h_{\mathrm{cov}}
from
v
.

Shortcut Construction.

Now we are ready to construct the
h
-LC-flow shortcut.

Step 1.
First, we take a neighborhood cover
{\cal N}
of
G
with

h_{\mathrm{diam}}=h_{\mathrm{ed}},h_{\mathrm{cov}}=h_{\mathrm{diam}}/\log n\text{ and }\omega=O(\log n).

It is known that such
{\cal N}
exists.
We further partition clusters in
{\cal N}
into
small clusters

{\cal N}_{\mathrm{small}}
and
large clusters

{\cal N}_{\mathrm{large}}
, such that
{\cal N}_{\mathrm{small}}
collects all clusters of size at most
n/\sigma
and
{\cal N}_{\mathrm{large}}
contains the rest.
Here
\sigma
is some subpolynomial parameter, and here we set

\sigma=2^{\log^{0.75}n}.

Step 2.
For each small cluster
S\in{\cal N}_{\mathrm{small}}
, we
inductively
assume there exists an
h_{\mathrm{cov}}
-LC-flow shortcut
H_{S}
for the graph
G[S]
(the subgraph induced by
S
) with length slack
\hat{\lambda}
, congestion slack
\hat{\kappa}
and step
\hat{t}
(think of
\hat{\lambda}
as roughly
1+\epsilon
).

Step 3.
For each large cluster
S\in{\cal N}_{\mathrm{large}}
, we add a
star graph

H_{S}
in which a newly created center vertex
x_{S}
is connected to each original vertex
v\in S
by an edge with length
h_{\mathrm{diam}}s
and capacity
A_{\deg}(v)
. Intuitively, the star edges have such lengths and capacities because we use the star
H_{S}
as a low-step simulation of the expander routing. Recall that we choose
h_{\mathrm{diam}}=h_{\mathrm{ed}}
, so any
A_{\deg}
-respecting demand on
S
(which is definitely
h_{\mathrm{ed}}
-length) can be routed in the LC-expander
G
with length
h_{\mathrm{diam}}s
and low congestion.

Step 4.
For each pair of large clusters
S_{1},S_{2}\in{\cal N}_{\mathrm{large}}
, and each
h^{\prime}\in[1,h]
rounded up to the nearest integral power of
(1+\epsilon)
, we add an edge, called an
inter-cluster edge
, connecting the star centers
x_{S_{1}}
and
x_{S_{2}}
with length
h^{\prime}+2h_{\mathrm{diam}}s
. Moreover, the capacity is the value of the maximum
(h^{\prime}+2h_{\mathrm{diam}}s)
-length single-commodity flow from
x_{S_{1}}
to
x_{S_{2}}
in
G\cup H_{S_{1}}\cup H_{S_{2}}
. Intuitively, these parallel edges between
x_{S_{1}}
and
x_{S_{2}}
provide
1
-step shortcuts for any feasible
h
-length flow
F
routing an
A_{\deg}
-respecting demand
D_{F}
from
S_{1}
to
S_{2}
.

Step 5.
The final shortcut
H
is the union of
H_{S}
from steps 2 and 3, and the inter-cluster edges from step 4.

Forward and Backward Mappings.

We are not going to give a rigorous analysis here but only some intuitions about the forward and backward mappings.

For the forward mapping, we can hope for low length slack because we pay roughly
1+\epsilon
multiplicative length slack and additionally
O(h_{\mathrm{diam}}s)=O(\epsilon h)

additive length slack
in this inductive step. To see this, consider an
h
-length flow path
P
in the given
h
-length flow
F^{G}
. Let us partition
P
into roughly
O(h/h_{\mathrm{cov}})=\tilde{O}(1/\epsilon)
many subpaths
\hat{P}
, each of which has length at most
h_{\mathrm{cov}}
. Thus each subpath
\hat{P}
is totally contained by some cluster
\hat{S}
in the neighborhood cover
{\cal N}
.

Figure 1:
An example for the forward mapping in LC-expanders. Given flow path
P=P_{u,v}
, the red path is the output of the forward mapping.
We only draw few subpaths on
P
and omit others for simplicity.
We note that the routing given in the small-cluster part is only for illustration, which represents the inductive forward mapping results.

Small-Cluster Subpaths.
For each subpath
\hat{P}
whose
\hat{S}
is a small cluster, we are good because the shortcut
H_{\hat{S}}
from Step 2 can reroute
\hat{P}
with multiplicative length slack
\hat{\lambda}
(roughly
1+\epsilon
) and step
\hat{t}
.

Large-Cluster Subpaths.
For those
\hat{P}
in large clusters
\hat{S}
, instead of rerouting them separately using star graphs on large clusters, we will do something different using the step-4 shortcut edges so that we only pay
O(h_{\mathrm{diam}}s)
additive length slack. As illustrated in
Figure

1
, let
\hat{P}_{L}
and
\hat{P}_{R}
be the leftmost and rightmost large-cluster subpaths, and let
w_{L}
and
w_{R}
be the left endpoint of
\hat{P}_{L}
and the right endpoint of
\hat{P}_{R}
. Then we reroute the whole part of
P
between
w_{L}
and
w_{R}
(denoted by
P[w_{L},w_{R}]
and called the
jumping subpath
) using shortcut edges

(w_{L},x_{\hat{S}_{L}})\circ(x_{\hat{S}_{L}},x_{\hat{S}_{R}})\circ(x_{\hat{S}_{R}},w_{R}).

Here
(w_{L},x_{\hat{S}_{L}})
and
(x_{\hat{S}_{R}},w_{R})
are from the star graphs
H_{\hat{S}_{L}}
and
H_{\hat{S}_{R}}
, so they both have lengths
h_{\mathrm{diam}}s
. In the middle,
(x_{\hat{S}_{L}},x_{\hat{S}_{R}})
is the inter-cluster edge between the star centers with appropriate length
(1+\epsilon)\cdot\mathrm{leng}(P[w_{L},w_{R}])+2h_{\mathrm{diam}}s
. In other words, rerouting the jumping subpath
P[w_{L},w_{R}]
incurs
1+\epsilon
multiplicative length slack and
4h_{\mathrm{diam}}s
additive slack.

Therefore, for this inductive step, the multiplicative length slack is
\max\{\hat{\lambda},1+\epsilon\}
and additive length slack is
4h_{\mathrm{diam}}s
as desired. Moreover, after rerouting, the step bound is roughly
\tilde{O}(1/\epsilon)\cdot\hat{t}
, because there are
\tilde{O}(1/\epsilon)
subpaths and the bottleneck is the small-cluster subpaths. In summary, the key intuition behind this forward mapping argument is that we can get small additive length slack (
O(\epsilon h)
instead of
\tilde{O}(h)
) by paying a
\tilde{O}(1/\epsilon)
multiplicative factor on the step bound.

Regarding the backward mapping, we briefly discuss its congestion slack below. In Step 2, all shortcuts
H_{S}
of small clusters together cause congestion slack
\hat{\kappa}\cdot\omega=O(\hat{\kappa}\log n)
because
{\cal N}
has width
\omega
. In Step 3, all star graphs of large clusters together cause congestion slack
O(\log n/\phi)\cdot\omega
by the expander routing and the width of
{\cal N}
. In Step 4, each inter-cluster edge
(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}})
causes congestion slack
1+O(\log n/\phi)
, because it corresponds to an underlying feasible flow in
G\cup H_{S_{1}}\cup H_{S_{2}}
and further projecting this flow to
G
requires expander routing inside
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
. The number of inter-cluster edges is at most
(\omega\sigma)^{2}\cdot O(\log h/\epsilon)
(note that there are at most
\omega\sigma
large clusters and each pair has
O(\log h/\epsilon)
parallel edges with different lengths), so step 4 causes congestion slack
O((\omega\sigma)^{2}\cdot(\log n/\phi)\cdot O(\log h/\epsilon))
. Adding up all above, the total congestion slack is
\tilde{O}(\hat{\kappa})+\tilde{O}(\sigma^{2}/(\phi\epsilon))
.

The Final Shortcut Quality.

From the analysis above, one inductive step gives multiplicative length slack
\lambda=\hat{\lambda}+5\epsilon
(after merging the additive slack
4\epsilon h
into the multiplicative slack), congestion slack
\kappa=\tilde{O}(1)\cdot\hat{\kappa}+\tilde{O}(\sigma^{2}/(\phi\epsilon))
and step
t=\tilde{O}(1/\epsilon)\cdot\hat{t}
. Suppose we are in the ideal case that all graphs in the whole induction tree are good LC-expanders. Note that the induction depth is
z=\log_{\sigma}n=O(\log^{0.25}n)
because the number of vertices drops by a factor of
\sigma=2^{\log^{0.75}n}
in each inductive step. Therefore, the final shortcut has length slack
\lambda=1+O(\log^{0.25}n)\cdot\epsilon
, congestion slack
\kappa=\tilde{O}(1)^{O(\log^{0.25}n)}\cdot\tilde{O}(2^{3\log^{0.75}n}/\epsilon)
and step
t=\tilde{O}(1/\epsilon)^{O(\log^{0.25}n)}
. This implies
\lambda=1+\epsilon
,
\kappa=\hat{O}(1)
and
t=\hat{O}(1)
.

2.1.2
h
-LC-Flow Shortcuts for General Graphs

We will leverage
LC-expander decompositions
and
LC-expander hierarchies
to extend the argument in
Section

2.1.1
to general graphs, and now we give their definitions.

The first is LC-expander decompositions. An
h_{C}
-length moving cut

C
is a function that assigns each edge
e
a cut value
C(e)\in\{0,1/h_{C},2/h_{C},...,1\}
. The
graph
G
with
C
applied
, denoted by
G-C
, is the same graph but with different edge lengths
\ell_{G-C}=\ell_{G}+h_{C}\cdot C
. An
(h_{\mathrm{ed}},s)
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition
of a node-weighting
A
in
G
is an
(h_{\mathrm{ed}}\cdot s)
-length moving cut
C
such that
A
is
(h_{\mathrm{ed}},s)
-length
\phi
-expanding in
G-C
(recall the term “expanding” in
Section

2.1.1
). From now we fix

s=\log n\text{ and }\phi=1/2^{\log^{0.75}n}.

It is known that (see e.g.
[
HRG22
]
), given any node-weighting
A
and length parameter
h_{\mathrm{ed}}
, there exists an
(h_{\mathrm{ed}},s)
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition
C
of
A
in
G
with
|C|\leq\tilde{O}(\phi)\cdot|A|
,
where
|A|=\sum_{v\in V}A(v)
and
|C|=\sum_{e\in E}C(e)\cdot U(e)
are sizes of
A
and
C
respectively. An interpretation of an LC-expander decomposition is that we can make
A
expand in
G
by increasing the edge lengths a bit.

Next we consider LC-expander hierarchies. For simplicity, we only formally define a two-level LC-expander hierarchy, and assume the graph
G
admits such a two-level hierarchy. We fix

h_{\mathrm{diam}}=\epsilon h/s,\ h_{\mathrm{cov}}=h_{\mathrm{diam}}/\log n

to be consistent with step 1 in
Section

2.1.1
.

•

At the first level, we start with the degree node-weighting
A_{1}:=A_{\deg}
. Then we select
h_{\mathrm{ed},1}=\epsilon(1+2\epsilon)h_{\mathrm{cov}}/s
, and take an
(h_{\mathrm{ed},1},s)
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition
C_{1}
of
A_{1}
. Note that
C_{1}
is an
(h_{\mathrm{ed},1}\cdot s)
-length moving cut.

•

Turn to the second level. We define the
cut node-weighting
of
C_{1}
, denoted by
A_{C_{1}}
, by setting
A_{C_{1}}(v)=\sum_{e\in E\text{ incident to }v}C_{1}(e)\cdot U(e)
for each vertex
v
. Then the second-level node-weighting
A_{2}
is exactly
A_{C_{1}}
, and we assume
A_{2}
is
(h_{\mathrm{ed},2},s)
-length
\phi
-expanding in
G
for
h_{\mathrm{ed},2}=h_{\mathrm{diam}}
(so the hierarchy ends at the second level).

Figure 2:
An example of a two-level expander decomposition hierarchy with shortcuts over large-clusters. For simplicity, we omit small-clusters and inter-cluster edges.
(b) Dashed edges represent cut edges
e
with
C_{1}(e)=1
(in this figure, we assume each
C_{1}(e)
is either
0
or
1
).
(c) Vertices are not in the support set of
A_{2}
is marked with hollow circles.

Shortcut Construction.

The construction of the shortcut is almost identical to that in
Section

2.1.1
, except for the following. See
Figure

2
for an illustration.

Step 0.
We add a Step 0 before Step 1. Assume
inductively
, for
h_{1}=(1+2\epsilon)h_{\mathrm{cov}}
, there exists an
h_{1}
-LC-flow shortcut
H_{1}
for the graph
G-C_{1}
with length slack
\tilde{\lambda}
, congestion slack
\tilde{\kappa}
and step
\tilde{t}
. Recall that
A_{1}=A_{\deg}
is
(h_{\mathrm{ed},1},s)
-length
\phi
-expanding in
G-C_{1}
for
h_{\mathrm{ed},1}=\epsilon h_{1}/s
, so this is exactly the same scenario with when building an
h_{1}
-LC-flow shortcut for
G-C_{1}
in
Section

2.1.1
.

This completes the shortcut construction for level
1
, and we will move on to level 2, by following Steps 1 to 4 in
Section

2.1.1
(but replacing Step 3 with
3^{\prime}
).

Step
3^{\prime}
.
For each large cluster
S
in
{\cal N}_{2}
(where
{\cal N}_{2}
is the neighborhood cover from Step
1
), in the star graph
H_{S}
, the edge connecting the center
x_{S}
and a vertex
v\in S
now has capacity
A_{2}(v)
instead of
A_{\deg}(v)
(if some
v
has
A_{2}(v)=0
, this edge does not exist). This is a natural modification since we only have
A_{2}
expanding in
G
now.

Step
5^{\prime}
.
Further add the first-level shortcut
H_{1}
from Step 0 into the final shortcut
H
.

The Forward Mapping.

There is no big change to the backward mapping, but the forward mapping becomes more involved. Consider a path
P
from
F^{G}
, and we still start with partitioning
P
into
\tilde{O}(1/\epsilon)
subpaths
\hat{P}
of length
exactly
5
5

5

We assume this to simplify the discussion, but generally it should be at most.

h_{\mathrm{cov}}
. The small-cluster shortcuts from Step 2 can still take care of all the small-cluster subpaths.

However, now the jumping subpath
P[w_{L},w_{R}]
cannot be simply rerouted using the star graphs from Step
3^{\prime}
and the inter-cluster edges from Step
4
, because the star graphs is for the node-weighting
A_{2}
. In the extreme case that
A_{2}(w_{L})=0
, the star edge connecting
w_{L}
and
x_{S_{1}}
does not even exist.

Fixing this needs to exploit the first-level shortcut
H_{1}
from Step 0. We further classify large-cluster subpaths into
light subpaths
and
heavy subpaths
, where light subpaths are those
\hat{P}
with
C_{1}(\hat{P})=\sum_{e\in\hat{P}}C_{1}(e)\leq 1
and heavy subpaths are the rest.

Light Large-Cluster Subpaths.
Each light subpath
\hat{P}
can be rerouted using the
h_{1}
-LC-flow shortcut
H_{1}
of
G-C_{1}
because in
G-C_{1}
,
\hat{P}
has length
\ell_{G-C_{1}}(\hat{P})=\ell_{G}(\hat{P})+(h_{\mathrm{ed},1}\cdot s)C_{1}(\hat{P})\leq h_{\mathrm{cov}}+\epsilon h_{1}\leq h_{1}
by
h_{1}=(1+2\epsilon)h_{\mathrm{cov}}
and
h_{\mathrm{ed},1}=\epsilon h_{1}/s
. After rerouting, the length and step become
\tilde{\lambda}h_{1}
and
\tilde{t}
, so the multiplicative length slack w.r.t.
\ell_{G}(\hat{P})=h_{\mathrm{cov}}
is
\tilde{\lambda}h_{1}/h_{\mathrm{cov}}\leq(1+2\epsilon)\tilde{\lambda}
.

Figure 3:
An example of the jumping subpath between heavy large-cluster subpaths. The blue region marks
Y_{L}
and
Y_{R}
in the leftmost and rightmost heavy large-cluster subpaths, respectively. The black dashed arrows represent the relocation using the first-level shortcut
H_{1}
, where we omit for simplicity.

Heavy Large-Cluster Subpaths.
The subpaths that have not been shortcut are heavy large-cluster subpaths. Let
\hat{P}_{L}
and
\hat{P}_{R}
be the leftmost and rightmost heavy large-cluster subpaths (say
\hat{S}_{L}
and
\hat{S}_{R}
are the clusters containing
\hat{P}_{L}
and
\hat{P}_{R}
respectively). The
jumping subpath

P[w_{L},w_{R}]
is between the left endpoint
w_{L}
of
\hat{P}_{L}
and the right endpoint
w_{R}
of
\hat{P}_{R}
. Since now
\hat{P}_{L}
is further heavy (i.e.
A_{2}(\hat{P}_{L})\geq C_{1}(\hat{P}_{L})\geq 1
is properly large), we can upload the flow units to
x_{S_{1}}
from the
\hat{P}_{L}
-vertices
y_{1}
(called
portals
) with enough
A_{2}(y_{L})
, assuming that we can
relocate
the flow units from
w_{L}
to these
y_{L}
.

To choose these portals
y_{L}
, we first take the
shortest
prefix
\hat{P}^{\prime}_{L}
of
\hat{P}_{L}
such that
C_{1}(\hat{P}^{\prime}_{L})\geq 1
. Then let
Y_{L}=\{\text{left endpoint $y_{L,e}$ of $e$}\mid e\in\hat{P}^{\prime}_{L},C_{1}(e)>0\}
be the portals, and we restrict that each portal
y_{L,e}
can receive up to
C_{1}(e)\cdot\mathrm{value}(P)
flow units relocated from
w_{1}
, which will not hurt since only
\mathrm{value}(P)
flow units need to be relocated. This restriction is to ensure that we will not violate the capacities of star edges at the very end (i.e. ensure the obtained
F^{H}
has congestion
1
). For the right side, we choose
Y_{R}
and apply the restriction similarly.

Rerouting the flow units from left portals
Y_{L}
to right portals
Y_{R}
can be done using star edges and inter-cluster edges similar to the argument in
Section

2.1.1
. It remains to relocate the units from
w_{L}
to
Y_{L}
(also from
Y_{R}
to
w_{R}
). We can do this using the first-level shortcut
H_{1}
again similar to the argument for light large-cluster subpaths, because for any
y_{L}\in Y_{L}
, the subpath
P[w_{L},y_{L}]
has length in
G-C_{1}
at most

\ell_{G}(P[w_{L},y_{L}])+(h_{\mathrm{ed},1}\cdot s)\cdot C_{1}(P[w_{L},y_{L}])\leq h_{\mathrm{cov}}+\epsilon(1+2\epsilon)h_{\mathrm{cov}}\leq h_{1}.

Recall that
h_{1}=(1+2\epsilon)h_{\mathrm{cov}}
and
h_{\mathrm{ed},1}=\epsilon h_{1}/s
.
Note that
C_{1}(P[w_{L},y_{L}])\leq 1
because of the “shortest” property of
\hat{P}^{\prime}_{L}
.

We directly state the length slack and the step bound of this inductive step without detailed explanations (which is quite straightforward following the argument in the expander-case in
Section

2.1.1
). The multiplicative length slack is
\lambda=\max\{\hat{\lambda},(1+2\epsilon)\tilde{\lambda},1+\epsilon\}+O(\epsilon)
, where
\hat{\lambda}
is from the small-cluster subpaths,
(1+2\epsilon)\tilde{\lambda}
is from the light large-cluster subpaths, and
1+\epsilon
and
O(\epsilon)
are multiplicative and additive length slacks from rerouting (from left to right portals of) the jumping subpath. The step bound is
t=\tilde{O}(1/\epsilon)\cdot\max\{\hat{t},\tilde{t}\}
. For the backward mapping, the congestion slack is
\kappa=\tilde{O}(\hat{\kappa})+\tilde{O}(\sigma^{2}/(\phi\epsilon))+\tilde{\kappa}
(just add one more term
\tilde{\kappa}
from the first-level shortcut
H_{1}
).

The Final Shortcut Quality.

Although we focus on a two-level hierarchy above, generally the hierarchy has
r=O(\log^{0.25}n)
levels because going up one level will reduce the size of node-weighting by a factor of roughly
2^{\log^{0.75}n}
(recall that
|A_{2}|=2|C_{1}|\leq\tilde{O}(\phi)|A_{1}|
and we choose
\phi=1/2^{\log^{0.75}n}
). Also we have shown that the first-dimensional induction has
z=\log_{\sigma}n=O(\log^{0.25}n)
levels. Let
\lambda_{(\alpha,\beta)}
,
\kappa_{(\alpha,\beta)}
and
t_{(\alpha,\beta)}
be the shortcut quality when we are at level
\alpha\in[1,z]
of the first-dimensional induction and level
\beta\in[1,r]
of the hierarchy. The argument above gives the following recurrence relations:

\displaystyle\lambda_{(\alpha,\beta)}=\max\{\lambda_{(\alpha-1,r)},(1+2\epsilon)\lambda_{(\alpha,\beta-1)},1+\epsilon\}+O(\epsilon),

\displaystyle\kappa_{(\alpha,\beta)}=\tilde{O}(\kappa_{(\alpha-1,r)})+\kappa_{(\alpha,\beta-1)}+\tilde{O}(\sigma^{2}/(\phi\epsilon)),

\displaystyle t_{(\alpha,\beta)}=\tilde{O}(1/\epsilon)\cdot t_{(\alpha,\beta-1)},

solving which gives
\lambda_{(z,r)}=1+O(\log^{0.5}n)\cdot\epsilon
,
\kappa_{(z,r)}=\tilde{O}(1)^{O(\log^{0.5}n)}\cdot\tilde{O}(2^{3\log^{0.75}n}/\epsilon)
and
t_{(z,r)}=\tilde{O}(1/\epsilon)^{O(\log^{0.5}n)}
. Substituting the above
\epsilon
with an
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon/\log n
gives a
h
-LC-flow shortcut with length slack
1+\epsilon
, congestion slack
\hat{O}(1)
and step
\hat{O}(1)
.

2.1.3
Algorithmic Shortcuts for Vertex-Capacitated Graphs

Lastly, we make some comments on making the above shortcut construction algorithmic and work for undirected graphs with vertex capacities and lengths. In fact, there is no significant barrier, except that we need
length-constrained vertex expander decompositions
and they should be algorithmic. The existence of such decompositions was shown in a recent work
[
HLSW25
]
, but there is still no fast parallel algorithm for them to date. Therefore, we develop the first close-to-optimal parallel length-constrained vertex expander decomposition algorithm based on the LC-(edge)-expander decomposition algorithm in
[
HHT24
]
.

2.2
.

Let
G
be an undirected graph with vertex capacities and lengths. Given a node-weighting
A
and parameters
h\geq 1,\epsilon\in(0,1),\phi\in(0,1)
, there is an algorithm that computes an
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-vertex-expander decomposition
C
of
A
in
G
such that
s=\exp(\exp(O(\log\epsilon^{-1})\cdot\epsilon^{-1}))
and
|C|\leq n^{O(\epsilon)}|A|/\phi
. The work is
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}
and the depth is
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}
.

There are two small issues when applying
Theorem

2.2
. First, the tradeoff between the length slack
s=\exp(\exp(O(\log\epsilon^{-1})\cdot\epsilon^{-1}))
and the coefficient
n^{O(\epsilon)}
(called the
cut slack
) in the bound of
|C|
is much worse than the existential bound
[
HLSW25
]
. Hence, we need to tune the parameter in the argument accordingly. We point out that in
Section

5
, for convenience our full argument deviates a bit from the above overview: (1) we actually consider
pure additive length slacks
when analysing the forward mapping, and (2) the parameters we select are very loose (but still get the same asymptotic bounds at the end).

Second, note that the work and depth of
Theorem

2.2
depend on
\mathrm{poly}(h)
, which means we cannot compute
h
-LC-flow shortcuts for large
h
. Therefore, to get (general) LC-flow shortcuts, we use a standard technique called
stacking/bootstrapping
(which has been used extensively, see e.g.
[
Coh00
,
BGS21
,
HHL+24
]
). Concretely, we will bootstrap
h
-LC-flow shortcuts (for some subpolynomial
h
) by building them on top of each other.

2.2
Low-Step Multi-Commodity Mincost Flows in Low Depth

In this part, given a demand
D
, we want to compute a flow
F
routing
D
in
G
with low congestion (say
\tilde{O}(1)
or
\hat{O}(1)
), such that the total length (i.e. cost) of
F
is within a
1+O(\epsilon)
multiplicative factor from the min-total-length
t
-step feasible flow
F^{*}
routing
D
.

This problem can be reduced to the
(approximate) length-constrained maximum flow
problem, which has been studied by
[
HHS23
]
but mainly in the single-commodity setting. Intuitively, the reduction is a simple greedy strategy. Since congestion
\tilde{O}(1)
is allowed, we can consider length parameter
h=(1+\epsilon)^{i}
in an increasing order of
i
. For each such
h
, we try to route as much demand as possible using a feasible
h
-length. Concretely, we partially route
D
via a feasible
(1+\epsilon)h
-length flow
F_{h}
with value (approximately) at least the maximum
t
-step
h
-length feasible flow
F^{*}_{h}
partially routing
D
. Add this
F_{h}
into
F
, subtract from
D
the demand that has been routed, and then go to the next
h
. A small issue is that the LC-max flow subroutine will have work and depth depending on
\mathrm{poly}(h)
(as we will see soon), so we cannot compute
F_{h}
directly when
h
is large. However, since we only need
\mathrm{value}(F_{h})
comparable to the value of the
t
-step
h
-length
F^{*}_{h}
, we actually allow
\epsilon h/t
additive errors on the edge lengths. Hence we can just scale down the edge length by
\epsilon h/t
(then round up to integers) and compute a
(t/\epsilon+t)
-length maxflow instead.

Now we discuss our result on approximate multi-commodity LC-maxflows, which is our second intermediate result which may be of independent interest.

2.3
.

Let
G
be a directed graph with edge lengths and capacities. Let
D
6
6

6

The demand
D
is in this form since we are considering
non-concurrent
multi-commodity flows.
be a given demand such that at most
k
pairs of vertices
(u,v)
have
D(u,v)=\infty
and the rest has
D(u,v)=0
. Given parameters
h\geq 1
and
0<\epsilon<1
, there is an algorithm that computes a
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate feasible
h
-length maxflow
F
partially routing
D
. The work is
\tilde{O}(mk)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h,\epsilon^{-1})
and depth is
\tilde{O}(1)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h,\epsilon^{-1})
.

We note that, assuming subpolynomial
h
and
\epsilon^{-1}
, we are the first to show such an algorithm with work
\hat{O}(mk)
and depth
\hat{O}(1)
independent from
k
. Previously,
[
HHS23
]
mentioned a multi-commodity generalization of their single-commodity LC-maxflow result (in an almost black-box way), but their algorithm has depth
\tilde{O}(k)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h,\epsilon^{-1})
.

Our result is obtained by generalizing the single-commodity LC-maxflow algorithm
[
HHS23
]
in a white-box manner. Roughly speaking,
[
HHS23
]
showed that, in the single-commodity setting, computing
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate LC-maxflow can be reduced to computing blocking flows in an
h
-layer DAG, and
[
Coh95
]
can solve the latter using the idea of
path count flows
. We show that this reduction also works in the multi-commodity setting (in low depth), and observe that Cohen’s path count flows can naturally be computed for
k
commodities in parallel.

3
Preliminaries

Throughout the paper, all graphs are undirected unless specified to be directed.

We always use
n
and
m
to denote the number of vertices and edges of the original graph in the context (for example, the original graph of each main theorem is its input graph). For a real function
F:D\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
S\subseteq D
, we write
F(S)=\sum_{s\in S}F(s)
.

Polynomially-Bounded Integral Functions and Positive Lengths and Capacities.

Original graphs might have length, capacities, demands, node-weightings, etc, and we assume that all these values (which could be real numbers), after scaling up to integers, are upper bounded by a parameter denoted as
N
. We assume
N=\mathrm{poly}(n)
.

Especially, we assume that all length and capacities are
positive
integers. This is almost without loss of generality. We can ignore vertices/edges with zero capacity. For vertices/edges with zero length, we can add a very tiny length, and then scale up the whole length function. But we note that this scaling does not work in the context sensitive to absolute lengths, for example, in some algorithms whose work and depth depend on
h
, where
h
is some absolute length parameter. In this case, we will carefully enforce the input graphs have positive integral lengths.

Undirected graphs with vertex lengths and capacities.

A undirected graph with vertex lengths and capacities, denoted by
G=(V,E)
is an undirected graph where each vertex is associated with a length and a capacity from
\mathbb{N}^{+}
. We define the length function
\ell:V\to\mathbb{N}^{+}
and the capacity function
U:V\to\mathbb{N}^{+}
as mapping a vertex to its associated vertex length and capacity. We use
\ell_{G},U_{G}
to emphasize they are the length and capacity function for
G
. We use
V(G),E(G)
to denote the vertex and edge set of
G
.

For convenience, we will not assign lengths and capacities to edges for such graphs. Namely, each edge has length
0
and infinite capacity. This is without loss of generality because we can always transform edge lengths and capacities into vertex lengths and capacities by putting a vertex on each edge (and our work and depth bound are not sensitive to number of vertices).

Basic graph terminologies.

We define
\delta_{G}(u)=\{(u,v)\in E\}
to be all adjacent edges of
u
in
G
. We can extend this definition to
\delta_{H}(u)=\{(u,v)\in H\}
where
H\subseteq E
is an edge subset (or a subgraph). We use
N_{G}(u)
to denote the vertex set of the neighborhood of
u
. We write
N_{G}(S)=\cup_{v\in S}N_{G}(v)-S
. We write
N_{G}[v]=N_{G}(v)\cup\{v\}
and
N_{G}[S]=N_{G}(S)\cup S
.

Path and distances.

A simple path
P
in
G
is a sequence of distinct vertices
(v_{1},...,v_{k})
such that
(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in E
for every
i\in[k-1]
. We define
\mathrm{step}(P)=k
.We define
V(P)=\{v_{1},...,v_{k}\}
,
E(P)=\{(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in E\mid i\in[k-1]\}
, and
\mathrm{start}(P)=v_{1},\mathrm{end}(P)=v_{k}
. We define
\mathrm{succ}_{P}(v_{i})=v_{i+1}
and
\mathrm{prec}_{P}(v_{i})=v_{i-1}
.
For two vertices
v_{i},v_{j}\in V(P)
with
i\leq j
, we define
P[v_{i},v_{j}]
as the subpath
\{v_{i},v_{i+1},...,v_{j}\}
. Notice that
E(P)
only contains undirected edges, if we want to emphasis the path direction, we use
\vec{E}(P)
to denote the set of ordered pairs
(v_{i},v_{i+1})
for
i\in[k-1]
.

We define the length of the path
P
as
\ell(P)=\ell(V(P))
(also denoted by
\mathrm{leng}(P)
), and its step
|P|
as the number of edges in
E(P)
. When we want to emphasize that
\mathrm{leng}(P)
is with respect to
G
, we will write
\mathrm{leng}(P)
as
\mathrm{leng}(P,G)
.
We define
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)
as the minimum length of simple paths from
u
to
v
. We define
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(S,T)
for two vertex sets
S,T
as
\min_{s\in S,t\in T}\mathrm{dist}_{G}(s,t)
. We say
S,T
are
h
-far if
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(S,T)\geq h
. We define the
diameter
of
S
as
\max_{u,v\in S}\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)
. When
G
is clear from the context, we omit
G
from the subscript.

Graph Arboricity.

Give graph
G
, a
forest cover
of
G
consists of sub-graphs
F_{1},F_{2},\ldots,F_{k}
of
G
where each
F_{i}
is an edge-disjoint forest and every edge of
G
occurs in some
F_{i}
.
k
is called the size of the forest cover and the
arboricity

\alpha
of graph
G
is the minimum size of a forest cover of
G
.

3.1
Flows and Demands

(Multicommodity) Flows.

A (multicommodity)
flow

F
in
G
is a function that assigns to each (directed) simple path
P
in
G
a flow value
F(P)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
. A path
P
is a
flow path
of
F
if
F(P)>0
, and we use
\mathrm{path}(F)
to denote the set of all flow paths of
F
. Without ambiguity, each flow path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F)
is also a multicommodity flow (which has only one flow path
P
with value
F(P)
) and all the notations below can apply on
P
. The value of
F
is
\mathrm{value}(F)=\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}(F)}F(P)
.
We have the following parameters for a flow
F
.

•

Length
: The length of
F
is
\mathrm{leng}(F)=\max_{P\in\mathrm{path}(F)}\mathrm{leng}(P)
, and again we write
\mathrm{leng}(F)
as
\mathrm{leng}(F,G)
to emphasize that it is with respect to
G
. We say
F
is an
h
-length flow if
\mathrm{leng}(F)\leq h
.

•

Step
: The step of
F
is
\mathrm{step}(F)=\max_{P\in\mathrm{path}(F)}\mathrm{step}(P)
.

•

Congestion
: For each vertex
v\in V(G)
, the amount of
flow units
allocated on
v
by
F
is
F(v)=\sum_{v\in V(P),P\in\mathrm{path}(F)}F(P)
.
The congestion of
F
is
\mathrm{cong}(F)=\max_{v\in V(G)}F(v)/U(v)
.

For a flow path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F)
, a
flow subpath

\hat{P}
of
P
is a subpath of
P
, and
\hat{P}
also represents a multicommodity flow which has only one flow path
\hat{P}
with value
\mathrm{value}(P)
. A flow
F^{\prime}
is a
subflow
of
F
if
F^{\prime}(P)\leq F(P)
for each simple path
P
. Given two flows
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
,
F_{1}+F_{2}
denotes the flow where
(F_{1}+F_{2})(P)=F_{1}(P)+F_{2}(P)
for each simple path
P
.

A
path representation
of a flow represents the flow by storing all its flow paths and associated values. For
edge representation
, we define
\vec{E}
to be the directed counterpart of
E
, i.e. we put two opposite directed edges into
\vec{E}
for each edge in
E
. The
edge representation
of a flow
F
is representing the flow by storing
F(e)
for every edge
e\in\vec{E}
, where

F(e)=\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}(F),\vec{E}(P)\ni e}F(P).

Note that a flow path
P
has a direction, and we use
\vec{E}(P)
denote the directed edges on
P
consistent with
P
’s direction.

(Multi-Commodity) Demands.

A
demand

D:V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
assigns
a non-negative value
D(v,w)\geq 0
to each ordered pair of vertices in
V
to specify the units of demand
D(v,w)
vertex
v
wants to send to vertex
w
. We note that it is always safe to assume
D(u,u)=0
.
The size of a demand is written as
|D|
and is defined as
\sum_{v,w}D(v,w)
. The
support
of the demand
D
is defined as
\mathrm{supp}(D)=\{u,v\in V\mid D(u,v)>0\}
. For each vertex
v
, we let
D(v,\cdot)=\sum_{w}D(v,w)
and
D(\cdot,v)=\sum_{w}D(w,v)
denote the total demand starting and ending at
v
respectively.
The vertex set of
D
, denoted by
V(D)
, is the set of all vertices
v
with
D(v,\cdot)>0
or
D(\cdot,v)>0
.
We say a demand
D
is

•

h-length constrained
: if for each
v,w\in V
with
D(v,w)>0
, we have
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(v,w)\leq h
;

•

sub-demand for
D^{\prime}
: if for any pairs of
v,w\in V
,
D(v,w)\leq D^{\prime}(v,w)
;

•

A
-respecting for some node-weighting
A
: if for any
v\in V
,
\max\{D(v,\cdot),D(\cdot,v)\}\leq A(v)
;

For a flow
F
, the demand routed by/corresponding to
F
is denoted by
\mathrm{Dem}(F)
or
D_{F}
, where for each
u,v\in V(G)
,
D_{F}(u,v)=\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}(F)~\text{s.t. $P$ is a $(u,v)$-path}}F(P)
.
We say a demand
D
is routable with length
h
, congestion
\gamma
and step
t
if there is a flow
F
routing
D
with length
h
, congestion
\gamma
and step
t
.

Single-Commodity Demands and Flows.

A
single-commodity demand

D:V\to{\mathbb{R}}
assigns a (possibly negative) value
D(v)
to each vertex in
V
, satisfying that
\sum_{v\in V}D(v)=0
. We say a single-commodity demand
D_{\mathrm{sg}}
is
corresponding to
a multi-commodity demand
D_{\mathrm{mt}}
if for each
v\in V
,

D_{\mathrm{sg}}(v)=D_{\mathrm{mt}}(v,\cdot)-D_{\mathrm{mt}}(\cdot,v).

For a single-commodity demand
D
, we say a flow
F
is a
single-commodity flow routing
D
if
\mathrm{Dem}(F)
is corresponding to
D
.

k
-Commodity Demands and Flows.

For an integer
k
, a
k
-commodity demand

D
is a collection of
k
single-commodity demand
D_{1},D_{2},...,D_{k}
. A
k
-commodity flow

F
is a collection of
k
flows
\{F_{i}\mid i\in[k]\}
, and we call
F_{i}
the
i
-th commodity flow of
F
. We say a
k
-commodity flow
F
routes a
k
-commodity demand
D
if each
F_{i}
routes
D_{i}
. The
(
k
-commodity) edge representation
of
F
stores the edge representation of each
F_{i}
.

3.2
Neighborhood covers.

We define neighborhood cover as follows.

3.1
Neighborhood covers.

Given a graph
G=(V,E)
with vertex length, a
neighborhood cover

{\mathcal{N}}
is a collection of sets called
clusterings

{\mathcal{S}}_{1},...,{\mathcal{S}}_{\omega}
where each
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
contains vertex disjoint subsets of
V
, we care about the following parameters of a neighborhood cover.

1.

Covering radius
h_{\mathrm{cov}}
:
Every subset
A\subseteq V
with diameter at most
h_{cov}
is a subset of some
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
for some
i
.

2.

Diameter
h_{\mathrm{diam}}
:
For every
i
and every
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
,
S
has diameter at most
h_{\mathrm{diam}}
.

3.

Separation-factor
s
:
For every
i
, every two different sets in
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
are
\left(s\cdot h_{\mathrm{diam}}\right)
-far.

4.

Width:
the number of clustering, denoted by
w
.

The following theorem is an algorithm for neighborhood cover for graphs with vertex length. It is implied by previous works about neighborhood cover for graphs with edge lengths.

3.2
.

There exists an algorithm that is given a graph
G
with vertex lengths, parameters
h_{\mathrm{cov}},s>1
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, output a neighborhood cover with covering radius
h_{\mathrm{cov}}
, separation-factor
s
, diameter and width

h_{\mathrm{diam}}=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\cdot O(s)^{O(1/\epsilon)}\cdot h_{\mathrm{cov}}\qquad\qquad\omega=n^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot\log n

in
h_{\mathrm{diam}}\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
depth and
m\cdot h_{\mathrm{diam}}\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
work with high probability.

Proof.

The theorem follows from the algorithm by the Lemma 8.15 of
[
HRG22
]
, which is restated as Theorem 5.32 from
[
HHT24
]
with the same parameters under the setting of edge length. We show that we could transfer a vertex graph into a corresponding graph with edge length, and apply the algorithm from
[
HRG22
]
to get the same result.
Namely, let
G_{v}=(V,E)
be a graph with vertex length
\ell_{G_{v}}
, we create a copy of it and denote it as
G_{e}=(V,E)
. For any edge
\{u,w\}
in
E
, assign
(\ell_{G_{v}}(u)+\ell_{G_{v}}(w))/2
to its length
\ell_{G_{e}}(\{u,w\})
.
We note that from the construction, we have that for any pair of vertices
u,w\in V
,
\mathrm{dist}_{G_{e}}(u,w)\leq\mathrm{dist}_{G_{v}}(u,w)\leq 2\mathrm{dist}_{G_{e}}(u,w)
.
We then apply the algorithm from
[
HRG22
]
with the same covering radius
h_{\mathrm{cov}}
and separation-factor
s
over the graph
G_{e}
, the resulting neighborhood cover is also a valid one for the graph
G_{v}
with the required parameters.
∎

When we do not care about the separation factor, there is a more efficient algorithm.

3.3
.

Given an undirected graph
G
with vertex lengths and parameters
h_{\mathrm{cov}}
and
\beta
, there is an algorithm that computes a neighborhood cover
{\cal N}
in
G
with covering radius
h_{cov}
, diameter
h_{\mathrm{diam}}=\beta\cdot h_{\mathrm{cov}}
and width
\omega=n^{O(1/\beta)}\log n
in
h_{\mathrm{diam}}\cdot n^{O(1/\beta)}
depth and
m\cdot h_{\mathrm{diam}}\cdot n^{O(1/\beta)}
work.

3.3
Length-Constrained Vertex Expander Decomposition

The goal of this section is to introduce the concept of length-Constrained vertex expander decomposition. We start with defining concepts related to length-constrained vertex expanders.

Node-Weightings

A
node-weighting

A:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
assigns a non-negative value
A(v)
to a vertex
v
. The
size
of
A
is denoted by
|A|=\sum_{v}A(v)
and let
\mathrm{supp}(A):=\{v:A(v)>0\}
. For two node-weightings
A,A^{\prime}
we define
\min(A,A^{\prime})

and
A+A^{\prime}
as pointwise operations, and we write
A\preceq A^{\prime}
if
A
is pointwise at most
A^{\prime}
. We say
A
restricting to a vertex set
S
(denoted by
A_{S}
) as another node-weighting which assigns the same weight on every vertex in
S
as
A
, and
0
weight for every other vertices.

For a demand
D
and a node-weighting
A
, we say
D
is
A
-respecting if for each vertex
v
,
D(v,\cdot)+D(\cdot,v)\leq A(v)
.
For a flow
F
, We say
F
routes
A
to
B
for some node-weightings
A,B
with
|A|=|B|
if
F
routes a demand
D
with
D(v,\cdot)=A(v)
and
D(\cdot,v)=B(v)
.

Vertex Moving Cuts.

An
h
-length (moving) cut
C:V\rightarrow\{0,\frac{1}{h},\frac{2}{h},\ldots\}
on a graph
G
assigns to each vertex
v
a fractional cut value which is a multiple of
\frac{1}{h}
. The size of
C
is defined as
|C|=\sum_{v}U_{G}(v)\cdot C(v)
.
The length increase associated with the
h
-length moving cut
C
is defined as
\ell_{C,h}(v)=h\cdot C(v)
.

3.4
.

For a graph
G
with length function
\ell
we define
G-C
(or
G+\ell_{C,h}(v)
) as the graph with an updated length function
\ell_{G-C}
which assigns each vertex
v
the length
\ell_{G-C}(v)=\ell(v)+\ell_{C,h}(v)
.

3.5
h
-Length Separated Demand.

For any
h
-length moving cut
C
and demand
D
on graph
G
, we define the amount of
h
-length separated demand as the sum of demands between vertices that are
h
-length separated by
C
. We denote this quantity with
\operatorname{sep}_{h}(C,D)
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\operatorname{sep}_{h}(C,D)=\sum_{u,v:\mathrm{dist}_{G-C}(u,v)>h}D(u,v).

3.6
.

In the notation of
\operatorname{sep}_{h}(C,D)
, we hide the inherent graph
G
where the cut
C
and demand
D
is on. When using the definition, we assume we are working on the graph
G
where
C
and
D
are defined. When we want to emphasize it is on a specific graph
G
, we will add it in the subscript. The same simplification applies to the rest of the definitions.

3.7
h
-Length Sparsity of a Cut
C
for Demand
D
.

For any demand
D
and any
h
-length moving cut
C
on graph
G
with
\operatorname{sep}_{h}(C,D)>0
, the
h
-length sparsity of
C
with respect to
D
is the ratio of
C
’s size to how much demand it
h
-length separates i.e.,

\displaystyle\operatorname{spars}_{h}(C,D)=\frac{|C|}{\operatorname{sep}_{h}(C,D)}.

3.8
(h,s)
-Length Sparsity of a Cut w.r.t. a Node-Weighting.

The
(h,s)
-length sparsity of any
h\cdot s
-length moving cut
C
with respect to a node-weighting
A
is defined as:

\displaystyle\operatorname{spars}_{(h,s)}(C,A)=\min_{A\text{-respecting h-length demand}\ D}\operatorname{spars}_{h\cdot s}(C,D).

We say
C
is an
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut (with respect to
A
on
G
) if
\operatorname{spars}_{(h,s)}(C,A)\leq\phi
.

Intuitively,
(h\cdot s)
-length sparsity of a cut measures how much it
h\cdot s
-length separates
h
-length demand w.r.t its own size.
Furthermore, for a given node-weighting, we associate the sparsest cut w.r.t the node-weighting with its conductance.

3.9
(h,s)
-Length Conductance of a Node-Weighting.

The
(h,s)
-length conductance of a node-weighting
A
in a graph
G
is defined as the
(h,s)
-length sparsity of the sparsest
h\cdot s
-length moving cut
C
with respect to
A
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\operatorname{cond}_{(h,s)}(A)=\min_{h\cdot s\text{-length moving cut }C}\operatorname{spars}_{(h,s)}(C,A).

3.10
(h,s)
-Length
\phi
-Expanding Node-Weightings.

We say a node-weighting
A
is
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expanding if the
(h,s)
-length conductance of
A
in
G
is at least
\phi
.

The above definition of length-constrained expanders characterizes them in terms of conductance. The below fact from
[
HLSW25
]
(see their Theorem 4.2) exactly characterizes length-constrained expanders as those graphs that admit both low congestion and low dilation routings under the setting of vertex capacity adapted from
[
HRG22
]
.

3.11
Routing Characterization of Length-Constrained Expanders,
[
HLSW25
]
.

Given graph
G
with vertex lengths and capacities and a node-weighting
A
, for any
h\geq 1
,
\phi<1
and
s\geq 1
we have:

•

Length-Constrained Expanders Have Good Routings
If
A
is
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expanding in
G
, then every
h
-length
A
-respecting demand can be routed in
G
with congestion at most
O(\frac{\log n}{\phi})
and dilation at most
h\cdot s
.

•

Not Length-Constrained Expanders Have a Bad Demand
If
A
is not
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expanding in
G
, then some
h
-length
A
-respecting demand cannot be routed in
G
with congestion at most
\frac{1}{2\phi}
and dilation at most
\frac{h\cdot s}{2}
.

The following definition is about length-constrained Vertex Expander Decomposition.

3.12
Length-Constrained Vertex Expander Decomposition.

Given a graph
G=(V,E)
, an
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition for a node-weighting
A
with length slack
s
and cut slack
\kappa
is an
h\cdot s
-length cut
C
of size at most
\kappa\cdot\phi|A|
such that
A
is
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expanding in
G-C
.

4
Length-Constrained Vertex Expander Decompositions

We show the first efficient parallel length-constrained vertex expander decomposition algorithm, which is also associated with an expander routing algorithm. The proof is deferred to the appendix.

4.1
.

There is a randomized algorithm
\textsc{VertexLC-ED}(G,A,h,\phi,\epsilon)
that takes inputs an undirected graph
G
with vertex capacities and lengths, a node-weighting
A
, length bound
h\geq 1
, conductance
\phi\in(0,1)
and a parameter
\epsilon
satisfying
0<\epsilon<1
, outputs a
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition
C
for
G,A
with cut slackness
\kappa
where

\displaystyle\kappa=n^{O(\epsilon)}\qquad\qquad s=\exp(\exp(\frac{O(\log(1/\epsilon))}{\epsilon}))

in
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}
work and
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}
depth.
7
7

7

We note that our length slackness parameter
s
is doubly exponential in
1/\varepsilon
, whereas in
[
HHT24
]
a theorem on length-constrained expander decompositions for graphs with edge lengths and capacities claimed that
s
is only single exponential in
1/\varepsilon
. Importantly, this discrepancy does not arise from our focus on vertex lengths and capacities. After discussions with the authors of
[
HHT24
]
, we have confirmed that their paper contains an error, and that a straightforward correction yields a length slackness that is doubly exponential in
1/\varepsilon
.

Moreover, given an
h
-length
A
-respecting demand
D
, there is an algorithm that outputs a path representation of a flow routing
D
in
G-C
with length
h\cdot s

and congesiton
n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}/\phi

in work
(|\mathrm{supp}(D)|+m)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}
and depth
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}

5
Length-Constrained Flow Shortcut with Length Slack
(1+\epsilon)

In this section, we will give an algorithm for computing an LC-flow shortcut with length slack
(1+\epsilon)
for arbitrarily small
\epsilon
on undirected graphs with vertex lengths and congestion. The definition of LC-flow shortcuts is given in
Definition

5.1
.

Before the definition, we need the following concept of
path-mappings between flows
.
Let
F^{G}
and
F^{H}
be two flows (which can be on different graphs
G
and
H
) routing the same demand. A
path-mapping
from
F^{G}
to
F^{H}
, denoted by
\pi
, is a mapping from flow paths
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})
to subflows
F^{H}_{P}
of
F^{H}
, satisfying that

•

\mathrm{Dem}(P)=\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H}_{P})
(which implies
\mathrm{value}(P)=\mathrm{value}(F^{H}_{P})
) for each
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})
, and

•

\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})}F^{H}_{P}=F^{H}
.

This path-mapping
\pi
has length slack
(\lambda,\delta)
if each
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})
has
\mathrm{leng}(F^{H}_{P})\leq\lambda\cdot\mathrm{leng}(P)+\delta
. Here we call
\lambda
and
\delta
the multiplicative and additive length slack respectively. When the additive length slack
\delta
is zero, we simply say
\pi
has length slack
\lambda
. When the multiplicative length slack
\lambda
is one, we simply say
\pi
has additive length slack
\delta
.

5.1
Length-Constrained Flow Shortcut.

Given a graph
G=(V,E)
with vertex length and capacities, we say an edge set
H
(possibly with endpoints outside
V
) is an
t
-step LC-flow shortcut
of
G
with
length slack

\lambda
and
congestion slack

\kappa
if the following holds.

•

(Forward Mapping) For every feasible flow
F^{G}
in
G
, there is a flow
F^{H}
in
G\cup H
routing
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{G})
with congestion 1 and step
t
. Furthermore, there is a path-mapping from
F^{G}
to
F^{H}
with length slack
\lambda
.

•

(Backward Mapping) For every feasible flow
F^{H}
in
G\cup H
such that
V(\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H}))\subseteq V(G)
, there is a flow
F^{G}
in
G
routing
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H})
with congestion
\kappa
. Furthermore, there is a path-mapping from
F^{H}
to
F^{G}
with length slack
1
.

We define a
backward mapping algorithm
associated with the LC-flow shortcut as follows. The input is an integer
k
and the edge representation of a
k
-commodity flow
F^{H}
in
G\cup H
routing some
k
-commodity demand
D
s.t.
V(D)\subseteq V
. The output is the edge representation of a
k
-commodity flow
F^{G}
in
G
routing
D
with
\mathrm{cong}(F^{G})\leq\kappa\cdot\mathrm{cong}(F^{H})
and
\mathrm{totlen}(F^{G})\leq\mathrm{totlen}(F^{H})
.

Before we state our result on LC-flow shortcuts, we make some comments on
Definition

5.1
.

•

We talk about path-mappings between flows in both forward and backward mappings because we want the length slacks to be
path-competitive
. Existing a path-mapping with length slack
\lambda
is a strong and flexible property in the sense that, taking the forward mapping as an example, it will imply claims such as
\mathrm{leng}(F^{H})\leq\lambda\mathrm{leng}(F^{G})
and
\mathrm{totlen}(F^{H})\leq\lambda\mathrm{totlen}(F^{G})
. We note that our analysis for forward/backward mappings will actually map each flow path separately, so we get path-mappings for free.

•

In the backward mapping algorithm, we talk about
k
-commodity flows because we want better work and depth when
k
is small. Also, when a flow is given in its edge representation, we lost the information of flow paths, so it no longer makes sense to talk about the length of the flow. However, we can still talk about total length (i.e. cost) because the total length is known given the edge representation. Moreover, we note that total length is exactly what we need when using LC-flow shortcuts in the future sections.

Theorem

5.2
states our result on LC-flow shortcuts.

5.2
.

Let
G
be an
n
-vertex
m
-edge undirected graph with vertex lengths and capacities. Let
1/\mathrm{polylog}(n)<\epsilon_{\ell}<1
and
\Omega(1/\log\log\log n)<\epsilon_{\kappa},\epsilon_{h}<1
be given parameters.
There is an algorithm that computes an LC-flow shortcut
H
of
G
of size
n^{1+\epsilon_{\kappa}}
with length slack
1+\epsilon_{\ell}
, congestion slack
n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}}
and step

t=\left(1/(\epsilon_{\ell}\epsilon_{h})\right)^{O\left(1/(\epsilon_{\kappa}\epsilon_{h})^{2}\right)}\cdot\exp(\exp(\log(1/(\epsilon_{\kappa}\epsilon_{h}))\cdot O(1/(\epsilon_{\kappa}\epsilon_{h}))))

in work
n^{O(\epsilon_{h}+\epsilon_{\kappa})}m
and depth
n^{O(\epsilon_{h})}
.

Moreover, the shortcut is associated with a backward mapping algorithm with work
n^{O(\epsilon_{h}+\epsilon_{\kappa})}(m+nk)
and depth
n^{O(\epsilon_{h})}
.

We make some comments on the work bound of the backward mapping algorithm. Recall that it takes the edge representation of a
k
-commodity flow
F^{H}
in
G\cup H
as input, which means the input size is already
mk
. However, we can achieve
m+nk
time because we assume the edge representation of
F^{H}
is already stored in the memory, and our algorithm just needs to update part of it. Roughly speaking, we just perform backward mapping for the restriction of
F^{H}
on
H
, and
H
only has size roughly
n
. We point out that a roughly
mk
work bound is already sufficient when we apply this theorem later.

Also, the restrictions
\epsilon_{\ell}>1/\mathrm{polylog}(n)
and
\epsilon_{\kappa},\epsilon_{h}>\Omega(1/\log\log\log n)
are mainly added for convenience (so they are not tight). That is, this enable us to hide some terms purely depending on
\epsilon_{\ell},\epsilon_{\kappa},\epsilon_{h}
into
n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
or
n^{O(\epsilon_{h})}
. For example, we can hide
\exp(\exp(1/\epsilon_{\kappa}))
into
n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}}
.

By choosing parameters
\epsilon_{\kappa}=\epsilon_{h}=\Theta(1/\log\log\log n)
, we immediate obtain the following cleaner version of
Theorem

5.2
.

5.3
.

Let
G
be an
n
-vertex
m
-edge undirected graph with vertex lengths and capacities. Let
1/\mathrm{polylog}(n)<\epsilon<1
be given parameters.
There is an algorithm that computes an LC-flow shortcut
H
of
G
of size
\hat{O}(n)
with length slack
1+\epsilon
, congestion slack
\hat{O}(1)
and step
\hat{O}(1)

in work
\hat{O}(m)
and depth
\hat{O}(1)
.
Moreover, the shortcut is associated with a backward mapping algorithm with work
\hat{O}(m+nk)
and depth
\hat{O}(1)
.

5.1
An Algorithm for Small Length Bound

In this section, we will provide an algorithm for efficiently constructing an LC-flow shortcut only for small enough (subpolynomial) length bound
h
. The exact trade-off between parameters is described in
Lemma

5.5
.

For some technical reason, we need to strengthen the definition of shortcut
Definition

5.1
. The difference is that we require the forward mapping to preserve the congestion of the original vertices in
V
, which will be exploited when showing the congestion slack of the forward mapping.

5.4
Path-mapping
V
-Preserving LC-Flow Shortcut.

Given a graph
G=(V,E)
with vertex length and capacities, we say an edge set
H
(possibly with endpoints outside
V
) is a
path-mapping
V
-preserving
h
-length
t
-step LC-flow shortcut
of
G
with
additive length slack

\delta
and
congestion slack

\kappa
if

•

(Forward Mapping) for every flow
F^{G}
in
G
with congestion
1
and length
h
, there is a flow
F^{H}
with congestion
1
and step
t
in
G\cup H
with
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{G})=\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H})
and for every
v\in V
we have
F^{H}(v)\leq F^{G}(v)
. Furthermore, there is a path-mapping from
F^{G}
to
F^{H}
with additive length slack
\delta
.

•

(Backward Mapping) for every flow
F^{H}
in
G\cup H
with congestion 1 such that
V(\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H}))\subseteq V(G)
, there is a flow
F^{G}
in
G
routing
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H})
with congestion
\kappa
.
Furthermore, there is a path-mapping from
F^{H}
to
F^{G}
with length slack
1
.

5.5
.

Let
G
be an
n
-vertex
m
-edge undirected graph with vertex lengths and capacities. Let
1/\mathrm{polylog}(n)<\epsilon_{\ell}<1
,
1/\Omega(\log\log\log n)<\epsilon_{\kappa}<1
, and
h\geq 1
be given parameters. There is an algorithm
\textsc{Shortcut}(G,\epsilon_{\ell},\epsilon_{\kappa},h)
computes a path-mapping
V
-preserving
h
-length
t
-step LC-flow shortcut of
G
of size
n^{1+O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
with additive length slack
\epsilon_{\ell}\cdot h
and congestion slack
n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}}
where

t=\left(1/\epsilon_{\ell}\right)^{O\left(1/\epsilon_{\kappa}^{2}\right)}\cdot\exp(\exp(\log(1/\epsilon_{\kappa})\cdot O(1/\epsilon_{\kappa}))).

in
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
depth and
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
work.

Moreover, the shortcut is associated with a backward mapping algorithm with
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
depth and
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot(m+nk)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
work.

Building Blocks.

There are three important building blocks for our algorithm: length-constrained vertex expander decomposition
Theorem

4.1
, neighborhood cover
Lemma

3.3
, and approximate length-constrained single-commodity max flows. We state them as below for convenience.

Theorem
4.1
.

There is a randomized algorithm
\textsc{VertexLC-ED}(G,A,h,\phi,\epsilon)
that takes inputs an undirected graph
G
with vertex capacities and lengths, a node-weighting
A
, length bound
h\geq 1
, conductance
\phi\in(0,1)
and a parameter
\epsilon
satisfying
0<\epsilon<1
, outputs a
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition
C
for
G,A
with cut slackness
\kappa
where

\displaystyle\kappa=n^{O(\epsilon)}\qquad\qquad s=\exp(\exp(\frac{O(\log(1/\epsilon))}{\epsilon}))

in
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}
work and
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}
depth.

Moreover, given an
h
-length
A
-respecting demand
D
, there is an algorithm that outputs a path representation of a flow routing
D
in
G-C
with length
h\cdot s

and congesiton
n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}/\phi

in work
(|\mathrm{supp}(D)|+m)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}
and depth
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}

Another important building block is neighborhood cover
Lemma

3.3
.

See
3.3

The last building block is a
0.5
-approximate length-constrained single-commodity max flow algorithm. We present here a simplified yet sufficient version in
Lemma

5.6
, and refer to
[
HHT24
]
or
Theorem

6.1
for detailed and generalized versions. We note that in the context of
Section

3.1
, an
h
-length single-commodity
s
-
t
flow
F_{s,t}
is just an
h
-length flow
partially
routing the single-commodity demand
D
where
D(s)=\infty
,
D(t)=-\infty
, and
D(v)=0
for other vertices
v
. It is feasible if it has congestion
1
. It is
0.5
-approximately maximum if
\mathrm{value}(F_{s,t})
is at least half of the maximum value of all such flows.

5.6
See
[
HHT24
]
or
Theorem

6.1
.

Let
G
be an undirected graph with vertex lengths and capacities. Given a source vertex
s
, a sink vertex
t
, and a length bound
h
, there is an algorithm that computes a feasible
h
-length single-commodity
s
-
t
flow
F_{s,t}
which is
0.5
-approximately maximum. The flow
F_{s,t}
is given in its path representation, which contains
\tilde{O}(m\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h))
flow paths. The work and depth are
\tilde{O}(m\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h))
and
\tilde{O}(\mathrm{poly}(h))
respectively.

Now we are ready to describe the algorithm
\textsc{Shortcut}(G,\epsilon_{\ell},\epsilon_{\kappa},h)
. The algorithm involves recursive calls to itself with a reduced number of vertices for the input graph
G
.

Global variables.

We fix
n
to be the number of vertices of the input graph of the root computation of the recursive algorithm, i.e.,
n
