does not change during the recursive calls. We let
c
to be a sufficiently large constant.

We will use the parameters
\kappa,s
from
Theorem

4.1
in our algorithm, which only depends on
n,\epsilon
. We fix
\epsilon
to be
\epsilon_{\kappa}/c^{2}
to
\textsc{Shortcut}(G,\epsilon_{\ell},\epsilon_{\kappa},h)
, among all recursive calls. i.e., we always have

\epsilon=\epsilon_{\kappa}/c^{2},\qquad\qquad\kappa\leq n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}/c},\qquad\qquad s=\exp\exp(\frac{O(\log(1/\epsilon_{\kappa}))}{\epsilon_{\kappa}}).

When we use
Theorem

4.1
, the input conductance
\phi
is a fixed value depending only on
n,\epsilon
, which we set to be

\phi=\frac{1}{n^{\frac{\epsilon_{\kappa}}{9}}}.

It follows that,

\phi\kappa=\frac{1}{n^{\frac{\epsilon_{\kappa}}{9}}}\cdot n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}/c}\leq\frac{1}{n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}/10}}.

We will use the parameters
\beta,\omega
from
Theorem

3.2
. Here
\omega
depends on
\beta,n
where we define
\beta
to be

\beta=\frac{c}{\epsilon_{\kappa}^{2}},

so that

\omega=n^{O(1/\beta)}\leq n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}^{2}/10}.

In the algorithm, we will use
\sigma
to denote the recursion parameter, i.e., every recursion decreases the graph size by a factor of
\sigma
. We fix
\sigma
to be

\sigma=n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}/6}.

The number of iterations in the algorithm is defined as

r=c\cdot\log_{\frac{1}{\kappa\phi}}N=O(\frac{1}{\epsilon_{\kappa}}).

Remember that
N=\mathrm{poly}(n)
is an upper bound for the summation of all the capacities.

It will be clear from the analysis why we define these parameters in this way, but let us give a high-level explanation here. As discussed in
Section

2.1
, our argument can be viewed as a two-dimensional induction. To distinguish steps along these two dimensions, we refer to the first as
recursions
and the second as
iterations
. We hope to bound both the recursion depth and number of iterations by
O(1/\epsilon_{\kappa})
, and as we will see, the former is controlled by
\sigma
and the latter is controlled by
\kappa\phi
, so we set them in this way. Regarding
\omega
, the intuition is that the total graph size at each recursion level increases by a factor of
\omega
, so we want
\omega\leq n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa}^{2})}
to obtain a size overhead of
n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
after
O(1/\epsilon_{\kappa})
levels.

Base case.

The base case is when
G
has fewer than
\sigma
vertices. However, we do not need to handle it separately because the following recursive algorithm
\textsc{Shortcut}(G,\epsilon_{\ell},\epsilon_{\kappa},h)
automatically covers this case (in which no further recursion is performed).

For better understanding, we describe a simpler and alternative treatment informally as follows. Because the number of vertices is at most
\sigma
, which is upper bounded by the desired congestion slack
n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}}
. We can simply create shortcuts between each pair of vertices. This introduces a factor of
\sigma^{2}
to the congestion slack, which is acceptable. Formally, we implement this by performing Step 5 once with each vertex treated as a singleton large cluster.

Recursive algorithm
\textsc{Shortcut}(G,\epsilon_{\ell},\epsilon_{\kappa},h)
.

Suppose the size of
G
is larger than a sufficiently large constant. The algorithm consists of

r

many iterations.
We set a node-weighting
A_{1}(v)=U_{G}(v)
.

We define the relax slackness for
\epsilon_{\ell}
as

\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}=\frac{\epsilon_{\ell}}{100\cdot 50^{r}}.

We define
h_{r}=h
, and we define recursively for each
i\in[r],

h_{\mathrm{diam},i}=\frac{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\cdot h_{i}}{s},\qquad\qquad h_{\mathrm{cov},i}=\frac{h_{\mathrm{diam},i}}{\beta},\qquad\qquad h_{i-1}=h_{\mathrm{cov},i}=\frac{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\cdot h_{i}}{\beta s}.

Now we formally describe the
i
-th iteration as follows. The
i
-th interaction will construct the shortcut
H_{i}
. Initially let
H_{i}=\emptyset
.

Step 1 (LC-expander decomposition).

We use
Theorem

4.1
to call

C_{i}\leftarrow\textsc{VertexLC-ED}(G,A_{i},h_{\mathrm{diam},i},\phi,\epsilon).

We set the node-weighting for the next iteration
A_{i+1}(v)=C_{i}(v)\cdot U_{G}(v)
.

Step 2 (neighborhood cover).

Define
G_{i}=G-C_{i}
. We use
Theorem

3.2
with input graph
G_{i}
, and covering radius
h_{\mathrm{cov},i}
.
Lemma

3.3
returns a neighborhood cover
{\mathcal{N}}_{i}
. We define
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}=\cup_{{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}}{\mathcal{S}}
.

Step 3 (recursions on small clusters).

Define
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{small},i}=\{S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}\mid|S|\leq n/\sigma\}
.
For every
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{small},i}
, we make the recursive call

H_{S}\leftarrow\textsc{Shortcut}(G[S],\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}},\epsilon_{\kappa},h_{\mathrm{cov},i}),

and add the returned LC-flow shortcut
H_{S}
to
H_{i}
.

Step 4 (stars on large clusters).

Define
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{large},i}={\mathcal{S}}_{i}-{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{small},i}
. For each
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{large},i}
, we construct a new artificial vertex
x_{S}
with capacity
\sum_{v\in V}U_{G}(v)
and length
1
. For each
v\in x_{S}
, we build a new artificial vertex
x_{S,v}
with length
h_{\mathrm{diam},i}\cdot s
and capacity
A_{i}(v)
. We build edges
(x_{S},x_{S,v})
and
(x_{S,v},v)
. We denote the set of these edges as
H_{S}
. We add
H_{S}
to
H_{i}
.

Step 5 (edges connecting large clusters).

For each pair of large clusters
S_{1},S_{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{large},i}
, construct

\bar{k_{i}}=\log_{1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}}(3h_{i})

nodes. For each
1\leq k\leq\bar{k}_{i}
, the
k
-th shortcut node denoted by
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k)
, has

length

(1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{k}+2h_{\mathrm{diam},i}\cdot s
, and

capacity

2
times the value of the
0.5
-approximate maximum
((1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{k}+2h_{\mathrm{diam},i}\cdot s)
-length single-commodity flow (with congestion 1) from
x_{S_{1}}
to
x_{S_{2}}
in
G_{i}\cup H_{S_{1}}\cup H_{S_{2}}
.

5.7
.

In the definitions, we require the length to be a positive integer. In the algorithm, we sometimes face a fractional length (for example
((1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{k}+2h_{\mathrm{diam},i}\cdot s)
. In this case, we can simply round it up or down with a small loss. For simplicity, we do not reflect the change in the algorithm unless the length is small enough so that rounding results in a large loss.

We can use
Lemma

5.6
to compute the capacity. Then we construct edges connecting
x_{S_{1}}
to the
\bar{k_{i}}
nodes, and edges connecting the
\bar{k_{i}}
nodes to
x_{S_{2}}
.

Outputs.

The returned LC-flow shortcut
H
for
\textsc{Shortcut}(G,\epsilon_{\ell},\epsilon_{\kappa},h)
is the union of
H_{i}
for all iterations
i\in[r]
.

Backward mapping algorithm.

We will describe and analyze the algorithm after the backward mapping correctness section in
Section

5.4
since it is almost identical to the backward mapping correctness proof.

Complexity of
Shortcut
.

Before proving the correctness, let us first calculate the complexity for the LC-flow shortcut part.

Step 1 takes
\mathrm{poly}(h_{\mathrm{diam},i})\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}=\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
depth and
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}\cdot m
work according to
Theorem

4.1
.

Step 2 takes
\mathrm{poly}(h_{\mathrm{diam},i})\cdot\omega=\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa}^{2})}
depth and
\mathrm{poly}(h_{\mathrm{diam},i})\cdot m\cdot\omega=\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m\cdot n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa}^{2})}
work according to
Theorem

3.2
.

Step 3 is a recursive procedure. We will analyze it in the end.

Step 4 can be done trivially.

Step 5 depends on the number of large clusters, which is calculated in the following lemma.

5.8
.

For every
i\in[r]
,
|{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{large},i}|\leq\omega\sigma\leq n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
.

Proof.

According to the definition of neighborhood cover
Definition

3.1
, a neighborhood cover contains
\omega
layers of clustering, where in each layer, different clusters are vertex disjoint. Thus, since each large cluster
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{large},i}
contains at least
n/\sigma
vertices, the number of large clusters in each layer is
\sigma
. There are
\omega
layers, so the lemma follows.
∎

Step 5 requires
(\omega\sigma)^{2}=n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
calling of approximate length-constrained single-commodity flow. Notice that the length bound is always at most
O(h_{i})=O(h)
. Thus, according to
Lemma

5.6
, the depth for each flow computation is
\tilde{O}(\mathrm{poly}(h))
depth and
\tilde{O}(\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m)
work. In total, the work becomes
n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m
.

Summing up all the steps, the depth is
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
and work is
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
besides the recursive call.

Now, we analyze the recursive procedure. According to the definition of neighborhood cover, each vertex is included in at most
\omega
many different clusters
S\in S_{i}
. There are
r
iterations. Thus, the
z
-th recursion layer contains
(\omega r)^{z}n
many vertices and
(\omega r)^{z}m
many edges. There are
\log_{\sigma}n=O(1/\epsilon_{\kappa})
many layers, thus, the total number of vertices and edges are at most
(\omega r)^{\log_{\sigma}n}=n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
factor of more vertices and edges than the original graph. For the input parameters, we decreased the input
\epsilon_{\ell}
and
h
in each layer of recursion. Decreasing the input for
h
can only decrease the complexity, and
\epsilon_{\ell}
does not affect the complexity. Thus, the complexity stated in
Lemma

5.5
follows.

Size of
H
.

Let us analyze the size of the output shortcut
H
. We only add edges to
H
by Step 4 and Step 5 besides recursion.

In Step 4, we add
O(|S|)
edges for each large cluster
S
. According to
Lemma

5.8
, there are at most
\omega\sigma rn=n^{1+O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
many edges added in this step.

In Step 5, we build
\bar{k_{i}}=\widetilde{O}\left(1/\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\right)=\tilde{O}(1)
(by our lower bounds on
\epsilon_{\ell}
and
\epsilon_{\kappa}
) edges between every two large clusters. This part is dominated by the number of edges in Step 4.

For recursion, as shown in the complexity analysis, the total number of vertices and edges among all recursions are
n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}\cdot n
and
n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}\cdot m
. Thus, the size of the returned shortcut
H
for the root recursion is
n^{1+O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
.

In the next two sections, we will prove that the returned edge set is indeed a LC-flow shortcut satisfying the parameters specified by
Lemma

5.5
. According to the definition of LC-flow shortcut
Definition

5.4
, we need to verify forward mapping and backward mapping.

Induction hypothesis and base cases.

The proofs are based on induction on the size of the input graph to
Shortcut
during the recursive calls, for which we denote by
G^{\prime}
.

5.9
Induction hypothesis for
\textsc{Shortcut}(G^{\prime},\epsilon_{\ell}^{\prime},\epsilon_{\kappa},h)
.

Let
G^{\prime}
be a graph with
n^{\prime}\leq n
vertices. The algorithm
\textsc{Shortcut}(G^{\prime},\epsilon_{\ell}^{\prime},\epsilon_{\kappa},h)
outputs a path-mapping
V
-preserving
h
-length LC-flow shortcut of
G^{\prime}
with additive length slack
\epsilon_{\ell}^{\prime}\cdot h
and the following congestion slack and step. Let
d^{\prime}=\lceil\log_{\sigma}n^{\prime}\rceil
. Then

\displaystyle\text{step}~~~~~~~~~t(n^{\prime},\epsilon_{\ell}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{40\beta s}{\epsilon_{\ell}^{\prime}}\right)^{(r+1)\cdot d^{\prime}}\cdot 50^{O(r^{2}\cdot(d^{\prime})^{2})},~\text{and}

\displaystyle\text{congestion}~~~~\kappa(n^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\omega r+1)^{d^{\prime}}\cdot n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}}.

When
n^{\prime}=n
and
\epsilon_{\ell}^{\prime}=\epsilon_{\ell}
, i.e., the root case, we plug in
\sigma=n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}/6},\beta=O(1/\epsilon_{\kappa}^{2}),s=\exp\exp(\log(1/\epsilon_{\kappa})\cdot O(1/\epsilon_{\kappa})),r=O(1/\epsilon_{\kappa})
and
\omega\leq n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}^{2}/10}
, and then obtain

\displaystyle t(n,\epsilon_{\ell})=(1/\epsilon_{\ell})^{O(1/\epsilon_{\kappa}^{2})}\cdot\exp\exp(\log(1/\epsilon_{\kappa})\cdot O(1/\epsilon_{\kappa})),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\kappa(n)=n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}

exactly as stated in
Lemma

5.5
(after scaling down
\epsilon_{\kappa}
by a large constant initially).

Base case.

The base case for
Lemma

5.9
is when
G
has fewer than
\sigma
vertices. Recall that we can choose not to handle it separately, and the following full argument shows that the returned LC-flow shortcut has desired guarantee. Or we can use the simpler alternative treatment. In this case, the output is clearly a
V
-preserving LC-flow shortcut with length slack
1+\epsilon_{\ell}
, congestion slack
n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}}

In the next several sections, we will assume all the recursive calls are correct (since they reduced the graph size by at least
1
) and assume
G
has
n^{\prime}\leq n
vertices, the input length slackness is
\epsilon_{\ell}
to prove
Lemma

5.9
.

5.2
Proof of Correctness: Forward Mapping

We will use induction to prove the following lemma holds for every
i
. Recall that we defined
G_{i}=G-C_{i}
where
C_{i}
is a
(h_{\mathrm{diam},i},s)
-length moving cut returned in step
1
.

5.10
Induction hypothesis for forward mapping.

For every
0\leq i\leq r
, for every
3h_{i}
-length flow
F^{G}
in
G_{i}
with congestion
1
, there is a flow
F^{H}
in
G\cup\left(\cup_{j\leq i}{H_{j}}\right)
routing
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{G})
with step
t^{(i)}
and congestion
1
. Furthermore, there is a path-mapping from
F^{G}
to
F^{H}
with additive length slackness
\epsilon_{\ell}^{(i)}\cdot h_{i}

such that

\epsilon_{\ell}^{(i)}=\frac{\epsilon_{\ell}}{2\cdot 50^{r-i}}\qquad\qquad t^{(i)}=\left(\frac{40\beta s}{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}}\right)^{i+1}\cdot t(n^{\prime}/\sigma,\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})

where
t(n^{\prime}/\sigma,\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})
denotes the function specified in
Lemma

5.9
. We do not write
\epsilon_{\kappa}
as a parameter to
t
because
\epsilon_{\kappa}
never changes during the recursive calls.

Moreover, for every
v\in V
, we have
F^{H}(v)\leq F^{G}(v)
.

Base case.

For convenience, we define
G_{0}=G-C_{0}
where
C_{0}
is a
(h_{\mathrm{diam},0},s)
-length moving cut such that
C_{0}(v)=1
for every
v
, even though there is no ‘
0
-th iteration’. The reason for defining
G_{0}
is that
Lemma

5.10
is easy to verify for the base case when
i=0
. However, we point out that this is just for formality. One can also view
i=1
as the base case and prove its guarantees directly using (a simplified version) of the general-case argument.

In
G-C_{0}
, every vertex has length at least
sh_{\mathrm{diam},0}
since
C_{0}(v)=1
for every
v\in V
. Thus, every
3h_{0}
-length flow
F^{G}
in
G-C_{0}=G_{0}
has step at most
3/\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}
, and
F^{G}
automatically has step at most
3/\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}
in
G
, satisfying
Lemma

5.10
. In the rest of the section, we will prove
Lemma

5.10
for
i>0
while assuming it is correct for every value less than
i
.

Ending case.

Before going into the induction step, let us first see
Lemma

5.10
implies the required forward mapping when
i=r
.

Remember that we set
A_{i+1}(v)=C_{i}(v)\cdot U(v)
. According to
Theorem

4.1
, the size of
A_{i+1}
decreases by a factor of
\kappa\phi
in each iteration. Initially
A_{1}
has size
N
, so after
r
iterations, the size of
A_{r}
decreases to at most
\frac{1}{N}
. This implies that the size of
C_{r}
is at most
\frac{1}{N}
. Notice that in
G_{r}=G-C_{r}
, the total length increase on all vertices is at most
\sum_{v}C_{r}(v)\cdot h_{r}\leq\frac{h_{r}}{N}
. Thus, we get that for every
h
-length flow
F^{G}
in
G
, which is a
h_{r}+\frac{h_{r}}{N}
-length flow in
G_{r}
, with congestion
1
, there is a flow
F^{H}
in
G\cup H
routing
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{G})
with congestion
1
and step
t^{(r)}
. Furthermore, there is a path-mapping from
F^{G}
for
F^{H}
with additive length slackness

\epsilon_{\ell}^{(r)}\cdot h_{r}+\frac{h_{r}}{N}\leq\epsilon_{\ell}\cdot h

and step

t^{(r)}=\left(\frac{40\beta s}{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}}\right)^{r+1}\cdot t(n^{\prime}/\sigma,\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})\leq t(n^{\prime},\epsilon_{\ell})

This is exactly the definition of forward mapping in
Definition

5.4
.

5.2.1
Construction of the Forward Mapping
F^{H}

Consider an arbitrary
3h_{i}
-length flow
F^{G}
in
G_{i}
. For each flow path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})
, we will construct a flow
F^{H}
in
H
routing
\mathrm{Dem}(P)
as follows.

Decompose the Flow Path
P
.

We first decompose
P
into flow subpaths
\hat{\cal P}
using the following lemma.

5.11
.

A path
P
can be decomposed into
z\leq 10\mathrm{leng}(P)/h_{\mathrm{cov},i}
subpaths
\hat{\cal P}=\{\hat{P}_{1},...,\hat{P}_{z}\}
such that

1.

\mathrm{start}(\hat{P}_{1})=\mathrm{start}(P),\mathrm{end}(\hat{P}_{z})=\mathrm{end}(P)
, for every
i\in[z-1]
,
\mathrm{end}(\hat{P}_{i})=\mathrm{prec}(\mathrm{start}(\hat{P}_{i+1}))
,

2.

each
\hat{P}_{i}
either

•

contains exactly
1
nodes, which we call a
trivial path
, or

•

has length at most
h_{\mathrm{cov},i}
.

Proof.

We will construct
\hat{P}_{i}
iteratively. Suppose we have constructed
\hat{P}_{i}
where
v=\mathrm{end}(\hat{P}_{i})
, we will construct
\hat{P}_{i+1}
based on
v
in the following way (if
i=0
, we define
v=\mathrm{start}(P)
): if
\ell(\mathrm{succ}(v))>h_{\mathrm{cov},i}/2
, then we let
\hat{P}_{i+1}
be the only vertex
\mathrm{succ}(v)
, which is a trivial path; otherwise, we let the subpath
\hat{P}_{i+1}
to start with vertex
\mathrm{succ}(v)
and end at the last vertex on
P
satisfying
\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P}_{i+1})\leq h_{\mathrm{cov},i}
.

Now we show that the number of paths is small. It is clear that paths are vertex-disjoint. The total length of all subpaths is at most
\mathrm{leng}(P)
. Thus, the number of trivial subpaths constructed in the first way is at most
2\mathrm{leng}(P)/h_{\mathrm{cov},i}
since each path has length at least
h_{\mathrm{cov},i}/2
. Moreover, let
\hat{P}_{i}
be a non-trivial path where
i<z
and
\hat{P}_{i+1}
is also a non-trivial path, then
\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P}_{i})\geq h_{\mathrm{cov},i}/2
since otherwise we can augment
\hat{P}_{i}
further. This means there are at most
2\mathrm{leng}(P)/h_{\mathrm{cov},i}+1
number of non-trivial paths even if we exclude all non-trivial paths before each trivial path. The total number of subpaths is at most
10\mathrm{leng}(P)/h_{\mathrm{cov},i}
.
∎

Remember that
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
contains all the clusters of a neighborhood cover with covering radius
h_{\mathrm{cov},i}
on the graph
G_{i}
, thus, each nontrivial subpath
\hat{P}\in\hat{\cal P}
corresponds to a cluster
S_{\hat{P}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
such that
\hat{P}
is totally inside
G[S_{\hat{P}}]
, and we call
\hat{P}
a
large-cluster subpath
(resp.
small-cluster subpath
) if
S_{\hat{P}}
is a large (resp. small) cluster.

We classify subpaths into
light subpaths
and
heavy subpaths
. For each subpath
\hat{P}\in\hat{\cal P}
, it is a light subpath if

C_{i-1}(\hat{P})\leq 1,

otherwise
\hat{P}
is a heavy subpath.

Let
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{HvLg},L}
(resp.
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{HvLg},R}
) be the leftmost (resp. rightmost) heavy and large-cluster subpath. Let
w_{L}
be the left endpoint of
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{HvLg},L}
and let
w_{R}
be the right endpoint of
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{HvLg},R}
.

We substitute the subpaths in
\hat{\cal P}
between
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{HvLg},L}
and
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{HvLg},R}
(including them) with one flow subpath
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump}}
(which is the subpath of
P
starting at
w_{L}
and ending at
w_{R}
), called the
jumping subpath
. Note that after substituting, each subpath
\hat{P}\in\hat{\cal P}
now belongs to one of the following types: trivial, small-cluster, light, and jumping.

Route the Flow Subpaths.

We will construct a routing
F^{H}_{P}
of
\mathrm{Dem}(P)
in
G\cup H
by routing
\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{P})
of each subpath
\hat{P}\in\hat{\cal P}
separately and concatenating them at the end.
Consider a subpath
\hat{P}\in\hat{\cal P}
.

Trivial Subpaths.
If
\hat{P}
is a trivial subpath, then we do not do anything: its routing
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}=\hat{P}
.

Small-cluster Subpaths.
Suppose
\hat{P}
is a small-cluster subpath. Since
\hat{P}
is not a trivial subpath, its length is at most
h_{\mathrm{cov},i}
. Let
S=S_{\hat{P}}
be the cluster containing
\hat{P}
. Remember that
H_{S}
is returned in Step 3 of the algorithm by
\textsc{Shortcut}(G[S],\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}},\epsilon_{\kappa},h_{\mathrm{cov},i})
. We collect all the flow subpaths contained in
S
and define

F_{S}=\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{P}\in\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}\\
S_{\hat{P}}=S\end{subarray}}\hat{P}

Notice that
F_{S}
is a
h_{\mathrm{cov},i}
-length flow. According to the definition of
H_{S}
,
\mathrm{Dem}(F_{S})
can be routed in
G\cup H_{S}
by a flow
F^{H}_{S}
with path mapping, congestion, and step guaranteed by
Lemma

5.5
. We will analyze the routing quality later. We define the routing for
\hat{P}
as the subflow in
F^{H}_{S}
routing the demand
\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{P})
, denoted by
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}
, notice that it satisfies the path mapping parameter stated in
Lemma

5.5
.

Light Subpaths.
Suppose
\hat{P}
is a light subpath. Notice that
\hat{P}
has length increase in
G_{i-1}=G-C_{i-1}
at most
s\cdot h_{\mathrm{diam},i-1}
according to the definition of light subpaths and that
C_{i-1}
is a
(h_{\mathrm{diam},i-1},s)
-length moving cut. Thus,
\hat{P}
in
G_{i-1}
has length at most

h_{\mathrm{cov},i}+s\cdot h_{\mathrm{diam},i-1}\leq 3h_{i-1},

because we define
h_{\mathrm{cov},i}=h_{i-1}
and
h_{\mathrm{diam},i-1}=\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\cdot h_{i}/s
. We collect all the light flow subpaths

F_{\mathrm{light}}=\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{P}\in\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}\\
\hat{P}\text{ is light}\end{subarray}}\hat{P}

Notice that
F_{\mathrm{light}}
has length at most
3h_{i-1}
. According to the induction hypothesis
Lemma

5.10
, there is a flow
F^{H}_{\mathrm{light}}
and a path mapping from
F_{\mathrm{light}}
to
F^{H}_{\mathrm{light}}
routing
\mathrm{Dem}(F_{\mathrm{light}})
in
G\cup H
with the quality specified by
Lemma

5.10
. We will analyze the quality later. We define
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}
as the mapped flow path in
F^{H}_{\mathrm{light}}
that routes the demand
\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{P})
.

Jumping Subpaths.
Lastly, consider the case that
\hat{P}
is the jumping subpath
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump}}=P[w_{L},w_{R}]
. This is the most complicated case and we need to first make some definitions.

Definition of flows routing
w_{L}
to vertices in
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{HvLg},L}
.

Let
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}
be the
shortest
prefix of
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{HvLg},L}
(so it is also a prefix of
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump}}
) such that
C_{i-1}(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}})\geq 1
. By the definition of heavy path, such a prefix always exists. Notice that
C_{i-1}(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}})\leq 2
since
C_{i-1}
assigns at most
1
to each vertex (otherwise we decrease it to
1
and it is still an length constraint expander decomposition). For every node
v\in V(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}})
, we define the flow path
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{pre},v}
as the subpath
P[w_{L},v]
carrying flow value
\mathrm{value}(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{pre},v})=\mathrm{value}(P)\cdot C_{i-1}(v)
. Notice that the length of
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{pre},v}
on the graph
G_{i-1}
is at most

h_{\mathrm{cov},i}+2\cdot h_{\mathrm{diam},i-1}s\leq 3h_{i-1}

because
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{pre},v}
has length at most
h_{\mathrm{cov},i}
on
G
, and
C_{i-1}
is a
(h_{\mathrm{diam},i-1},s)
-length cut.
We will also guarantee that the total flow value is exactly
\mathrm{value}(P)
, i.e.,

\sum_{v\in\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}}\mathrm{value}(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{pre},v})=\mathrm{value}(P)

This can be easily achieved by reducing the flow value of
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{pre},v}
for some vertices
v
.

We collect all
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{pre},v}
among all possible
P,v
and define

F_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}=\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})}\sum_{v\in\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}}\hat{P}_{\mathrm{pre},v}

Notice that
F_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}
is
(3h_{i-1})
-length. According to the induction hypothesis
Lemma

5.10
, there is a flow
F^{H}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}
and a path mapping routing
\mathrm{Dem}(F_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}})
in
G\cup H
with the quality specified by
Lemma

5.10
. We will analyze the quality later. We define
F^{H}_{\mathrm{pre},v}
as the mapped flow path in
F^{H}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}
that routes the demand
\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{pre},v})
.

Definition of flows routing vertices in
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{HvLg},R}
to
w_{R}
.

The definition is almost the same after changing
\mathrm{pre}
to
\mathrm{suf}
. For completeness we provide the full definition again as follows.

Let
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
be the
shortest
sufix of
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{HvLg},R}
(so it is also a sufix of
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump}}
) such that
C_{i-1}(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}})\geq 1
. By the definition of heavy path, such a prefix always exists. Notice that
C_{i-1}(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}})\leq 2
since
C_{i-1}
assigns at most
1
to each vertex. For every node
v\in V(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}})
, we define the flow path
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{suf},v}
as the subpath
P[v,w_{R}]
carrying flow value
\mathrm{value}(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{suf},v})=\mathrm{value}(P)\cdot C_{i-1}(v)
. Notice that the length of
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{suf},v}
on the graph
G_{i-1}
is at most

\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{suf},v})\leq h_{\mathrm{cov},i}+2\cdot h_{\mathrm{diam},i-1}s\leq 3h_{i-1}

The first inequality is because
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{suf},v}
has length at most
h_{\mathrm{cov},i}
on
G
, and
C_{i-1}
is a
(h_{\mathrm{diam},i-1},s)
-length cut.
We will also guarantee that the total flow value is exactly
\mathrm{value}(P)
, i.e.,

\sum_{v\in\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}}\mathrm{value}(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{suf},v})=\mathrm{value}(P)

This can be easily achieved by reducing the flow value of
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{suf},v}
for some vertices
v
.
We collect all
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{suf},v}
among all possible
P,v
and define

F_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}=\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})}\sum_{v\in\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}}\hat{P}_{\mathrm{suf},v}

Notice that
F_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
is
(3h_{i-1})
-length. According to the induction hypothesis
Lemma

5.10
, there is a flow
F^{H}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
routing
\mathrm{Dem}(F_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}})
in
G\cup H
with the quality specified by
Lemma

5.10
. We will analyze the quality later. We define
F^{H}_{\mathrm{suf},v}
as the mapped flow path in
F^{H}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
that routes the demand
\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{suf},v})
.

Now we are ready to define the flow
F^{H}_{\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump}}}
, which is a routing for
\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump}})
(
\mathrm{value}(P)
flows from
w_{L}
to
w_{R}
). We will route
\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump}})
by the following three steps.

Routing from
w_{L}
to
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}
.

For every
v\in\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}
, we send flow through the flow path
F^{H}_{\mathrm{pre},v}
. This guarantees that
\mathrm{value}(P)
units of flow in
w_{L}
are distributed to vertices in
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}
so that each vertex
v
gets
\mathrm{value}(F^{H}_{\mathrm{pre},v})\leq C_{i-1}(v)\cdot\mathrm{value}(P)
units of flow.

Routing from
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}
to
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
.

Denote the cluster in
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
containing
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{HvLg},L}
by
S_{L}
, denote the cluster in
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
containing
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{HvLg},R}
be
S_{R}
.

The routing first happens on the star shortcut
H_{S_{L}}
: for each vertex
v\in\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}
, we route the
\mathrm{value}(F^{H}_{\mathrm{pre},v})
units of flow from
v
to
x_{S_{L}}
through the vertex
x_{S_{L},v}
.

Then the routing happens through
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{L}},x_{S_{R}},k)
: Notice that now
x_{S_{L}}
cumulates
\mathrm{value}(P)
unites of flow. We pick the smallest integer
k
such that
(1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{k}\geq\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump}},G_{i})
, and route the
\mathrm{value}(P)
units of flow from
x_{S_{L}}
to
x_{S_{R}}
through the vertex
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{L}},x_{S_{R}},k)
.

At last the routing happens on the star shortcut
H_{S_{R}}
: for each vertex
v\in\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
, we route
\mathrm{value}(F^{H}_{\mathrm{suf},v})
units of flow from
x_{S_{R}}
to
v
through the vertex
x_{S_{R},v}
.

Routing from
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
to
w_{R}
.

For every
v\in\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
, we send flow through the flow path
F^{H}_{\mathrm{suf},v}
. This guarantees that
w_{R}
gets
\mathrm{value}(P)
units of flow and the routing finishes.

Concatenating the routing for flow subpaths.

We just defined
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}
for each
\hat{P}\in\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}
as a routing of value
\mathrm{value}(F^{H}_{\hat{P}})=\mathrm{value}(P)
from
\mathrm{start}(\hat{P})
to
\mathrm{end}(\hat{P})
. We construct the routing for
\mathrm{Dem}(P)
by concatenating all
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}
: sending
\mathrm{value}(P)
of flow from
\mathrm{end}(\hat{P})
to
\mathrm{start}(\hat{P}^{\prime})
where
\hat{P}^{\prime}
is the next subpath following
\hat{P}
in
\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}
, using the original edge connecting
\mathrm{end}(\hat{P})
to
\mathrm{start}(\hat{P}^{\prime})
.

This finishes the description of routing the demand
\mathrm{Dem}(P)
, for which we denoted by
F^{H}_{P}
. The flow
F^{H}
is the union of all the flows
F^{H}_{P}
for every flow path
P\in F^{G}
and the path mapping maps
P
to
F^{H}_{P}
.

5.2.2
Quality of the Forward Mapping
F^{H}

It remains to analyze the quality of
F^{H}
.

Length of
F^{H}_{P}
.

We will prove that there is a path-mapping from
P
to
F^{H}_{P}
with additive length slackness
\epsilon_{\ell}^{(i)}\cdot h_{i}
.

Consider a flow path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})
. We have that

\mathrm{leng}(F^{H}_{P})=\sum_{\hat{P}\in\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}\mathrm{leng}(F^{H}_{\hat{P}})

by the concatenation definition of
F^{H}_{P}
. We will analyze the length
\mathrm{leng}(F^{H}_{\hat{P}},G\cup H)
compared to
\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G_{i})
for different types of
\hat{P}
.

If
\hat{P}
is a
trivial subpath
, then
\mathrm{leng}(F^{H}_{\hat{P}},G\cup H)\leq\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G_{i})
since
G_{i}
only increases the length.

If
\hat{P}
is a
small-cluster subpath
, then
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}
is a subflow of
F^{H}_{S_{\hat{P}}}
. According to
Lemma

5.9
, we have that

\mathrm{leng}(F^{H}_{\hat{P}},G\cup H)\leq\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G_{i})+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\cdot h_{\mathrm{cov},i}\leq\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\cdot\frac{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\cdot h_{i}}{s\beta}

If
\hat{P}
is a
light subpath
, then on the graph
G_{i-1}
,
\hat{P}
has length at most
\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G)+s\cdot h_{\mathrm{diam},i-1}
because
C_{i-1}
is a
(h_{\mathrm{diam},i-1},s)
-length moving cut which has value at most
1
on
\hat{P}
. According to the induction hypothesis
Lemma

5.10
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{leng}(F^{H}_{\hat{P}},G\cup H)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G)+s\cdot h_{\mathrm{diam},i-1}+\epsilon_{\ell}^{(i-1)}\cdot h_{i-1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G_{i})+\left(\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}+\epsilon_{\ell}^{(i-1)}\right)\cdot\frac{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\cdot h_{i}}{s\beta}

If
\hat{P}
is a
jumping subpath
, then
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}
is composed by three parts. For the first and third parts, i.e., from
w_{L}
to
\hat{P_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}}
and from
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
to
w_{R}
, their lengths is by routing using
Lemma

5.10
. On
G_{i-1}
, the length of the path
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}
and
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
is at most
h_{\mathrm{cov},i}+2sh_{\mathrm{diam},i-1}
, because both
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}
and
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
have length at most
h_{\mathrm{cov},i}
on
G
and the value of the
(h_{\mathrm{diam},i-1},s)
-length cut
C_{i-1}
on
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}
and
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
is at most
2
. According to
Lemma

5.10
, the length of
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}
of the first and the thrid path is (similar to the light subpath)

h_{\mathrm{cov},i}+2sh_{\mathrm{diam},i-1}+\epsilon_{\ell}^{(i-1)}\cdot h_{i-1}

Now we analyze the second part of
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}
, i.e., from
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}
to
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
. This part consists of
5
vertices. (i) The vertex on the star
x_{S,v}
for some
v\in V(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}})
and
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
containing
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}
, this vertex has length
sh_{\mathrm{diam},i}
. (ii) The vertex on the center of the star
x_{S}
which has length
1
. (iii) The vertex
\mathrm{shortcut}(S_{1},S_{2},k)
for some
S_{1},S_{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
containing
\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}},\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
, and
k
is the smallest integer such that
(1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{k}\geq\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P}_{\mathrm{jump}},G_{i})
, which implies
(1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{k}\leq(1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G_{i})
. Thus, the length of
\mathrm{shortcut}(S_{1},S_{2},k)
is at most
(1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G_{i})+2sh_{\mathrm{diam},i}
. (iv) and (v), the last two vertices are symmetric to (i) and (ii). In total, this path has length

2(sh_{\mathrm{diam},i}+1)+(1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P})+2sh_{\mathrm{diam},i}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{leng}(F^{H}_{\hat{P}})

\displaystyle\leq(h_{\mathrm{cov},i}+2sh_{\mathrm{diam},i-1}+\epsilon_{\ell}^{(i-1)}\cdot h_{i-1})+(2(sh_{\mathrm{diam},i}+1)+(1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G_{i})+2sh_{\mathrm{diam},i})

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+2\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}+\epsilon_{\ell}^{(i-1)}\right)\cdot\frac{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\cdot h_{i}}{s\beta}+\left(4\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}h_{i}+2+\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G_{i})+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G_{i})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G_{i})+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G_{i})+\left(\frac{1+2\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}+\epsilon_{\ell}^{(i-1)}}{s\beta}+4\right)\cdot\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\cdot h_{i}+2.

Summary.
Summing up all the values above, we get the length of
F^{H}_{P}
. Remember that
\mathrm{leng}(P)\leq 3h_{i}
and the the total number of subpaths is at most

z\leq 10\cdot\frac{3h_{i}}{h_{\mathrm{cov},i}}=30\beta s/\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}

according to
Lemma

5.11
. There is exactly one jumping subpath and could be many small-cluster and light subpaths.

\displaystyle\mathrm{leng}(F^{H}_{P},G\cup H)-\mathrm{leng}(P,G_{i})\leq

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{30\beta s}{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}}\cdot\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\cdot\frac{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\cdot h_{i}}{s\beta}}_{\text{small-cluster subpath}}+\underbrace{\frac{30\beta s}{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}}\left(\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}+\epsilon_{\ell}^{(i-1)}\right)\cdot\frac{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\cdot h_{i}}{s\beta}}_{\text{light subpaths}}+

\displaystyle\underbrace{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\cdot\mathrm{leng}(P,G_{i})+\left(\frac{1+2\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}+\epsilon_{\ell}^{(i-1)}}{s\beta}+4\right)\cdot\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\cdot h_{i}+2}_{\text{one jumping subpath}}

By plugging in
\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}=\frac{\epsilon_{\ell}}{100\cdot 50^{r}},\epsilon_{\ell}^{(i-1)}=\frac{\epsilon_{\ell}}{2\cdot 50^{r-i+1}}
and
\mathrm{leng}(P,G_{i})\leq 3h_{i}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{leng}(F^{H}_{P})-\mathrm{leng}(P)

\displaystyle\leq\left(72\cdot\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}+31\cdot\epsilon_{\ell}^{(i-1)}\right)\cdot h_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon_{\ell}}{2\cdot 50^{r-i}}\cdot h_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{\ell}^{(i)}\cdot h_{i}

This finishes the length proof of
Lemma

5.10
.

Congestion of
F^{H}

Given that
F^{G}
has congestion
1
, we will show that (1)
F^{H}
has congestion
1
in
G\cup H
, i.e., for every vertex
v\in G\cup H
,
F^{H}(v)\leq U(v)
, and (2) the
V
-preserving-property, i.e.,
F^{H}(v)\leq F^{G}(v)
if
v\in V
. To this end, we categorize the vertices in
G\cup H
into different types.

Original vertices in
G
.
For a vertex
v\in V
where
V
is the vertex set of
G
, we will show that
F^{H}(v)\leq F^{G}(v)
. It suffices to show that for every
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})
, we have
F^{H}_{P}(v)\leq F^{G}_{P}(v)
since
F^{H}=\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})}F^{H}_{P}
and
F^{G}=\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})}F^{G}_{P}
(here we use
F^{G}_{P}
to denote the flow path
P
of
F^{G}
). Recall that we decompose
P
into vertex disjoint subpaths
\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}
and
F^{H}_{P}
is the concatenation of
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}
for
\hat{P}\in\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}
, we have
F^{H}_{P}(v)=\sum_{\hat{P}\in\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}}F^{H}_{\hat{P}}(v)
. Thus, it suffices to show
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}(v)\leq F^{G}_{\hat{P}}(v)
where
F^{G}_{\hat{P}}
is the flow subpath
\hat{P}
.

If
\hat{P}
is a trivial subpath, then
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}=\hat{P}
, so
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}(v)\leq F^{G}_{\hat{P}}(v)
.

If
\hat{P}
is a small-cluster subpath, then according to the induction hypothesis
Lemma

5.9
,
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}(v)\leq F^{G}_{\hat{P}}(v)
.

If
\hat{P}
is a light subpath, then according to the induction hypothesis
Lemma

5.10
,
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}(v)\leq F^{G}_{\hat{P}}(v)
.

If
\hat{P}
is a jumping subpath, according to the definition of jumping subpath,
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}(v)>0
only if
v
is on the subpath
\hat{P}
and
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}(v)\leq\mathrm{value}(P)
. Thus, we have
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}(v)\leq F^{G}_{\hat{P}}(v)
.

Vertices (in
H_{j}
) added in the
j
-th iteration for
j<i
.
Let
v
be a vertex not in
V
but in
H_{j}
for some
j<i
.
One can verify that only routing for light subpaths can go through
v
. This is because the routings for small-cluster subpaths use shortcut
H_{i}
(which is disjoint from
H_{j}
), and the routings for jumping subpaths use shortcuts constructed in Step 4 and 5, which is also in
H_{i}
. Thus, according to induction hypothesis
Lemma

5.10
, we have
F^{H}_{S}(v)\leq U(v)
.

Vertices (in
H_{i}
) added in Step 3 (recursions on small clusters).
Remember that in step 3, we add the LC-flow shortcut
H_{S}
for some
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
to
H_{i}
where
H_{S}
might contain vertices outside
V
. Let
v
be such a vertex. We need to show that
F^{H}(v)\leq F^{G}(v)
.

Recall that we define
F_{S}
as the union of all small-cluster flow subpaths whose corresponding small-cluster is
S
. According to the definition of flow subpaths, we have
F_{S}(w)\leq F^{G}(w)
for every
w\in V
. Thus,
F_{S}
has congestion
1
on
G
.
Remember that
F^{H}_{S}
is defined as the routing for
\mathrm{Dem}(F_{S})
according to the induction hypothesis
Lemma

5.9
.
Moreover, one can verify that only flow in
F^{H}_{S}
can go through
v\in H_{S}
: the routings for light subpaths are using induction hypothesis
Lemma

5.10
which only uses the shortcut
H_{j}
with
j<i
but we have
H_{S}\subseteq H_{i}
; the routing for jumping subpath is only using the shortcut for large clusters.
Thus, we have
F^{H}(v)\leq U(v)
according to the induction hypothesis
Lemma

5.9
.

Vertices (in
H_{i}
) added in Step 4 (stars on large clusters).
Let
v
be a vertex
X_{S}
for some
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
, then
X_{S}
has capacity
\sum_{v\in V}U_{G}(v)
, which is at least the value of
F^{G}
, so we must have
F^{H}(x_{S})\leq U(x_{S})
. We only need to consider the vertex
x_{S,w}
for some
w\in S
.

One can verify that only the routing
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}
for jumping subpaths
\hat{P}
can go through
x_{S,w}
: the routing for small-cluster subpaths only use
H_{S}
for small cluster
S
, and the routing for light subpaths only use
H_{j}
for
j<i
. Let
\hat{P}
be a jumping subpath, then
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}(x_{S,w})=\mathrm{value}(P)\cdot C_{i-1}(v)
according to the definition of
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}
. The total value of flow paths in
F^{G}
going through
w
is at most
U(w)
since
F^{G}
has congestion
1
. Thus, if we sum up all jumping subpaths, the value of flow going through
x_{S,w}
is at most
U(w)\cdot C_{i-1}(v)
. Remember that we define
A_{i}(v)=C_{i-1}(v)\cdot U(v)
, we have that
F^{H}(x_{S,w})\leq A_{i}(v)=U(x_{S,w})
, so the congestion on
x_{S,w}
is at most
1
.

Vertices (in
H_{i}
) added in Step 5 (edges connecting large clusters).
Let
v
be a vertex
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k)
for some large clusters
S_{1},S_{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
and
k\in[\bar{k_{i}}]
. One can verify that only the routing
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}
for jumping subpaths
\hat{P}
can go through
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k)
: the routing for small-cluster subpaths only use
H_{S}
for small cluster
S
, and the routing for light subpaths only use
H_{j}
for
j<i
.

Suppose
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}
for a jumping subpath
\hat{P}
goes through
v=\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k)
, according to the definition of
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}
, we have
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}(v)=\mathrm{value}(\hat{P})
and
\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P})\leq(1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{k}
. We define a flow
F_{\hat{P}}(S_{1},S_{2})
which routes
\mathrm{value}(\hat{P})
units of flow from
x_{S_{1}}
to
x_{S_{2}}
with length
\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P})
in the following way

1.

for every
w\in P_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}
,
x_{S_{1}}
sends at most
C_{i-1}(w)\cdot\mathrm{value}(\hat{P})
units of flow to
w
,

2.

along with path
\hat{P}
, the flows on
P_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{pre}}
goes to
P_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
such that every node
w\in P_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
gets at most
C_{i-1}(w)\cdot\mathrm{value}(\hat{P})
units of flow,

3.

for every
w\in P_{\mathrm{jump},\mathrm{suf}}
,
w
sends at most
C_{i-1}(w)\cdot\mathrm{value}(\hat{P})
units of flow to
x_{S_{2}}
.

Let
F(S_{1},S_{2})
be the summation of flows
F_{\hat{P}}(S_{1},S_{2})
for all jumping subpaths
\hat{P}
with
\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P})\leq(1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{k}
such that
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}
goes through
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k)
. It follows from the definition that
\mathrm{leng}(F(S_{1},S_{2}),G_{i})\leq 2h_{\mathrm{diam},i}+(1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{k}
. Moreover, every flow
F_{\hat{P}}(S_{1},S_{2})
corresponds to a flow path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})
, so
F(S_{1},S_{2})
cause congestion
1
on
G
. For star vertice,
F(S_{1},S_{2})
causes congestion
1
on them by the same argument as our analysis for vertices added in step 4. Thus,
F(S_{1},S_{2})
is a single-commodity flow with congestion
1
from
x_{S_{1}}
to
x_{S_{2}}
in
G_{i}\cup H_{S_{1}}\cup H_{S_{2}}
. By the definition of
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k)
, the congestion caused by
F^{H}
on it is at most
1
.

Step of
F^{H}

By the definition of path decomposition
Lemma

5.11
, for
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})
,
\mathrm{step}(F^{H}_{P})
is the summation of
\mathrm{step}(F^{H}_{\hat{P}})
for all
\hat{P}\in\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}
. We will analyze
\mathrm{step}(F^{H}_{\hat{P}})
based on different types of
\hat{P}
.

If
\hat{P}
is a
trivial subpath
, then
\mathrm{step}(F^{H}_{\hat{P}})=1
since a trivial subpath contains exactly
1
vertex.

If
\hat{P}
is a
small-cluster subpath
, remember that
\mathrm{step}(F^{H}_{\hat{P}})
is a subflow of
F_{S}
, where
F_{S}
is from a recursive call to
Lemma

5.5
. Thus, by the induction hypothesis
Lemma

5.9
, we have

\mathrm{step}(F^{H}_{\hat{P}})\leq t(n^{\prime}/\sigma,\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})

where we treat
t
as a function of
n
and
\epsilon_{\ell}
according to the parameter specified in
Lemma

5.5
. We do not write
\epsilon_{\kappa}
as a parameter to
t
because
\epsilon_{\kappa}
never changes during the recursive calls.

If
\hat{P}
is a
light subpath
, remember that
F^{H}_{\hat{P}}
is a subpath of
F_{\mathrm{light}}
, which according to the induction hypothesis
Lemma

5.10
has step

\mathrm{step}(F^{H}_{\hat{P}})\leq t^{(i-1)}

If
\hat{P}
is a
Jumping subpath
, according to the construction, the step is two times the step
t^{(i-1)}
plus a constant length path (with
5
vertices), thus,

\mathrm{step}(F^{H}_{\hat{P}})\leq 5+2t^{(i-1)}

Summary.
According to
Lemma

5.11
, the total number of subpath of a flowpath in
\mathrm{path}(F^{H})
is
z\leq\frac{30\beta s}{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}}
, thus,

\displaystyle\mathrm{step}(F^{H})

\displaystyle\leq z\cdot\left(1+t(n^{\prime}/\sigma,\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})+t^{(i-1)}\right)+5+2t^{(i-1)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{31\beta s}{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}}\cdot\left(t(n^{\prime}/\sigma,\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})+t^{(i-1)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{31\beta s}{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}}\cdot t(n^{\prime}/\sigma,\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})\cdot\left(1+\left(\frac{40\beta s}{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}}\right)^{i}\right)

\displaystyle\leq t(n^{\prime}/\sigma,\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})\cdot\left(\frac{40\beta s}{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}}\right)^{i+1}

\displaystyle=t^{(i)}

5.3
Proof of correctness: Backward Mapping

Let
F^{H}
be an arbitrary flow
F^{H}
in
G\cup H
with congestion
1
. We will prove the existence of a flow
F^{G}
in
G
with congestion
\kappa
and a path-mapping from
F^{H}
to
F^{G}
with length slack
1
. In the next section, we will show the correctness of the backward mapping algorithm, which is almost identical to the proof of backward mapping correctness.

Eliminating
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k)
.

We first construct a flow
F^{H^{\prime}}
in
G\cup H
routing the demand
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H})
such that every flow path of
F^{H^{\prime}}
does not contain Steiner vertices constructed in Step 5, i.e.,
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k)
.

Let
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{H})
and let
\hat{P}
be a flow subpath of
P
such that

\hat{P}=(x_{S_{1}},\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k),x_{S_{2}})

for some large clusters
S_{1},S_{2}
and some
k
. We will construct
F^{H^{\prime}}_{\hat{P}}
routing the demand
\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{P})
, then replace
\hat{P}
by
F^{H^{\prime}}_{\hat{P}}
for every such
\hat{P}
. This defines the flow
F^{H^{\prime}}
. Now we define the routing
F^{H^{\prime}}_{\hat{P}}
.

We define the largest
((1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{k}+2h_{\mathrm{diam},i}\cdot s)
-length single commodity flow from
x_{S_{1}}
to
x_{S_{2}}
in the graph
G\cup H_{S_{1}}\cup H_{S_{2}}
as
F^{H^{\prime}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}
. We define
F^{H^{\prime}}_{\hat{P}}
as scaling the flow
F^{H^{\prime}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}
to have flow value exactly
\mathrm{value}(\hat{P})
, i.e.,

F^{H^{\prime}}_{\hat{P}}=\frac{\mathrm{value}(\hat{P})}{\mathrm{value}(F^{H^{\prime}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k})}\cdot F^{H^{\prime}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}

It is easy to see that
\mathrm{value}(F^{H^{\prime}}_{\hat{P}})=\mathrm{value}(\hat{P})
and
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H^{\prime}}_{\hat{P}})=\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{P})
.

After defining
F^{H^{\prime}}
, we now continue to define the flow
F^{G}
that routes the demand
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H^{\prime}})
. For each flow path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{H^{\prime}})
, we will define a flow
F^{G}_{P}
routing the demand
\mathrm{Dem}(P)
. The final
F^{G}
will be the union of
F^{G}_{P}
for
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{H^{\prime}})
.

Decompose the Flow Path
P
.

We decompose
P
into subpaths
\hat{\cal P}=\{\hat{P}_{1},...,\hat{P}_{z}\}
such that

•

\mathrm{start}(\hat{P}_{1})=\mathrm{start}(P),\mathrm{end}(\hat{P}_{z})=\mathrm{end}(P)
and for every
i\in[z-1]
, we have
\mathrm{end}(\hat{P}_{i})=\mathrm{start}(\hat{P}_{i+1})
,

•

for every
\hat{P}\in\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}
, both
\mathrm{start}(\hat{P}),\mathrm{end}(\hat{P})
are in the original vertex set
V(G)
, all inner vertices
V(\hat{P})-\{\mathrm{start}(\hat{P}),\mathrm{end}(\hat{P})\}
are not in the original vertex set
V(G)
, i.e., they only contain Steiner nodes
V(G\cup H)-V(G)
.

Such a decomposition trivially exists. Now we categorize subpaths based on their inner vertices.

If
{\mathcal{P}}\in\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}
only contains original vertices
V(G)
, we call
{\mathcal{P}}
a
trivial subpath
.

For a small-cluster
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{small},i}
, notice that the way we construct
H_{S}
guarantees that Steiner nodes
V(H_{S})-V(G)
only have edges to
V(H_{S}\cup G)
. Thus, if
\hat{P}\in\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}
has inner vertices in
V(H_{S})-V(G)
, then all inner vertices of
\hat{P}
must be in
V(H_{S})-V(G)
. We call such path
small-cluster subpath
, and use
S_{\hat{P}}
to denote the corresponding small cluster.

If
{\mathcal{P}}
contains Steiner vertices as inner vertices, but they are not in
V(H_{S})-V(G)
for some small cluster
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{small},i}
. Remember the algorithm: step 5 constructs edges not connecting to the original vertices, step 3 constructs edges for small clusters, step 4 construct star shortcut connecting a large cluster. It must be the case that the second vertex of
{\mathcal{P}}
is
x_{S,v}
for some large cluster
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{large},i}
. Moreover,
{\mathcal{P}}
does not contain vertices constructed in Step 5. Thus, all inner vertices of
{\mathcal{P}}
are in
V(H_{S})-V(G)
. According to step 4, in this case, there are only three inner vertices of
{\mathcal{P}}
:
x_{S,v},x_{S},x_{S,w}
for some
v,w\in S
. We call
{\mathcal{P}}
a
large-cluster subpath
, and use
S_{\hat{P}}
to denote the corresponding large cluster.

Route the Flow Subpaths.

To construct the flow
F^{G}_{P}
, we will first construct, for each subpath
\hat{P}\in\hat{\cal P}
, a flow
F^{G}_{\hat{P}}
of
\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{P})
in
G
, and finally let
F^{G}_{P}
be the concatenation of all these flows
F^{G}_{\hat{P}}
. Consider a subpath
\hat{P}\in{\cal P}
.

Trivial subpaths.
If
{\mathcal{P}}
is a trivial subpath, we set
F^{G}_{\hat{P}}
to be just
{\mathcal{P}}
.

Small-cluster subpaths.
If
{\mathcal{P}}
is a small-cluster subpath with the corresponding small cluster
S
, we define the flow
F_{S}
collecting all the small-cluster subpath as follows

F^{H^{\prime}}_{S}=\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{H^{\prime}})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{P}\in\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}\\
S_{\hat{P}}=S\end{subarray}}\hat{P}

According to the induction hypothesis
Lemma

5.9
, there is a flow
F^{G}_{S}
on the original graph
G
routing the demand
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H^{\prime}}_{S})
with the parameter specified in
Lemmas

5.5
and
5.1
. We define
F^{G}_{\hat{P}}
as the subflow in
F^{G}_{S}
that routes the demand
\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{P})
.

Large-cluster subpaths.
If
{\mathcal{P}}
is a large-cluster suchpath with the corresponding large cluster
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
for some
i\in[r]
, we define the demand
D_{S}
collecting all the demand
\mathrm{Dem}({\mathcal{P}})
for all large-cluster suchpath as follows.

D_{S}=\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{H^{\prime}})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{P}\in\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}\\
S_{\hat{P}}=S\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{P})

The following lemma shows that
D_{S}
is not too large compared to node-weighting
A_{i}
so we can hope to use
Theorem

3.11
.

5.12
.

Suppose
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
for some
i\in[r]
. Then
D_{S}
is
A^{\prime}_{i}
-respecting for

A^{\prime}_{i}=\mathrm{cong}(F^{H^{\prime}})\cdot A_{i}

Proof.

We need to show that for every
v\in S
, we have
D_{S}(v,\cdot)\leq\mathrm{cong}(F^{H^{\prime}})\cdot A_{i}(v)
. The other direction
D_{S}(\cdot,v)\leq\cdot A_{i}(v)
is symmetric.

Notice that for a large-cluster subpaths
{\mathcal{P}}
such that
S_{{\mathcal{P}}}=S
, it can contribute
\mathrm{value}({\mathcal{P}})
to
D_{S}(v,\cdot)
only if
{\mathcal{P}}=(v,x_{S,v},x_{S},...)
. In other words,
{\mathcal{P}}
puts
\mathrm{value}({\mathcal{P}})
amount of flow on the vertex
x_{S,v}
. Notice that the total number of flow on
x_{S,v}
cannot exceed
U(x_{S,v})\cdot\mathrm{cong}(F^{H^{\prime}})
, we have

D_{S}(v,\cdot)\leq U(x_{S,v})\cdot\mathrm{cong}(F^{H^{\prime}})=A_{i}(v)\cdot\mathrm{cong}(F^{H^{\prime}})

This finishes the proof.
∎

Then we can use
Theorem

3.11
to prove the following lemma.

5.13
.

The demand
D_{S}
can be routed by a flow
F^{G}_{S}
of congestion
O(\mathrm{cong}(F^{H^{\prime}})\cdot\frac{\log n}{\phi})
and length
h_{\mathrm{diam},i}\cdot s
.

Proof.

Given
Lemma

5.12
, we have that
\frac{1}{\mathrm{cong}(F^{H^{\prime}})}\cdot D_{S}
is
A_{i}
-respecting. According to Step 1 (LC-expander decomposition), we have that
G_{i}
is a
(h_{\mathrm{diam},i},s)
-length
\phi
-expander. Notice that
D_{S}
is a
h_{\mathrm{diam},i}
-length demand since the diameter of
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
is guaranteed to be bounded by
h_{\mathrm{diam},i}
. According to
Theorem

3.11
,
\frac{1}{\mathrm{cong}(F^{H^{\prime}})}\cdot D_{S}
can be routed in
G_{i}
with congestion at most
O(\frac{\log n}{\phi})
and length at most
h_{\mathrm{diam},i}\cdot s
. Notice that vertex length in
G_{i}
is at least the vertex length in
G
. The lemma follows by scaling the flow up by a factor of
\mathrm{cong}(F^{H^{\prime}})
.
∎

We define the flow
F^{G}_{\hat{P}}
as the subflow of
F^{G}_{S}
routing the demand
\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{P})
.

Length of
F^{G}
.

We first analyze the length of
F^{H^{\prime}}
. Notice that
F^{H^{\prime}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}
has length at most
((1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{k}+2h_{\mathrm{diam},i}\cdot s)=\ell(\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k))
. Thus, by substituting the subpath
\hat{P}=(x_{S_{1}},\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k),x_{S_{2}})
with
F^{H^{\prime}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}
, then length does not increase. We have
\mathrm{leng}(F^{H^{\prime}})\leq\mathrm{leng}(F^{H})
. This length upper bound is path-wise, so the path mapping holds.

Compared to
F^{H^{\prime}}
, the routing in
F^{G}
for trivial subpaths does not increase the length, for small-cluster subpaths does not increase the length because of the induction hypothesis
Lemma

5.9
. The only non-trivial part is for large-cluster subpaths.

According to
Lemma

5.13
, for
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
,
\mathrm{leng}(F^{G}_{S})\leq h_{\mathrm{diam},i}\cdot s
. Let
\hat{P}
be a large-cluster subpath with
S_{\hat{P}}=S
, according to the definition, we must have

\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P})\geq h_{\mathrm{diam},i}\cdot s\geq\mathrm{leng}(F^{G}_{S})

The first inequality is because
\hat{P}
must contain a vertex
x_{S,v}
for some
v\in S
with length
h_{\mathrm{diam},i}\cdot s
. Thus, substituting
\hat{P}
by a subflow of
F^{G}_{S}
does not increase the length.

Congestion of
F^{G}
.

The rerouting for trivial subpaths does not change the congestion.

Small-cluster subpath.
We first need the following lemma certifying
F^{H^{\prime}}
does not increase the congestion of small-cluster subpath compared to
F^{H}
.

5.14
.

For every small cluster
S
, the collection of all small-cluster subpath
\hat{P}
with
S_{\hat{P}}=S
, denoted by
F^{H^{\prime}}_{S}
, satisfies
\mathrm{cong}(F^{H^{\prime}}_{S})=1
.

Proof.

For every small-cluster subpath
\hat{P}
, it corresponds to a subpath of
F^{H}
because
F^{H^{\prime}}
only creates the flow
F^{H^{\prime}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}
which does not contain any Steiner vertices of
H_{S}
for a small cluster
S
. Thus, according to
\mathrm{cong}(F^{H})=1
, we have
\mathrm{cong}(F^{H^{\prime}}_{S})=1
.
∎

Let
{\mathcal{N}}_{i}
be the neighborhood cover computed in the
i
-th iteration and let
{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}
be a vertex disjoint vertex sets. Let
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
be a small cluster, according to the induction hypothesis
Lemma

5.9
, since
F^{H^{\prime}}_{S}
has congestions
1
, the congestion of
F^{G}_{S}
is

\mathrm{cong}(F^{G}_{S})\leq\kappa(n^{\prime}/\sigma)

Notice that
F^{G}_{S}
only uses vertices in
S
. Vertex sets in
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
are disjoint. Thus, we can collect all small clusters and get

\mathrm{cong}\left(F^{G}_{\mathrm{small}}:=\sum_{i\in[r]}\sum_{{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in{\mathcal{S}}\\
S\text{ is a small cluster}\end{subarray}}F^{G}_{S}\right)\leq r\omega\cdot\kappa(n^{\prime}/\sigma)

Large-cluster subpath.
We first give the congestion bound of
F^{H^{\prime}}
.

5.15
.

\mathrm{cong}(F^{H^{\prime}})\leq 1+r\cdot\left(\omega\sigma\right)^{2}\cdot\bar{k}_{r}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k)
be a Steiner vertex constructed in Step 5, let
{\mathcal{P}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}
contain all subpath
\hat{P}
of some path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{H})
such that
\hat{P}=(x_{S_{1}},\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k),x_{S_{2}})
, then we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\hat{P}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}}F^{H^{\prime}}_{\hat{P}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\hat{P}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}}\frac{\mathrm{value}(\hat{P})}{\mathrm{value}(F^{H^{\prime}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k})}\cdot F^{H^{\prime}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{U(\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k))}{\mathrm{value}(F^{H^{\prime}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k})}\cdot F^{H^{\prime}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot F^{H^{\prime}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}

The first inequality is because
F^{H}
has congestion
1
. The second inequality is because of the definition of
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k)
and
F^{H^{\prime}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}
.

Notice that
F^{H^{\prime}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}
has congestion
1
, so
\sum_{\hat{P}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}}F^{H^{\prime}}_{\hat{P}}
has congestion
2
. We have

\mathrm{cong}(F^{H^{\prime}})\leq 1+\sum_{i\in[r]}\sum_{S_{1},S_{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{large},i}}\sum_{k\in[\bar{k}_{i}]}\mathrm{cong}(\sum_{\hat{P}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}}F^{H^{\prime}}_{\hat{P}})

The first term
1
comes from the original congestion from
F^{H}
. The second term is summing over all possible
S_{1},S_{2},k
. The size of
S_{\mathrm{large},i}
is provided in
Lemma

5.8
.

Finally, we get (remember that
\bar{k_{i}}
is increasing on
i
)

\mathrm{cong}(F^{H^{\prime}})\leq 1+r\cdot\left(\omega\sigma\right)^{2}\cdot\bar{k}_{r}

Notice that the flow
F^{H^{\prime}}_{S_{1},S_{2},k}
does not contain any Steiner vertices of
H_{S}
for a small cluster
S
. Thus, the lemma follows.
∎

By combining
Lemmas

5.8
,
5.13
and
5.15
, we can collect all the routings of large clusters and get

\mathrm{cong}\left(F^{G}_{\mathrm{large}}:=\sum_{i\in[r]}\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{large},i}}F^{G}_{S}\right)\leq r\cdot\omega\sigma\cdot\left(1+r\cdot(\omega\sigma)^{2}\cdot\bar{k_{r}}\right)\cdot\frac{\log n}{\phi}

Summary.
By summing up the congestion of
F^{G}_{\mathrm{small}}
and
F^{G}_{\mathrm{large}}
and plugging in the corresponding parameters, we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{cong}(F^{G})

\displaystyle\leq F^{G}_{\mathrm{small}}+F^{G}_{\mathrm{large}}

\displaystyle\leq r\omega\cdot\kappa(n^{\prime}/\sigma)+r\cdot\omega\sigma\cdot\left(1+r\cdot(\omega\sigma)^{2}\cdot\bar{k_{r}}\right)\cdot\frac{\log n}{\phi}

Remember that
\omega\leq n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}^{2}/10}
,
\sigma=n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}/6}
,
\phi=1/n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}/9}
,
\Omega(\log\log\log n)\leq\epsilon_{\kappa}\leq 1
and

\bar{k_{r}}=\log_{1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}}(3h)=\widetilde{O}\left(1/\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\right)=\tilde{O}(1)

because
1/\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}=\tilde{O}(1)
for
all
recursive calls in the recursion tree. Concretely, it is not hard to see
1/\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\leq 50^{O(r\cdot\lceil\log_{\sigma}n\rceil)}/\epsilon_{\ell}=50^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa}^{2})}/\epsilon_{\ell}=\tilde{O}(1)
, where we slightly abuse the notation by letting
\epsilon_{\ell}
denote the
\epsilon_{\ell}
of the
root
instance, which satisfies
1/\epsilon_{\ell}=\mathrm{poly}\log n
, the condition required in
Lemma

5.5
.

Therefore, we have

\mathrm{cong}(F^{G})\leq(\omega r)\cdot\kappa(n^{\prime}/\sigma)+n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}}\leq\kappa(n^{\prime})

as required by
Lemma

5.9
(recall that
\kappa(n^{\prime})=(\omega r+1)^{\lceil\log_{\sigma}n^{\prime}\rceil}\cdot n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}}
).

5.4
Backward Mapping Algorithm

We now describe a backward mapping algorithm for
Lemma

5.5

F^{G}\leftarrow\textsc{BackwardMapping}(G,H,F^{H}).

This algorithm is given
G
, the shortcut
H
constructed by the procedure
\textsc{Shortcut}(G,\epsilon_{\ell},\epsilon_{\kappa},h)
, and the edge representation of a
k
-commodity flow
F^{H}
on
G\cup H
. From the edge representation of
F^{H}
, we can obtain the
k
-commodity demand
D
routed by
F^{H}
, and the edge representation of each
q
-th commodity flow
F^{H}_{q}
of
F^{H}
. We intend to output a
k
-commodity flow
F^{G}
in
G
routing
D
, with
\mathrm{cong}(F^{G})\leq n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}}\cdot\mathrm{cong}(F^{H})
and
\mathrm{totlen}(F^{G})\leq\mathrm{totlen}(F^{H})
.

Eliminating
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},j)
.

We will construct an edge representation of a
k
-commodity flow
F^{H^{\prime}}
in
G\cup H
(in fact,
F^{H^{\prime}}
is obtained by
updating

F^{H}
), routing the same
k
-commodity demands as
F^{H}
without any flow passing through the shortcut vertices constructed in Step 5.

For every vertex
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},j)
, suppose there are
\gamma
units of
k
-commodity flows passing through this vertex in the direction of
x_{S_{1}}
to
x_{S_{2}}
(we do the same for the reverse direction
x_{S_{2}}
to
x_{S_{1}}
). We first use
Lemma

5.6
to compute a
0.5
-approximate
((1+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{j}+2h_{\mathrm{diam},i}\cdot s)
-length single-commodity max flow
F_{\mathrm{sg}}
from
x_{S_{1}}
to
x_{S_{2}}
in the graph
G\cup H_{S_{1}}\cup H_{S_{2}}
. Note that
F_{\mathrm{sg}}
is given in its path representation with
\tilde{O}(m\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h))
flow paths. Then we scale
F_{\mathrm{sg}}
to make sure that
\mathrm{value}(F_{\mathrm{sg}})=\gamma
, i.e., the
k
-commodity flow value passing through
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},j)
.

Finally, we use
F_{\mathrm{sg}}
to allocate the flow values of every commodity from
x_{S_{1}}
to
x_{S_{2}}
passing through
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},j)
, as follows. For each commodity
1\leq q\leq k
, suppose
\gamma_{q}
units of flow are passing through
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},j)
(note that
\sum_{1\leq q\leq k}\gamma_{q}=\gamma
). The allocation can be done by a straightforward
sequential
algorithm, which just scans flow paths of
F_{\mathrm{sg}}
one by one to allocate the
\gamma_{q}
units of the
q
-th commodity flow in increasing order of
q
(namely, once
\gamma_{q}
units of the
q
-th commodity are fully allocated, we proceed to
\gamma_{q+1}
). Furthermore, this sequential algorithm can be easily simulated by a parallel algorithm, which takes
\tilde{O}((|\mathrm{path}(F_{\mathrm{sg}})|+k)h)
work and
\tilde{O}(1)
depth. The extra
h
factor in work because we go through each
O(h)
-length flow path in
\mathrm{path}(F_{\mathrm{sg}})
to update
F^{H}
.

Recursive routing.

For every small cluster
S
, we run

F^{G}_{S}\leftarrow\textsc{BackwardMapping}(G[S],H_{S},F^{H}[H_{S}])

where
F^{H}[H_{S}]
is a
k
-commodity flow only preserving the flow of
F^{H}
on edges in
H_{S}
. We replace the flow
F^{H}[H_{S}]
by
F^{G}_{S}
for every small cluster
S
to get
F^{H}_{\mathrm{large}}
. Notice that
F^{H}_{\mathrm{large}}
routes the same demand as
F^{H}
.

Expander routing.

It remains to map the
F^{H}_{\mathrm{large}}
-flows on star edges back to
G
, and we will exploit expander routing. For every large cluster
S
and every commodity of
F^{H}_{\mathrm{large}}
defined as
F^{H}_{\mathrm{large},q}
(for the
q
-th commodity), let
F^{S}_{\mathrm{large},q}
be the restriction of
F^{H}_{\mathrm{large},q}
on the star graph
H_{S}
. Let
D_{\mathrm{sg},S,q}
be a single-commodity demand on vertices
S
, where for each
v\in S
,
D_{\mathrm{sg},S,q}(v)
is the net
F^{S}_{\mathrm{large},q}
-flow value going out
v
. We take a multi-commodity demand
D_{S,q}
corresponding to
D_{\mathrm{sg},S,q}
(recall the definition of
corresponding
in
Section

3.1
), such that
\mathrm{supp}(D_{S,q})=O(|S|)
(note that such
D_{S,q}
always exists). Note that, when we get a (multi-commodity) flow routing
D_{S,q}
later, it is a single-commodity flow routing
D_{\mathrm{sg},S,q}
.

Let
D_{S}
be the union of all
D_{S,q}
for
q\in[k]
. We have
\mathrm{supp}(D_{S})=O(|S|\cdot k)
.
For
i\in[r]
and
{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}
, define

D_{i,{\mathcal{S}},\mathrm{large}}=\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{large},i}}D_{S}

Notice that
D_{i,{\mathcal{S}},\mathrm{large}}
is
h_{\mathrm{diam},i}
-length because each large cluster has a diameter
h_{\mathrm{diam},i}
according to the definition of neighborhood cover. Moreover,
D_{i,{\mathcal{S}},\mathrm{large}}
is
A_{i}
respecting because clusters in
{\mathcal{S}}
are vertex disjoint, so every vertex
v
has at most
1
adjacent edges in
\cup_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}H_{S}
, which is
(v,x_{S,v})
.

F^{H}
has congestion
1
, so that
F^{H}_{\mathrm{large}}
put at most
A_{i}(u)
flow on the vertex
x_{S,u}
, this means
D_{i,{\mathcal{S}},\mathrm{large}}
is
A_{i}
-respecting.

According to
Theorem

4.1
, there is an algorithm that outputs a path representation (which can be easily transformed into an edge representation) of a flow routing
D_{i,{\mathcal{S}},\mathrm{large}}
with the length and congestion specified in
Theorem

4.1
, let the flow be
F_{i,{\mathcal{S}},\mathrm{large}}
. We replace flow
F^{H}_{\mathrm{large}}
on
\cup_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{i,\mathrm{large}}}H_{S}
(i.e. those
F^{S}_{\mathrm{large}}
) by
F_{i,{\mathcal{S}},\mathrm{large}}
for every
{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}
and
i\in[r]
(taking the different commodities into consideration). The resulting flow
F_{G}
is on the graph
G
and will be returned.

Correctness.

Now, we proceed to the correctness. The correctness is based on induction on the size of
G
. The base case is trivial since we do not need to reroute. Now suppose the algorithm is correct for every graph with size less than
G
.

Correctness: congestion.

Without loss of generality, assume
\mathrm{cong}(F^{H})=1
.

Notice that the way we eliminate
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k)
is the same as in
Section

5.3
with at most a constant factor of loss in congestion. Thus, according to the same proof as in
Lemma

5.15
, we have

\mathrm{cong}(F^{H^{\prime}})\leq O(r\cdot(\omega\sigma)^{2}\cdot\bar{k}_{r})

For the expander routing, according to
Theorem

4.1
, the congestion applied to
G
in this step is at most

r\cdot\omega\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}\cdot n^{O(1/\epsilon_{\kappa})}=O(n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})})

Finally, analogous to the proof of
Lemma

5.9
, the whole recursion introduces an overhead of
(\omega r+1)^{\lceil\log_{\sigma}n\rceil}=n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
to the congestion. Therefore, the congestion of
F^{G}
in
G
is

\mathrm{cong}(F^{G})=n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}\cdot O(r\cdot(\omega\sigma)^{2}\cdot\bar{k}_{r})\cdot O(n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})})\leq n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}\leq n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}.

Correctness: total length.

Firstly, we show that
F^{H^{\prime}}
does not increase the total length of
F^{H}
. Notice that the way we eliminate
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k)
is by replacing a flow containing three vertices
(x_{S_{1}},\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k),x_{S_{2}}),x_{S_{2}})
by a flow path with length at most
\ell(\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k))
and the same value
q
. The total length is at most
q\cdot\ell(\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k))
, which is at most the total length of the original flow.

Then, consider the recursive routing step, according to the induction hypothesis, the total length does not increase by rerouting the flow in
H_{S}
for small clusters in
H
to
G
.

Lastly, we show that the expander routing does not increase the total length. Consider an expander routing to the demand
D_{i,{\mathcal{S}},\mathrm{large}}
which corresponds to an original flow of value
q
such that each unit of flow must go through
x_{S,v}\in V(H_{S})
for some large cluster
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i,\mathrm{large}}
. Remember that the length of
x_{S,v}
is
h_{\mathrm{diam},i}\cdot s
, thus, the total length is at least
q\cdot h_{\mathrm{diam},i}\cdot s
.
According to
Theorem

4.1
, the expander routing results in a flow with length bound
h_{\mathrm{diam},i}\cdot s
with the same flow value. Thus, the total length of
F^{G}
is
q\cdot h_{\mathrm{diam},i},\cdot s
. This proves that the total length does not get increased by the expander routing.

Complexity.

Consider the step for eliminating
\mathrm{shortcut}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},k)
. For every pair of large clusters in
{\mathcal{S}}_{i,\mathrm{large}}
and
k\in[\bar{k}_{i}]
for
i\in[r]
, we have one call to
Lemma

5.6
and a parallel subroutine to allocate the
k
-commodity flow values to the single-commodity flow given by
Lemma

5.6
, both of which take
\tilde{O}(\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m)
work and
\tilde{O}(\mathrm{poly}(h))
depth.
Lemma

5.8
shows that the number of large clusters is
n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
, which dominates
\bar{k}_{i}
and
r
. Hence, the number of calls to
Lemma

5.6
is
n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
, which takes totally
n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m
work and
\tilde{O}(\mathrm{poly}(h))
depth.

The step for recursive routing involves recursive calls, which we will analyze later.

The step for expander routing involves one call to
Theorem

4.1
expander routing algorithm per
{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}
and
i\in[r]
. Each of time involves routing a demand with support size
O(nk)
, so the total work is
(kn+m)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}\cdot\omega r=(kn+m)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
and the total depth is
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
.

The recursion, as analyzed in the complexity of
Shortcut
, in total apply a factor of
n^{O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
to the total number of edges and vertices. The parameter
k
does not change in every recursion since the number of flow commodities never increases. Thus, the total work is
(kn+m)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
and total depth is
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
.

5.5
Bootstrapping: Proof of
Theorem

5.2

In
Lemma

5.16
, we first turn the additive length slack in
Lemma

5.5
into a multiplicative length slack.

5.16
.

Let
G
be an undirected graph with vertex lengths and capacities. Let
1/\mathrm{polylog}(n)<\epsilon_{\ell}<1
,
1/\Omega(\log\log\log n)<\epsilon_{\kappa}<1
, and
h\geq 1
be given parameters. There is an algorithm
\textsc{Shortcut}(G,\epsilon_{\ell},\epsilon_{\kappa},h)
computes an
h
-length
t
-step LC-flow shortcut of
G
of size
n^{1+O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
with length slack
(1+2\epsilon_{\ell})
and congestion slack
n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}}\cdot O(\log n)
where

t=\left(1/\epsilon_{\ell}\right)^{O\left(1/\epsilon_{\kappa}^{2}\right)}\cdot\exp(\exp(\log(1/\epsilon_{\kappa})\cdot O(1/\epsilon_{\kappa})))

in
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
depth and
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
work.

Moreover, the shortcut is associated with a backward mapping algorithm with
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
depth and
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot(m+k)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
work.

Proof.

The general idea is that we compute
h^{\prime}
-length flow shortcut with addtive length slack for all
h^{\prime}\leq h
.
Namely, let
h_{j}=2^{j}
for
0\leq j\leq\lceil\log_{2}h\rceil
, and we apply
Lemma

5.5
to compute
H_{j}=\textsc{Shortcut}(G,\epsilon_{\ell},\epsilon_{\kappa},h_{j})
for each
j
.
We denote the resulting flow shortcut as
H=\bigcup_{j}H_{j}
.
Since there are at most
O(\log n)
many shortcuts
H_{j}
, the overall edge size is still
n^{1+O(\epsilon_{\kappa})}
.

Forward Mapping.
For any flow
F^{G}
in
G
with congestion
1
, we decompose it into
F_{j}^{G}
such that
h_{j-1}\leq\mathrm{leng}(F_{j}^{G},G)\leq h_{j}
for each
j
,
and thus
F^{G}=\sum_{j}F_{j}^{G}
.
Since
H_{j}
is an
h_{j}
-length flow shortcut by
Lemma

5.5
, then we have a flow
F^{H_{j}}
in
G\cup H
with
\mathrm{Dem}(F_{j}^{G})=\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H_{j}})
and
F^{H_{j}}
has congestion
1
,
and step
t
.
Note that the congestion from
F^{H_{j}}
remains the same for parts in graph
G
, and congestion over each
H_{j}
are independent from each other.
Furthermore, there is a path-mapping from
F_{j}^{G}
to
F^{H_{j}}
with additive length slack
\delta=\epsilon_{\ell}\cdot h_{j}
.
Namely, for each path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F_{j}^{G})
, the path-mapping maps it to some subflows
F_{P}^{H_{j}}
of
F^{H_{j}}
with the same flow value of
P
and

\mathrm{leng}(F_{P}^{H_{j}},G\cup H_{j})\leq\mathrm{leng}(P,G)+\epsilon_{\ell}\cdot h_{j}\leq\mathrm{leng}(P,G)+2\epsilon_{\ell}\cdot\mathrm{leng}(P,G)\leq(1+2\epsilon_{\ell})\mathrm{leng}(P,G),

which certifies the path-mapping to admit a multiplicative length slack of
(1+2\epsilon_{\ell})
.
By the union of
F^{H_{j}}
for all
j
, we have a flow
F^{H}
routing
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{G})
with the same congestion
1
and step
t
.
What is more, similarly by the union of path-mapping from each
H_{j}
, there is a path-mapping from
F^{G}
to
F^{H}
with length slack
(1+2\epsilon_{\ell})
.

Backward Mapping.

Given the flow
F^{H}
in
G\cup H
with congestion
1
,
for any path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{H})
, let
P^{G}
and
P^{H_{j}}
denote the path restrict in
G
and
H_{j}
respectively.
We further let
F^{H_{j}}
be the flow collecting the flow subpaths restricted in
H_{j}
from each
P
,
and
F_{0}^{G}
be the one collecting parts from
P
in
G
.
Then by the shortcut property of
H_{j}
, we have a flow
F_{j}^{G}
in
G
routing the same demand with congestion
n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}}
.
Again by concatenating flows
F_{j}^{G}
for all
j
, we have a flow
F^{G}
routing the demand
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H})
.
We note that
\lceil\log_{2}h\rceil=O(\log n)
, and since we just take the union of
O(\log n)
many flow shortcuts with the same congestion slack
n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}}
, the overall congestion of
F^{G}
is at most
n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}}\cdot O(\log n)
.
Since each
P^{H_{j}}
is mapped to some subflows in
G
with at most the same length by path-mapping from each
H_{j}
, we can concatenate those subflows together and the overall length is at most
\mathrm{leng}(P,G\cup H)
.

Moreover, the backward mapping algorithm works similarily as the analysis above, which concludes the backward mapping property for
H
.

Running Time.

Each shortcut
H_{j}
can be computed independently, thus there is only an extra
O(\log n)
factor in the total work compared to
Lemma

5.5
, which can be incorporated in the polynomial factor over
\epsilon_{\kappa}
.
The argument also applies to the running time of the backward mapping algorithm.
∎

Now we start the bootstrapping. At the beginning, we receive parameters
1/\mathrm{poly}\log(n)<\epsilon_{\ell}<1
and
1/\Omega(\log\log\log n)<\epsilon_{\kappa},\epsilon_{h}<1
from the input of
Theorem

5.2
. Let
c
be some sufficiently large constant. Let

\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}=\epsilon_{h}\cdot\epsilon_{\ell}/c\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}}=\epsilon_{h}\cdot\epsilon_{\kappa}/c.

Then we refer to

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}

\displaystyle=1+2\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}},

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}

\displaystyle=n^{\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}}}\cdot O(\log n)=n^{O(\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}})},

\displaystyle t_{0}

\displaystyle=\left(1/\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\right)^{O\left(1/\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}}^{2}\right)}\cdot\exp(\exp(\log(1/\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}})\cdot O(1/\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}})))

as the length slack, congestion slack and step bound from
Lemma

5.16
with
\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}
and
\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}}
as input.

Next, we let
h=n^{\epsilon_{h}}
,
d=\lceil\log_{h}N\rceil=O(1/\epsilon_{h})
and

h_{0}=\frac{20(\lambda_{0}+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{d}\cdot t_{0}\cdot h}{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}}=n^{O(\epsilon_{h})}

Construct Shortcut
H
.

We let
{\cal A}_{0}
be the algorithm
\textsc{Shortcut}(G_{0},\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}},\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}},h_{0})
we will bootstrap. Note that given a graph
G_{0}
,
{\cal A}_{0}
will compute an
h_{0}
-length flow shortcut
H_{0}
of
G_{0}
with length slack
\lambda_{0}
, congestion slack
\kappa_{0}
and step
t_{0}
.

Recall that
G
denotes the input graph of
Theorem

5.2
. We let
V(G^{a})=\{v\in V(G)\mid\ell_{G}(v)\leq a\}
and denote by
G^{a}
the graph induced by
V(G^{a})
.
Consider the following bootstrapping procedure. Initially, let
H_{1}
be an
h_{0}
-LC flow shortcut of
G_{1}:=G^{h}
by calling
{\cal A}_{0}
on
G_{1}
, and let
\bar{H}_{1}=G_{1}\cup H_{1}
. For each
2\leq i\leq d
,

•

Let
G_{i}=\bar{H}_{i-1}\cup(G^{h^{i}}\setminus G^{h^{i-1}})
.

•

Let
G^{\prime}_{i}
be
G_{i}
but with different vertex lengths, and
\ell_{i}=\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}\cdot h^{i-1}/(10t_{0})
denote the length regularization factor for
i
, for each vertex
v\in V(G^{\prime}_{i})
,

\ell_{G^{\prime}_{i}}(v)=\lceil\ell_{G_{i}}(v)/\ell_{i}\rceil.

•

Let
H^{\prime}_{i}
be an
h_{0}
-length flow shortcut of
G^{\prime}_{i}
by calling
{\cal A}_{0}
on
G^{\prime}_{i}
.

•

Let
H_{i}
be
H^{\prime}_{i}
but with different vertex lengths: for each vertex
v\in V(H_{i})
,

\ell_{{H}_{i}}(v)=\ell_{H^{\prime}_{i}}(v)\cdot\ell_{i}.

•

Let
\bar{H}_{i}=G_{i}\cup H_{i}
.

We let the final shortcut
H
be

H=\bigcup_{j\leq d}H_{j}.

A key observation is that
G\cup H
is exactly the same as
G_{d}\cup H_{d}
, because we choose
d
such that
h^{d}
is at least the maximum edge length.

Shortcut Size.

When
i=1
we have
|V(G_{1})|\leq n
. For each
i\geq 2
, we have

|V(G_{i})|\leq|V(\bar{H}_{i-1})|+n\leq|V(G_{i-1})|+|V(H_{i-1})|+n\leq|V(G_{i-1})|+(|V(G_{i-1})|)^{1+O(\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}})}+n,

where the last inequality is by
Lemma

5.16
. Therefore, we have

|V(G_{i})|\leq n^{1+O(i\cdot\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}})}\leq n^{1+O(\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}}/\epsilon_{h})}

since
d=O(1/\epsilon_{h})
. Using
Lemma

5.16
again, we have

|H|=\sum_{i}|H_{i}|\leq\sum_{i}|V(G_{i})|^{1+O(\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}})}\leq n^{1+O(\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}}/\epsilon_{h})}/\epsilon_{h}\leq n^{1+\epsilon_{\kappa}}

by our choice of
\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}}
.

Shortcut Construction Work and Depth.

We call the backward mapping algorithm of
{\cal A}_{0}
on the graphs
G^{\prime}_{i}
sequentially. Each
G^{\prime}_{i}
has at most
n^{1+\epsilon_{\kappa}}
vertices and
m+n^{1+\epsilon_{\kappa}}
edges from our argument on shortcut size above. By
Lemma

5.16
, the work is

\mathrm{poly}(h_{0})\cdot(m+n^{1+\epsilon_{\kappa}})\cdot(n^{1+\epsilon_{\kappa}})^{\mathrm{poly}(\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}})}\cdot O(1/\epsilon_{h})=n^{O(\epsilon_{h}+\epsilon_{\kappa})}\cdot m,

and the depth is

\mathrm{poly}(h_{0})\cdot(n^{1+\epsilon_{\kappa}})^{\mathrm{poly}(\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}})}\cdot O(1/\epsilon_{h})=n^{O(\epsilon_{h})}.

Shortcut Quality.

The quality of
H
is given by the lemma below, and we defer the proof to
Section

5.5.1
.

5.17
.

H
is an LC-flow shortcut of
G
with length slack
\lambda=(\lambda_{0}+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{d}+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}/5
, congestion slack
\kappa=(\kappa_{0}+1)^{d}
and step
t=t_{0}
.

Recall that
d=O(1/\epsilon_{h})
,
\lambda_{0}=1+2\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}
,
\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}=\epsilon_{h}\cdot\epsilon_{\ell}/c
and
\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}}=\epsilon_{h}\cdot\epsilon_{\kappa}/c
for some sufficiently large
c
. For
H
, the length slack is
1+\epsilon_{\ell}
, the congestion slack is
n^{\epsilon_{\kappa}}
, and the step is

t=\left(1/(\epsilon_{\ell}\epsilon_{h})\right)^{O\left(1/(\epsilon_{\kappa}\epsilon_{h})^{2}\right)}\cdot\exp(\exp(\log(1/(\epsilon_{\kappa}\epsilon_{h}))\cdot O(1/(\epsilon_{\kappa}\epsilon_{h}))))

Algorithmic Backward Mapping.

Recall that the input of the backward mapping algorithm is the edge representation of a
k
-commodity flow
F^{H}
in
G\cup H
. We want to output the edge representation of a
k
-commodity flow
F^{G}
in
G
routing the same
k
-commodity demand, satisfying that
\mathrm{cong}(F^{G})\leq\kappa\cdot\mathrm{cong}(F^{H})
and
\mathrm{totlen}(F^{G})\leq\mathrm{totlen}(F^{H})
. We note that all the operations between flows (such as addition and subtraction) are under their edge representations (the operations are natural so we will not define them formally).

The algorithm is as follows. Initially
F^{H}
is a flow in
G_{d}\cup H_{d}
. Start from the last
i=d
and let
F^{H_{d}}=F^{H}
.
We iterate
i
from
d
to
1
.

1.

Let
F^{H_{i}}_{\mathrm{true}}
be the restriction of
F^{H_{i}}
on
H_{i}
. Let
F^{G_{i}}_{\mathrm{old}}=F^{H_{i}}-F^{H_{i}}_{\mathrm{true}}
be the restriction of
F^{H_{i}}
on
G_{i}
.

2.

Apply the associated backward mapping algorithm of the shortcut
H_{i}
(precisely, it should be
H^{\prime}_{i}
) with
F^{H_{i}}_{\mathrm{true}}
as the input. The output is the edge representation of a
k
