-commodity flow
F^{G_{i}}_{\mathrm{new}}
in
G^{\prime}_{i}
(so also
G_{i}
) with
\mathrm{totlen}(F^{G_{i}}_{\mathrm{new}},G^{\prime}_{i})\leq\mathrm{totlen}(F^{H_{i}}_{\mathrm{true}},H^{\prime}_{i})
and
\mathrm{cong}(F^{G_{i}}_{\mathrm{new}},G^{\prime}_{i})\leq\kappa_{0}\cdot\mathrm{cong}(F^{H_{i}}_{\mathrm{true}},H^{\prime}_{i})
. Because
\mathrm{totlen}(F^{H_{i}}_{\mathrm{true}},H_{i})=\ell_{i}\cdot\mathrm{totlen}(F^{H_{i}}_{\mathrm{true}},H^{\prime}_{i})
and
\mathrm{totlen}(F^{G_{i}}_{\mathrm{new}},G_{i})\leq\ell_{i}\cdot\mathrm{totlen}(F^{G_{i}}_{\mathrm{new}},G^{\prime}_{i})
, we have

\mathrm{totlen}(F^{G_{i}}_{\mathrm{new}},G_{i})\leq\mathrm{totlen}(F^{H_{i}}_{\mathrm{true}},H_{i}).

Also, we have

\mathrm{cong}(F^{G_{i}}_{\mathrm{new}},G_{i})\leq\kappa_{0}\cdot\mathrm{cong}(F^{H_{i}}_{\mathrm{true}},H_{i})

because the capacity of
G_{i}
and
G^{\prime}_{i}
(also
H_{i}
and
H^{\prime}_{i}
) is the same.

3.

Let
F^{G_{i}}=F^{G_{i}}_{\mathrm{old}}+F^{G_{i}}_{\mathrm{new}}
. Intuitively, we concatenate two flows
F^{G_{i}}_{\mathrm{old}}
and
F^{G_{i}}_{\mathrm{new}}
, and observe that the concatenation indeed forms a flow. Naturally, we have

\mathrm{totlen}(F^{G_{i}},G_{i})\leq\mathrm{totlen}(F^{G_{i}}_{\mathrm{old}},G_{i})+\mathrm{totlen}(F^{H_{i}}_{\mathrm{true}},H_{i})=\mathrm{totlen}(F^{H_{i}},G_{i}\cup H_{i}),\text{ and}

\mathrm{cong}(F^{G_{i}},G_{i})\leq\mathrm{cong}(F^{G_{i}}_{\mathrm{old}},G_{i})+\kappa_{0}\cdot\mathrm{cong}(F^{H_{i}}_{\mathrm{true}},H_{i})\leq(\kappa_{0}+1)\cdot\mathrm{cong}(F^{H_{i}},G_{i}\cup H_{i}),

4.

Let
F^{H_{i-1}}
be the restriction of
F^{G_{i}}
on
\bar{H}_{i-1}=G_{i-1}\cup H_{i-1}
. Let
F^{G,i}_{\mathrm{output}}
be the restriction of
F^{G_{i}}
on
(G^{h^{i}}\setminus G^{h^{i-1}})
. This means

\mathrm{totlen}(F^{H_{i-1}},G_{i-1}\cup H_{i-1})=\mathrm{totlen}(F^{G_{i}},G_{i})-\mathrm{totlen}(F^{G,i}_{\mathrm{output}},G^{h^{i}}\setminus G^{h^{i-1}}),

\mathrm{cong}(F^{H_{i-1}},G_{i-1}\cup H_{i-1})\leq\mathrm{cong}(F^{G_{i}},G_{i})\text{ and }\mathrm{cong}(F^{G,i}_{\mathrm{output}},G^{h^{i}}\setminus G^{h^{i-1}})\leq\mathrm{cong}(F^{G_{i}},G_{i})

Let the final output be
F^{G}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq d}F^{G,i}_{\mathrm{output}}
. From the analysis above, we know

\mathrm{totlen}(F^{G},G)\leq\mathrm{totlen}(F^{H},G\cup H)
and
\mathrm{cong}(F^{G},G)\leq(\kappa_{0}+1)^{d}\cdot\mathrm{cong}(F^{H},G\cup H)
. So our backward mapping algorithm has the desired quality.

Regarding the work and depth, note that we call the backward mapping algorithm of
{\cal A}_{0}
on the graphs
G^{\prime}_{i}
sequentially. Each
G^{\prime}_{i}
has at most
n^{1+\epsilon_{\kappa}}
vertices and
m+n^{1+\epsilon_{\kappa}}
edges from our argument on shortcut size above. By
Lemma

5.16
, the work is

\mathrm{poly}(h_{0})\cdot(m+n^{1+\epsilon_{\kappa}}+(n^{1+\epsilon_{\kappa}})k)\cdot(n^{1+\epsilon_{\kappa}})^{\mathrm{poly}(\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}})}\cdot O(1/\epsilon_{h})=n^{O(\epsilon_{h}+\epsilon_{\kappa})}\cdot(m+nk),

and the depth is

\mathrm{poly}(h_{0})\cdot(n^{1+\epsilon_{\kappa}})^{\mathrm{poly}(\tilde{\epsilon_{\kappa}})}\cdot O(1/\epsilon_{h})=n^{O(\epsilon_{h})}.

5.5.1
Proof of
Lemma

5.17

5.18
Forward Mapping.

The following holds for each
1\leq i\leq d
. Let
F^{G}
be an
h^{i}
-length flow. Then there is a flow
F^{H_{i}}
in
\bar{H}_{i}
routing
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{G})
with
\mathrm{cong}(F^{{H}_{i}})\leq\mathrm{cong}(F^{G})
,
and
\mathrm{step}(F^{{H}_{i}})\leq t_{0}
.
Moreover, there is a path-mapping from
F^{G}
to
F^{H_{i}}
with length slack
(\lambda_{i},\delta_{i})

where
\lambda_{i}=(\lambda_{0}+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{i}
and
\delta_{i}=\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}h^{i-1}/5
.

As mentioned in the construction, we take
H=H_{\leq d}
as the resulting flow shortcut, and for any flow
F^{G}
,
we decompose it as
F^{G}=\bigcup_{i}F^{G_{i}}
such that each path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G_{i}})
has length
h^{i-1}\leq\mathrm{leng}(P,G)\leq h^{i}
.
From
lemma

5.18
, for each
F^{G_{i}}
, there is a flow
F^{H_{i}}
routing the same demand with
\mathrm{cong}(F^{H_{i}})\leq\mathrm{cong}(F^{G_{i}})\leq\mathrm{cong}(F^{G})
and
\mathrm{step}(F^{H_{i}})\leq t_{0}
.
And there exists a path-mapping from
F^{G_{i}}
to
F^{H_{i}}
s.t. for each path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G_{i}})
and the corresponding subflows
F_{P}^{H_{i}}
in
G\cup H
, we have

\mathrm{leng}(F_{P}^{H_{i}},G\cup H)\leq(\lambda_{0}+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{i}\cdot\mathrm{leng}(P,G)+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}h^{i-1}/5.

Note that
\mathrm{leng}(P,G)\geq h^{i-1}
, we further have

\mathrm{leng}(F_{P}^{H_{i}},G\cup H)\leq(\lambda_{0}+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{d}\cdot\mathrm{leng}(P,G)+(\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}/5)\cdot\mathrm{leng}(P,G),

which shows that the path-mapping from each
H_{i}
has a length slack
(\lambda_{0}+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{d}+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}/5
.
Let
F^{H}=\bigcup_{i}F^{H_{i}}
, since each shortcut
H_{i}
is disjoint, and the congestion over vertices in
G
is unchanged, we conclude
\mathrm{cong}(F^{H})\leq\mathrm{cong}(F^{G})
,
\mathrm{step}(F^{H})\leq t_{0}
and the overall path-mapping has length slack
(\lambda_{0}+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{d}+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}/5
.

Forward Mapping: Proof of
Lemma

5.18

For
i=1
, we note that
h_{0}>h
, and thus the statement holds by the way we construct
H_{1}
and
Lemma

5.16
. To prove the statement for a general
i\geq 2
, we will use induction and assume the statement holds for
i-1
.

Decompose Flow Paths of
F^{G}
.

For each flow path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})
, similarly as
lemma

5.11
, we decompose it into flow subpaths
\hat{\cal P}
such that each subpath
\hat{P}\in\hat{\cal P}
either has
\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G)\leq h^{i-1}
(called a
non-trivial subpath
) or contains exactly one vertex
v
with vertex length larger than
h^{i-1}
(called a
trivial subpath
).
We note that a simple greedy argument shows there exists a decomposition
\hat{\cal P}
of size

|\hat{\cal P}|\leq 2\mathrm{leng}(P)/h^{i-1}\leq 2h+1.

Let
F^{G}_{\mathrm{short}}
collect all non-trivial flow subpaths.

Route
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{G}_{\mathrm{short}})
in
\bar{H}_{i-1}
.

By the construction,
F^{G}_{\mathrm{short}}
have length at most
h^{i-1}
and congestion at most
\mathrm{cong}(F^{G})
. By the induction hypothesis, there is a flow
F^{H_{i-1}}_{\mathrm{short}}
in
\bar{H}_{i-1}
routing
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{G}_{\mathrm{short}})
on
\bar{H}_{i-1}
with
\mathrm{cong}(F^{H_{i-1}}_{\mathrm{short}})\leq\mathrm{cong}(F^{G})
and
\mathrm{step}(F^{H_{i-1}}_{\mathrm{short}})\leq t_{0}
.
And there is a path-mapping from
F^{G}_{\mathrm{short}}
to
F^{H_{i-1}}_{\mathrm{short}}
with length slack
(\lambda_{i-1},\delta_{i-1})
.

Route
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{G})
in
G_{i}
and
G^{\prime}_{i}
.

We construct a routing
F^{G_{i}}
of
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{G})
in
G_{i}
by, for each flow path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})
, routing
\mathrm{Dem}(P)
in
G_{i}
via
F^{G_{i}}_{P}
defined as follows (we use
F^{G^{\prime}_{i}}
denote the same routing on
G^{\prime}_{i}
).

•

For each non-trivial subpath
\hat{P}\in{\cal\hat{P}}
, the corresponding flow
F^{H_{i-1}}_{\hat{P}}
in
\bar{H}_{i-1}\subseteq G_{i}
routes
\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{P})
. Note that
\mathrm{step}(F^{H_{i-1}}_{\hat{P}})\leq\mathrm{step}(F^{H_{i-1}}_{\mathrm{short}})\leq t_{0}
, and the length slack of the path-mapping guarantees

\mathrm{leng}(F^{H_{i-1}}_{\hat{P}},H_{i-1})\leq\lambda_{i-1}\cdot\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G)+\delta_{i-1}

•

For each trivial subpath
\hat{P}\in\hat{\cal P}
containing vertex
v
, clearly
h^{i-1}<\ell_{G}(v)\leq h^{i}
, and we route
\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{P})
in
G_{i}
using the same vertex
v
.

Lastly, we get
F^{G_{i}}_{P}
by replacing each non-trivial subpath with
F^{H_{i-1}}_{\hat{P}}
and keeping each trivial subpath unchanged.
Clearly we can bound the the congestion of
F^{G_{i}}
by
\mathrm{cong}(F^{G_{i}})\leq\max\{\mathrm{cong}(F^{H_{i-1}}_{\mathrm{short}}),1\}=\mathrm{cong}(F^{H_{i-1}}_{\mathrm{short}})\leq\mathrm{cong}(F^{G})
.
Note that we can naturally define a path-mapping
\pi_{F^{G}\to F^{G^{\prime}_{i}}}
by mapping each
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})
to
F^{G_{i}}_{P}
.
We let
F^{G^{\prime}_{i}}_{P}
be the same routing as
F^{G_{i}}_{P}
but with the different length function.
By the way we define the vertex lengths
\ell_{G^{\prime}_{i}}(\cdot)
of
G^{\prime}_{i}
, for each flow path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{leng}(F^{G^{\prime}_{i}}_{P},G^{\prime}_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{non-trivial subpath}\\
\hat{P}\in\hat{\cal P}\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{leng}(F^{H_{i-1}}_{\hat{P}},\bar{H}_{i-1})}{\ell_{i}}+\mathrm{step}(F^{H_{i-1}}_{\hat{P}})\right)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{trivial subpath}\\
\hat{P}=\{v\}\in\cal{\hat{P}}\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{\ell_{G}(v)}{\ell_{i}}+1\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{non-trivial subpath}\\
\hat{P}\in\hat{\cal P}\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{\lambda_{i-1}\cdot\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G)+\delta_{i-1}}{\ell_{i}}+t_{0}\right)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{trivial subpath}\\
\hat{P}=\{v\}\in\cal{\hat{P}}\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{\ell_{G}(v)}{\ell_{i}}+1\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda_{i-1}\cdot\mathrm{leng}(P,G)}{\ell_{i}}+\frac{|\hat{\cal P}|\cdot\delta_{i-1}}{\ell_{i}}+|\hat{\cal P}|\cdot t_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda_{i-1}\cdot\mathrm{leng}(P,G)}{l_{i}}+|\hat{\cal P}|\cdot(2/h+1)t_{0}

where the last inequality we use
\delta_{i-1}=\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}h^{i-2}/5
and
\ell_{i}=\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}h^{i-1}/(10t_{0})
.
Plugging in
\mathrm{leng}(P,G)\leq h^{i}
, and
|\hat{\cal P}|\leq 2h
, we further have

\mathrm{leng}(F^{G_{i}}_{P},G^{\prime}_{i})\leq\frac{10\lambda_{i-1}t_{0}}{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}}\cdot h+(2h+1)\cdot(2/h+1)t_{0}\leq\frac{20(\lambda_{0}+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})^{d}t_{0}}{\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}}\cdot h=h_{0}.

That is,
F^{G^{\prime}_{i}}
is an
h_{0}
-length flow on
G^{\prime}_{i}
.

Route
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{G})
in
H^{\prime}_{i}
and
H_{i}
.

By the induction hypothesis and because
F^{G^{\prime}_{i}}
is an
h_{0}
-length flow on
G^{\prime}_{i}
, there is a flow
F^{H^{\prime}_{i}}
in
G^{\prime}_{i}\cup H^{\prime}_{i}
routing
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{G})
(we use
F^{H_{i}}
denote the same routing in
G_{i}\cup H_{i}
) with
\mathrm{cong}(F^{H^{\prime}_{i}})\leq\mathrm{cong}(F^{G^{\prime}_{i}})\leq\mathrm{cong}(F^{G})
and
\mathrm{step}(F^{H^{\prime}_{i}})\leq t_{0}
as desired.
Furthermore, there is a path-mapping
\pi_{F^{G^{\prime}_{i}}\to F^{H^{\prime}_{i}}}
from
F^{G^{\prime}_{i}}
to
F^{H^{\prime}_{i}}
with length slack
\lambda_{0}
.

Namely, for each
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})
, from above we can map it to
F_{P}^{G^{\prime}_{i}}
with path-mapping
\pi_{F^{G}\to F^{G^{\prime}_{i}}}
.
Further by composing it with
\pi_{F^{G^{\prime}_{i}}\to F^{H^{\prime}_{i}}}
, we obtain a path-mapping
\pi_{F^{G}\to F^{H^{\prime}_{i}}}
that maps each
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})
to a flow
F^{H^{\prime}_{i}}_{P}
in
G^{\prime}_{i}\cup H^{\prime}_{i}

such that

\mathrm{leng}(F^{H^{\prime}_{i}}_{P},G^{\prime}_{i}\cup H^{\prime}_{i})\leq\lambda_{0}\cdot\mathrm{leng}(F^{G^{\prime}_{i}}_{P},G^{\prime}_{i}).

Since
F^{H_{i}}
and
F^{H^{\prime}_{i}}
are the same routing again, we use
\pi_{F^{G}\to F^{H_{i}}}=\pi_{F^{G}\to F^{H^{\prime}_{i}}}
denote the same path-mapping from
F^{G}
to
F^{H_{i}}
.
By the way we define the vertex length
\ell_{H_{i}}(\cdot)
of
H_{i}
, for each path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G})
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{leng}(F^{H_{i}}_{P},G_{i}\cup H_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{leng}(F^{H^{\prime}_{i}}_{P},G^{\prime}_{i}\cup H^{\prime}_{i})\cdot\ell_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{0}\cdot\mathrm{leng}(F^{G_{i}}_{P},G^{\prime}_{i})\cdot\ell_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{0}\cdot\left(\frac{\lambda_{i-1}\cdot\mathrm{leng}(P,G)}{\ell_{i}}+|\hat{\cal P}|\cdot(2/h+1)t_{0}\right)\cdot\ell_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{0}\cdot\lambda_{i-1}\mathrm{leng}(P,G)+\lambda_{0}\cdot\left(\frac{2\mathrm{leng}(P,G)}{h^{i-1}}+1\right)\cdot(2/h+1)\cdot t_{0}\ell_{i}

\displaystyle\leq(\lambda_{0}\lambda_{i-1}+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})\mathrm{leng}(P,G)+\lambda_{0}\cdot(2/h+1)\cdot\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}h^{i-1}/10

\displaystyle\leq(\lambda_{0}+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}})\cdot\lambda_{i-1}\mathrm{leng}(P,G)+\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}h^{i-1}/5.

For the fourth inequality, we use the fact that
|\hat{\cal P}|\leq 2\mathrm{leng}(P,G)/h^{i-1}+1
, and we apply
\ell_{i}=\tilde{\epsilon_{\ell}}h^{i-1}/(10t_{0})
in the fifth inequality. Note that we assume
\lambda_{0}
is close to
1
and
h
is large enough such that
\lambda_{0}\cdot(2/h+1)\leq 2
.
This concludes the
lemma

5.18
.

5.19
Backward Mapping.

The following holds for each
1\leq i\leq d
. Let
F^{H_{i}}
be a flow with
V(\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H_{i}}))\subseteq V(G)
and congestion
1
in
G_{i}\cup H_{i}
. Then there is a flow
F^{G}
in
G
routing
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H_{i}})
with
\mathrm{cong}(F^{G})\leq(\kappa_{0}+1)^{i}
.
And here is a path-mapping from
F^{H_{i}}
to
F^{G}
with length slack
1
.

Backward Mapping: Proof of
Lemma

5.19

For
i=1
, let
G_{1}=G^{h}
, and we note that
H_{1}
is the
h_{0}
-length flow shortcut over
G_{1}
,
thus the statement similarly holds from the definition of LC-flow shortcut. For
i\geq 2
, we then assume the statement holds for
i-1
and use induction to prove the lemma.

Given the flow
F^{H_{i}}
with
V(\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H_{i}}))\subseteq V(G)
and congestion
1
in
G_{i}\cup H_{i}
,
we would like to show that the demand
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H_{i}})
can be routed in
G
with congestion
\kappa_{0}^{i}
and at most the same length.

Route
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H_{i}})
in
G^{\prime}_{i}\cup H_{i}^{\prime}
.

By the way we define the vertex length between
G_{i},H_{i}
and
G^{\prime}_{i},H^{\prime}_{i}
respectively,
this naturally gives the same routing
F^{H^{\prime}_{i}}
in
G^{\prime}_{i}\cup H^{\prime}_{i}
with the same congestion
1
but different length.
Further we let the trivial path-mapping between them as
\pi_{F^{H_{i}}\to F^{H^{\prime}_{i}}}
.

Route
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H_{i}})
in
G_{i}^{\prime}
.

For each path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{H^{\prime}_{i}})
, we decompose it into two collections of subpaths
\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{H^{\prime}_{i}}
and
\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{G^{\prime}_{i}}
s.t. subpaths from them are restricted on
H^{\prime}_{i}
and
G^{\prime}_{i}
respectively.
Let
{F}_{\mathrm{sub}}^{H^{\prime}_{i}}
and
F_{\mathrm{sub}}^{G^{\prime}_{i}}
collect all flow paths from
\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{H^{\prime}_{i}}
and
\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{G^{\prime}_{i}}
respectively for each
P
.

Note that the decomposition of each path
P
takes place at the boundary between
{H^{\prime}}_{i}
and
G^{\prime}_{i}
, we can have
V(\mathrm{Dem}({F}_{\mathrm{sub}}^{H^{\prime}_{i}}))\subset V(G^{\prime}_{i})
.
Then since
H_{i}^{\prime}
is an LC-flow shortcut over
G^{\prime}_{i}
, given
{F}_{\mathrm{sub}}^{H^{\prime}_{i}}
,
there exists a flow
\hat{F}^{G^{\prime}_{i}}
routing
\mathrm{Dem}(\mathrm{Dem}({F}_{\mathrm{sub}}^{H^{\prime}_{i}}))

with congestion
\kappa_{0}
.

We construct
F^{G^{\prime}_{i}}
by concatenating
\hat{F}^{G^{\prime}_{i}}
and
F_{\mathrm{sub}}^{G^{\prime}_{i}}
,
and we have
\mathrm{cong}(F^{G^{\prime}_{i}})\leq\mathrm{cong}(\hat{F}^{G^{\prime}_{i}})+\mathrm{cong}(F_{\mathrm{sub}}^{G^{\prime}_{i}})\leq\kappa_{0}+1
.
Further, from the backward path-mapping from
H^{\prime}_{i}
,
for each path
P\in F^{H^{\prime}_{i}}
, each subpath
\hat{P}
restricted on
H^{\prime}_{i}
is mapped to subflows
F_{\hat{P}}^{G^{\prime}_{i}}
with the length at most
\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G^{\prime}_{i}\cup H^{\prime}_{i})
.
In general, by replacing each subpath
\hat{P}
with
F_{\hat{P}}^{G^{\prime}_{i}}
, we can map each path
P
to some subflows
F_{P}^{G^{\prime}_{i}}
, which gives a path-mapping
\pi_{F^{H^{\prime}_{i}}\to F^{G^{\prime}_{i}}}
from
F^{H^{\prime}_{i}}
to
F^{G^{\prime}_{i}}
.

Route
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H_{i}})
in
G_{i}
.

Considering
G_{i}
is the same graph as
G^{\prime}_{i}

where the length of each vertex is increased by a multiplicative factor of at most
\ell_{i}
.
then we also have a flow
F^{G_{i}}
directly from
F^{G^{\prime}_{i}}
with the same congestion.
Let the trivial path-mapping between
G^{\prime}_{i}
and
G_{i}
as
\pi_{F^{G^{\prime}_{i}}\to F^{G_{i}}}
, by composing it with all path-mappings above, we have a path-mapping
\pi_{F^{H_{i}}\to F^{G_{i}}}
from
F^{H_{i}}
to
F^{G_{i}}
.

For each path
P\in F^{H_{i}}
(then also in
F^{H^{\prime}_{i}}
), we can map it to subflows
F_{P}^{G_{i}}
.
Let
\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{H_{i}}
and
\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{G_{i}}
defined similarly as above,
we note that

\sum_{\hat{P}\in\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{H}_{i}}\sqcup\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{G_{i}}}\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G_{i}\cup H_{i})=\mathrm{leng}(P,G_{i}\cup H_{i}).

Given any subpath
\hat{P}\in\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{H}_{i}}
, it is also in the
\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{H}^{\prime}_{i}}
.
As discussed in the previous step, it can be mapped to some subflows
F_{\hat{P}}^{G^{\prime}_{i}}
(then also in
F^{G_{i}}
) with length at most
\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G^{\prime}_{i}\cup H^{\prime}_{i})
.
We have

\mathrm{leng}(F_{\hat{P}}^{G_{i}},G_{i})\leq\mathrm{leng}(F_{\hat{P}}^{G^{\prime}_{i}},G^{\prime}_{i})\cdot\ell_{i}\leq\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G^{\prime}_{i}\cup H^{\prime}_{i})\cdot\ell_{i}\leq\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G_{i}\cup H_{i})

where the last inequality use the fact that
\hat{P}
is restricted in
H^{\prime}_{i}
.
Further, any subpath
\hat{P}
restricted in
G_{i}
remains the same under the path-mapping, and thus contribute the same length in
\mathrm{leng}(F_{P}^{G_{i}})
.
We conclude that
\mathrm{leng}(F_{P}^{G_{i}})\leq\mathrm{leng}(P,G_{i}\cup H_{i})
, which shows the path-mapping
\pi_{F^{H_{i}}\to F^{G_{i}}}
has length slack
1
.

Decompose Flow Paths of
F^{G_{i}}
.

Note that
G_{i}=\bar{H}_{i-1}\cup(G^{h^{i}}\setminus G^{h^{i-1}})
where
\bar{H}_{i-1}=G_{i-1}\cup H_{i-1}
.
We apply the same technique to each flow path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G_{i}})
.
Namely, we decompose it into two collections of subpaths
\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\bar{H}_{i-1}}
and
\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{G^{\prime}}
s.t. subpaths from them are restricted on the
\bar{H}_{i-1}
and
(G^{h^{i}}\setminus G^{h^{i-1}})
respectively.

Let
F^{H_{i-1}}
collect all flow paths from
\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\bar{H}_{i-1}}
for each
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G_{i}})
,
we can similarly have
V(\mathrm{Dem}(F^{{H}_{i-1}}))\subset V(G)
, and then by induction hypothesis there is a flow
F^{G^{\prime\prime}}
in
G
routing the same demand with
\mathrm{cong}(F^{G^{\prime\prime}})\leq(\kappa_{0}+1)^{i-1}\cdot\mathrm{cong}(F^{\bar{H}_{i-1}})\leq(\kappa_{0}+1)^{i-1}\cdot\mathrm{cong}(F^{G_{i}})\leq(\kappa_{0}+1)^{i}
.
We further note that the congestion from it only involves vertex with length samller than
h^{i-1}
.

We then similarily define
F^{G^{\prime}}
to collect all flow paths from
\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{G^{\prime}}
for each
P
, and by concatenating it with
F^{G^{\prime\prime}}
, we have a flow
F^{G}
routing the demand
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{G_{i}})=\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H_{i}})
with congestion
\max\{\mathrm{cong}(F^{G^{\prime\prime}}),\mathrm{cong}(F^{G^{\prime}})\}\leq(\kappa_{0}+1)^{i}
.

Considering the path-mapping
\pi_{F^{H_{i-1}}\to F^{G^{\prime\prime}}}
from induction hypothesis, for each path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F^{G_{i}})
,
each subpath
\hat{P}\in\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{\bar{H}_{i-1}}
corresponds to subflows
F_{\hat{P}}^{G^{\prime\prime}}
with
\mathrm{leng}(F_{\hat{P}}^{G^{\prime\prime}},G)\leq\mathrm{leng}(\hat{P},G_{i})
.
By concatenating all subpaths
\hat{P}\in\hat{{\mathcal{P}}}_{G^{\prime}}
, each path
P
corresponds to subflows
F_{P}^{G}
in
G
with length
\mathrm{leng}(F_{P}^{G})\leq\mathrm{leng}(P,G_{i})
.
This gives a path-mapping
\pi_{F^{G_{i}}\to F^{G}}
from
F^{G_{i}}
to
F^{G}
with length slack
1
.

Route
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{H_{i}})
in
G
.

We conclude by combining previous steps.
Given a feasible flow
F^{H_{i}}
in
G_{i}\cup H_{i}
, there exists flow
F^{G}
in
G
routing the same demand with congestion
(\kappa_{0}+1)^{i}
.
Further by composing
\pi_{F^{H_{i}}\to F^{G_{i}}}
and
\pi_{F^{G_{i}}\to F^{G}}
, we have a path-mapping from
F^{H_{i}}
to
F^{G}
with length slack
1
.
This concludes the
lemma

5.19
.

6
Length-Constrained Multi-Commodity Maxflows in Low Depths

In this section we focus on directed graphs with edge lengths and capacities. Note that this setting is stronger than undirected graphs with vertex lengths and capacities because of the standard vertex-splitting reduction.

In this section, the notations of flows are slightly different from
Section

3.1
, because we want the notations to be consistent with a previous work
[
HHS23
]
. Precisely, we will talk about multi-commodity flows in a directed graph with edge lengths
\ell
, capacities
U
, and source-sink pairs
\{(S_{i},T_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq k,\ S_{i},T_{i}\subseteq V(G)\text{ are disjoint}\}
. Consider a length parameter
h\geq 1
. Let
{\cal P}_{h}(S_{i},T_{i})
collect all simple
S_{i}
-
T_{i}
paths with length at most
h
. Then an
h
-length multi-commodity flow

F=\{F_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
and
moving cuts

w
are formalized by the following LP and its dual.

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~\max\

\displaystyle\mathrm{value}(F)=\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}\sum_{P\in{\cal P}_{h}(S_{i},T_{i})}F_{i}(P)~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\min\ |w|=\sum_{e\in E(G)}U(e)w(e)

s.t.

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{P\ni e}F_{i}(P)\leq U(e),\forall e\in E(G)~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\text{s.t. }\sum_{e\in P}w(e)\geq 1,\forall P\in\bigcup_{i}{\cal P}_{i}(S_{i},T_{i})

\displaystyle F_{i}(P)\geq 0,\forall i\in[k],P\in{\cal P}_{h}(S_{i},T_{i})~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~w(e)\geq 0,\forall e\in E(G)

An
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate
h
-length multi-commodity flow and moving cut pair
(F,w)
is such that
|w|\leq(1+\epsilon)\mathrm{value}(F)
.

In fact, in the context of
Section

3.1
, an (
h
-length) multi-commodity flow
F
defined as above is equivalent to an (
h
-length)
k
-commodity flow
F

partially
routing the
k
-commodity demand
D
defined as follows. For each commodity
1\leq i\leq k
, the
i
-th commodity demand
D_{i}
of
D
is, for each
v\in V(G)
, if
v\in S_{i}
,
D_{i}(v)=\infty
; if
v\in T_{i}
,
D_{i}(v)=-\infty
; otherwise,
D_{i}=0
. Intuitively, the multi-commodity flows in this section are non-concurrent.

The following is the main result of this section.

6.1
.

Let
G=(V(G),E(G))
be a directed graph with positive integral edge lengths
\ell
, integral capacities
U
and source-sink pairs
\{(S_{i},T_{i})\subseteq V(G)\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
. Given length parameter
h\geq 1
and approximation parameter
0<\epsilon<1
, there is a parallel algorithm that computes a feasible multi-commodity
h
-length flow, moving cut pair
(F,w)
that is
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate. Furthermore,
F
is
1/O(kh^{2}\log n/\epsilon^{3})
-fractional and it is outputted in its path representation, where the number of flow paths for each commodity is
O(mh^{5}\log n/\epsilon^{4})
.
The work and depth are
\tilde{O}(mkh^{8}/\epsilon^{4})
and
\tilde{O}(h^{5}/\epsilon^{3})
respectively.

We point out that,
[
HHS23
]
showed a single-commodity version of
Theorem

6.1
. They also generalized their single-commodity result to the multi-commodity setting, but with work
\tilde{O}(mk)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h,\epsilon^{-1})
and depth
\tilde{O}(k)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h,\epsilon^{-1})
. Our multi-commodity result is stronger in the sense that we get depth
\tilde{O}(\mathrm{poly}(h,\epsilon^{-1}))
independent on
k
. To the best of our knowledge, we are the first to achieve this result and it may be of independent interest.

However, the flows they output have better integrality, i.e. their solution is
1/\tilde{O}(\epsilon^{-2})
-fractional, while ours is
1/\tilde{O}(k\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h,\epsilon^{-1}))
-fractional. But we note that we will not exploit the fractionality guarantee in
Theorem

6.1
in the later sections.

Our approach is a while-box generalization of
[
HHS23
]
, so the techniques have a large overlap with theirs.

6.1
Section Preliminaries

6.2
Sparse Decomposition of Acyclic Flows, Theorem 10.1 in
[
HHS23
]
.

Let
G
be an
h
-layer DAG with source and sink vertices
S,T\subseteq V(G)
. Given a
(1/\mu)
-fractional edge flow function
f
, there is an algorithm that computes the path representation of a
(1/\mu)
-fractional single-commodity flow
F
with
|\mathrm{path}(F)|\leq m
whose edge representation is
f
. The work is
\tilde{O}(mh)
and depth is
\tilde{O}(h)
.

6.3
Parallel Flow Rounding
[
Coh95
]
.

Let
G
be a directed graph with source and sink vertices
S,T\subseteq V(G)
. Given the edge representation of a fractional single-commodity flow
F
in
G
, there is an algorithm that computes the edge representation of an integral single-commodity flow
\hat{F}
in
G
satisfying that

•

\lceil\mathrm{value}(F)-1/\mathrm{poly}(n)\rceil\leq\mathrm{value}(\hat{F})\leq\lceil\mathrm{value}(F)\rceil
,

•

for each edge
e\in E(G)
,
\lfloor F(e)\rfloor\leq\hat{F}(e)\leq\lceil F(e)\rceil
.

The work is
\tilde{O}(m)
and the depth is
\tilde{O}(1)
.

6.4
.

Let
G
be a directed graph with source and sink vertices
S,T\subseteq V(G)
. Given a rounding parameter
\mu\leq\mathrm{poly}(n)
which is a power of
2
, and the edge representation of a fractional single-commodity flow
F
in
G
, there is an algorithm that computes the edge representation of a
(1/\mu)
-fractional single-commodity flow
\hat{F}
in
G
satisfying that

•

\lceil\mu\cdot\mathrm{value}(F)-1/\mathrm{poly}(n)\rceil\leq\mu\cdot\mathrm{value}(\hat{F})\leq\lceil\mu\cdot\mathrm{value}(F)\rceil
,

•

for each edge
e\in E(G)
,
\lfloor\mu\cdot F(e)\rfloor\leq\mu\cdot\hat{F}(e)\leq\lceil\mu\cdot F(e)\rceil
.

The work is
\tilde{O}(m)
and the depth is
\tilde{O}(1)
.

Proof.

Round
\mu\cdot F
to an integral
\hat{F}^{\prime}
using
Lemma

6.3
. Return
\hat{F}=\hat{F}^{\prime}/\mu
.
∎

6.2
Multi-Commodity
h
-Length
(1+\epsilon)
-Lightest Path Blockers

6.5
.

Let
G=(V(G),E(G))
be a graph with lengths
\ell
, capacities
U
, weights
w
and source-sink pairs
\{(S_{i},T_{i})\subseteq V(G)\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
. Fix parameters
\epsilon>0
,
h\geq 1
,
\lambda\leq\min_{1\leq i\leq k}d^{(h)}_{w}(S_{i},T_{i})
where
d^{(h)}_{w}(S_{i},T_{i}):=\min_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}_{h}(S_{i},T_{i})}w(P)
,
\mu\geq 1
and
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
, an
h
-length
(1/\mu)
-fractional multi-commodity flow
F
is an
(1/\mu)
-fractional
h
-length
(1+\epsilon)
-lightest path
\alpha
-blocker if

•

Each flow path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F)
has
w(P)\leq(1+2\epsilon)\lambda
, and

•

For each
P\in\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq k}{\cal P}_{h}(S_{i},T_{i})
such that
w(P)\leq(1+\epsilon)\lambda
, there is some
e\in E(P)
such that
F(e)\geq\alpha\cdot U(e)
.

6.6
.

Let
G=(V(G),E(G))
be a directed graph with positive integral lengths
\ell
, integral capacities
U
, (possibly fractional) weights
w
and source-sink pairs
\{(S_{i},T_{i})\subseteq V(G)\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
. Given parameters
h\geq 1
,
0<\epsilon<1
, and
\lambda\leq\min_{1\leq i\leq k}d^{(h)}_{w}(S_{i},T_{i})
, there is an algorithm that computes the path representation of a
(1/\mu)
-fractional
h
-length
(1+\epsilon)
-lightest path
\alpha
-blocker
F
, where
\alpha=\Omega(\epsilon/h^{2})
and
\mu=O(kh^{2}/\epsilon)
. Furthermore, the number of flow paths in the path representation is
|\mathrm{path}(F)|\leq O(mh^{2}/\epsilon)
. The work and depth are
\tilde{O}(mkh^{5}/\epsilon)
and
\tilde{O}(h^{2})
.

6.2.1
Length-Weight Expanded DAG

Let
G
be a directed graph with positive integral edge lengths
\ell
, capacities
U
, weights
w
and source-sink pairs
\{(S_{i},T_{i})\subseteq V(G)\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
, where edge lengths are positive integers. Given parameters
h\geq 1
,
0<\epsilon<1
and
\lambda\leq\min_{1\leq i\leq k}d^{(h)}_{w}(S_{i},T_{i})
, its
length-weight expanded DAG

D^{(h,\lambda)}
is an
(h+1)
-layer directed graph with edge capacities
U^{\prime}
and source-sink pairs
\{(S^{\prime}_{i},T^{\prime}_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
.

Before defining
D^{(h,\lambda)}
, we first define another edge weight
\tilde{w}
which rounds up
w
to the nearest multiple of
(\epsilon/h)\cdot\lambda
, i.e. for each
e\in E(G)
,

\tilde{w}(e)=(\epsilon/h)\cdot\lambda\cdot\left\lceil\frac{w(e)}{(\epsilon/h)\cdot\lambda}\right\rceil.

Intuitively,
\tilde{w}
discretizes
w
.
Now we define
D^{(h,\lambda)}
as follows.

•

The vertex set
V(D^{(h,\lambda)})
is constructed by, for each
v\in V(G)
, making copies
v(x,h^{\prime})
where
0\leq h^{\prime}\leq h
and
x
ranges over multiples of
\frac{\epsilon}{h}\cdot\lambda
between
0
and
(1+2\epsilon)\cdot\lambda
. Therefore, each original vertex
v\in V(G)
has
\kappa=(h+1)\cdot(\lfloor\frac{(1+2\epsilon)\cdot\lambda}{(\epsilon/h)\cdot\lambda}\rfloor+1)=O(h^{2}/\epsilon)
copies.

•

For each source-sink pair
(S_{i},T_{i})
of
G
. The corresponding source-sink pair
(S^{\prime}_{i},T^{\prime}_{i})
of
D^{(h,\lambda)}
is defined by
S^{\prime}_{i}=\{v(0,0)\mid v\in S_{i}\}
and
T^{\prime}_{i}=\{\text{all copies of $v$ in $V(D^{(h,\lambda)})$}\mid v\in T_{i}\}
.

•

Consider the edge set
E(D^{(h,\lambda)})
. For each original edge
e=(u,v)\in E(G)
, we add an edge from
u(x,h^{\prime})
to
v(x+\tilde{w}(e),h^{\prime}+\ell(e))
(called a copy of
e
) for each
x,h^{\prime}
such that
x+\tilde{w}(e)\leq(1+2\epsilon)\cdot\lambda
and
h^{\prime}+\ell(e)\leq h
. We let
E^{\prime}(e)
denote the set of all copies of
e
and note that
|E^{\prime}(e)|\leq\kappa
.

•

For each edge
e^{\prime}\in E(D^{(h,\lambda)})
which is a copy of an original edge
e\in E(G)
, the capacity of
e^{\prime}
is
U^{\prime}(e^{\prime})=U(e)
.

Next, we discuss how to project paths between
G
and
D^{(h,\lambda)}
. We first consider the forward projection.

Forward Projection.
For each
1\leq i\leq k
and original path
P\in{\cal P}_{h}(S_{i},T_{i})
with
w(P)\leq\lambda\cdot(1+\epsilon)
, we define its projection
P^{\prime}
on
D^{(h,\lambda)}
as follows. Say
P
visits original vertices
v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{k}
where
v_{1}\in S_{i}
and
v_{k}\in T_{i}
. Then
P^{\prime}
starts at the copy
v_{1}(0,0)
of
v_{1}
, walks along copies of edges in
P
, and ends at some copy of
v_{k}
. Note that
P^{\prime}
must exist because (1)
x
can be (almost) as large as
(1+2\epsilon)\lambda
when creating vertex copies, and (2)
\tilde{w}(e)
overestimates
w(e)
by at most
(\epsilon/h)\cdot\lambda
when creating edge copies (it means
\tilde{w}(P)\leq w(P)+\epsilon\lambda\leq(1+2\epsilon)\lambda
because
P
is
h
-length).

Backward Projection.
For each
1\leq i\leq k
and an
S^{\prime}_{i}
-
T^{\prime}_{i}
path
P^{\prime}
in
D^{(h,\lambda)}
, we can project it back to an original path
P
in
G
by simply taking the original edge of each edge in
P^{\prime}
. Observe that
w(P)\leq(1+2\epsilon)\lambda
because
x
is at most
(1+2\epsilon)\lambda
when creating vertex copies.

6.2.2
Multi-Commodity Blocking Flows in
h
-Layer DAGs

6.7
.

Let
G
be an
h
-layer DAG with integral capacities
U
and source-sink pairs
\{(S_{i},T_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
.
Given a constant
0<\alpha<1
, there is an algorithm that computes the edge representation of a multi-commodity
(1/O(k))
-fractional
\alpha
-blocking flow
F
. The work is
\tilde{O}(mkh^{3})
and the depth is
\tilde{O}(h^{2})
.

Proof.

Path Counts and the Path Count Flow.

We first introduce the notion of
path counts
and the
path count flow
with respect to an arbitrary
(1/\mathrm{poly}(n))
-fractional capacity function
U^{\prime}
of
G
. We point out that it is possible that
U^{\prime}(e)=0
for some edges
e\in E(G)
, and we will ignore such edges in the following definition.

For any simple path
P
in
G
, we let
c(P)=\prod_{e\in P}U^{\prime}(e)
.
For each
1\leq i\leq k
and edge
e\in E(G)
, we define its
commodity-
i
path count
to be

c(e,i)=\sum_{P\in{\cal P}(S_{i},T_{i})\text{ s.t. }e\in P}c(P).

Equivalently, we can interpret
c(e,i)
as follows. For each vertex
v\in V(G)
, let

c^{-}(v,i)=\sum_{P\in{\cal P}(S_{i},v)}c(P)
and
C^{+}(v,i)=\sum_{P\in{\cal P}(v,T_{i})}c(P)
. Then for each edge
e=(u,v)\in E(G)
, we have

c(e,i)=c^{-}(u,i)\cdot U^{\prime}(e)\cdot c^{+}(v,i).

For each
e\in E(G)
, we let
c(e)=\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}c(e,i)
be its total path counts of all commodities. Let
c_{\max}=\max_{e\in E(G)}c(e)
be the maximum path counts.

The
commodity-
i
path count flow
w.r.t.
U^{\prime}
, denoted by
\widetilde{F}_{i}
is defined by the following edge representation: for each
e\in E(G)
,

\widetilde{F}_{i}(e)=U^{\prime}(e)\cdot c(e,i)/c_{\max}.

The
multi-commodity path count flow
is then
\widetilde{F}=\{\widetilde{F}_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
. Note that
\widetilde{F}
is a feasible multi-commodity flow w.r.t.
U^{\prime}
in
G
since the the flow conservation is guaranteed, and it can be computed efficiently as shown in
Lemma

6.8
.

6.8
.

Given a
(1/\mathrm{poly}(n))
-fractional capacity function
U^{\prime}
of
G
, there is an algorithm that computes the edge representation of
\widetilde{F}_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
in
\tilde{O}(mkh^{2})
work and
\tilde{O}(h)
depth.

Proof.

As we discussed above, computing the edge representation of
\widetilde{F}_{i}
can be reduced to computing
c^{+}(v,i)
and
c^{-}(v,i)
for all
1\leq i\leq k
and vertices
v\in V(G)
. It is easy to see that, for a fixed
1\leq i\leq k
, a dynamic-programming-type algorithm can compute
c^{-}(v,i)
and
c^{+}(v,i)
for all vertices
v
in
\tilde{O}(mh^{2})
work and
\tilde{O}(h)
depth. Run the dynamic programming for each
1\leq i\leq k
in parallel gives the lemma.

In fact, the dynamic programming takes
O(m)
work and
O(h)
depth assuming multiplying two large numbers only takes constant work. Without this assumption, note that each of these
c^{-}(v,i),c^{+}(v,i)
and
c(e,i)
can be represented precisely using
\tilde{O}(h)
bits, because the number of simple paths is
n^{O(h)}
and each
c(P)
is
1/n^{O(h)}
-fractional and at most
n^{O(h)}
. Multiplying two
\tilde{O}(h)
-bit numbers can be done trivially in
\tilde{O}(h^{2})
work and
\tilde{O}(1)
depth, which becomes the overheads to the final work and depth.
∎

The Algorithm.
Now we are ready to computes the multi-commodity
(1/O(k))
-fractional
\alpha
-blocking flow
F
with respect to the graph
G
and capacities
U
. On a high level, the algorithm iteratively computes a path count flow, rounds it to be
(1/O(k))
-fractional, and subtracts it from the capacity.

Formally speaking, we start with the initial capacities
U^{(0)}=U
. At each iteration
j\geq 0
, the algorithm do the following.

1.

If the maximum path counts
c^{(j)}_{\max}
w.r.t.
U^{(j)}
is
0
, terminate the algorithm. Otherwise, compute the multi-commodity path count flow
\widetilde{F}^{(j)}=\{\widetilde{F}^{(j)}_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
w.r.t.
U^{(j)}
using
Lemma

6.8
.

2.

For all
1\leq i\leq k
in parallel, Round
\widetilde{F}^{(j)}_{i}
to a
(1/\mu)
-fractional flow
\hat{F}^{(j)}_{i}
using
Corollary

6.4
, where
\mu
is the smallest power of
2
such that
\mu\geq 4k/(1-\alpha)
. It guarantees that for each
e\in E(G)
,
\lfloor\mu\cdot\widetilde{F}^{(j)}_{i}(e)\rfloor\leq\mu\cdot\hat{F}^{(j)}_{i}(e)\leq\lceil\mu\cdot\widetilde{F}^{j}_{i}(e)\rceil
, which implies
\widetilde{F}^{(j)}_{i}(e)-1/\mu\leq\hat{F}^{(j)}_{i}(e)\leq\widetilde{F}^{j}_{i}(e)+1/\mu
.

3.

Let
F^{(j)}=\{\hat{F}^{(j)}_{i}/2\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
. For each edge
e\in E(G)
, update its capacity to

U^{(j+1)}(e)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &0,~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\text{ if }U^{(j)}(e)-F^{(j)}(e)\leq 1-\alpha\\
&U^{(j)}(e)-F^{(j)}(e),~\text{otherwise}\\
\end{aligned}\right.

At the end, let
F=\sum_{j\geq 0}F^{(j)}
be the output.

Correctness.
We now argue that
F
is indeed a multi-commodity
(1/O(k))
-fractional
\alpha
-blocking flow.

First, we show that
F
is a feasible flow in
G
w.r.t.
U
. It suffices to show that for each
j
,
F^{(j)}
is feasible w.r.t.
U^{(j)}
. To see this, for each edge
e\in E(G)
, we have
\widetilde{F}^{(j)}(e)\leq U^{(j)}(e)
since we have shown that
\widetilde{F}^{(j)}
is feasible w.r.t.
U^{(j)}(e)
. Then, we have

F^{(j)}(e)=\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}\hat{F}^{(j)}_{i}(e)/2\leq(\widetilde{F}^{(j)}(e)+k/\mu)/2\leq(U^{(j)}(e)+1-\alpha)/2\leq U^{(j)}(e),

where the last inequality is because each non-zero
U^{(j)}(e)
is at least
1-\alpha
.

Second,
F
is
\alpha
-blocking by the following reason. Let
\bar{j}
be the last iteration such that
c^{(\bar{j})}_{\max}=0
. Consider an arbitrary path
P\in\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq k}{\cal P}(S_{i},T_{i})
. Note that
\prod_{e\in P}U^{(j)}(e)=0
because
c^{(\bar{j})}_{\max}=0
. It means there is an edge
e\in P
such that
U^{(\bar{j})}(e)=0
. Then, by the update rule of capacities, we have
U(e)-\sum_{j<\bar{j}}F^{(j)}(e)\leq 1-\alpha
, which implies
F(e)\geq U(e)-(1-\alpha)\geq\alpha\cdot U(e)
(since
U(e)\geq 1
). Therefore,
P
is
\alpha
-blocked by
F
.

Third,
F
is
1/(O(k))
-fractional because each
\hat{F}^{j}_{i}
is
(1/\mu)
-fractional, each
F^{(j)}
is
(1/(2\mu))
-fractional, and
\mu=4k/(1-\alpha)=O(k)
.

Work and Depth.
It remains to argue that the algorithm above computes
F
in required work and depth. It suffices to show that the number of iterations is at most
\tilde{O}(h)
.

The key observation is that, in each iteration
j
, we have
F^{(j)}(e)\geq U^{(j)}(e)/8
for each edge
e\in E(G)
s.t.
c^{(j)}(e)\geq c^{(j)}_{\max}/2
. To see this, for each such edge
e
, we have
\widetilde{F}^{(j)}(e)\geq U^{(j)}(e)/2
by the definition. Thus

\hat{F}^{(j)}(e)=\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}\hat{F}^{j}_{i}(e)\geq\widetilde{F}^{(j)}(e)-k/\mu\geq\widetilde{F}^{(j)}(e)-(1-\alpha)/4\geq U^{(j)}(e)/4,

where the last inequality is because
U^{(j)}(e)\geq 1-\alpha
. Finally,
F^{(j)}(e)=\hat{F}^{(j)}(e)/2\geq U^{(j)}(e)/8
.

Providing this observation, we show that
c_{\max}
will be halved every
O(\log U_{\max})
iterations. Formally speaking, for each
j_{1}
such that
c_{\max}^{(j_{1})}>0
, we have
c^{(j_{2})}_{\max}\leq c^{(j_{1})}_{\max}/2
where
j_{2}=j_{1}+\Theta(\log U_{\max})
. Assume the opposite for contradiction. Then some edge
e
has
c^{(j)}(e)\geq c_{\max}^{(j_{1})}/2
during for each
j_{1}\leq j\leq j_{2}
. By the observation, every
j_{1}\leq j\leq j_{2}-1
has
U^{(j+1)}(e)\leq 7U^{(j)}(e)/8
, meaning that
U^{(j_{2})}(e)<1-\alpha
. Hence
U^{(j_{2})}(e)=0
and
c^{(j_{2})}(e)=0
, contradicting that
c^{(j_{2})}(e)\geq c^{(j_{1})}_{\max}/2
.

Therefore, after
O(h\log(n\cdot U_{\max}))\cdot O(\log U_{\max})=\tilde{O}(h)
iterations,
c_{\max}
will drop below
(1-\alpha)^{h}
(note that initially
c^{(0)}_{\max}\leq n^{O(h)}\cdot(U_{\max})^{h}
). When
c_{\max}<(1-\alpha)^{h}
, we have
c_{\max}=0
because the edge capacity is either at least
1-\alpha
or
0
.

∎

6.2.3
Computing Path Blockers: Proof of
Theorem

6.6

The Algorithm.
The algorithm is as follows.

1.

Construct the length-weight expanded DAG
D^{(h,\lambda)}
.

2.

Compute the path representation of a multi-commodity
(1/O(k))
-fractional
0.5
-blocking flow
F^{\prime}
in
D^{(h,\lambda)}
by applying
Lemma

6.7
and
Lemma

6.2
.

3.

Initialize
F
to be a zero flow. For each flow path
P^{\prime}\in\mathrm{path}(F^{\prime})
, project it to an original path
P
in
G
(according to the backward projection in
Section

6.2.1
), and add
P
to the flow
F
(i.e. update
F(P)\leftarrow F(P)+F^{\prime}(P^{\prime})/\kappa
), where
\kappa=O(h^{2}/\epsilon)
is from the definition of
D^{(h,\lambda)}
.

4.

Return
F
.

Correctness.
Now we show that
F
is a
(1/\mu)
-fractional
h
-length
(1+\epsilon)
-lightest path
\alpha
-blocker for
\alpha=\Omega(\epsilon/h^{2})
and
\mu=O(kh^{2}/\epsilon)
. First
F
is
(1/\mu)
-fractional because
F^{\prime}
is
1/O(k)
-fractional and
\kappa=O(h^{2}/\epsilon)
.
Next, each path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F)
has
w(P)\leq(1+2\epsilon)\lambda
as we discussed when introducing the backward path projection. It is also clear that the number of flow paths of each commodity in the path representation of
F
is
O(mh^{2}/\epsilon)
, because of
Lemma

6.2
and because
D^{(h,\lambda)}
has
O(mh^{2}/\epsilon)
edges.

It remains to show that, for each
P\in\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq k}{\cal P}_{h}(S_{i},T_{i})
such that
w(P)\leq(1+\epsilon)\lambda
, there is an edge
e\in P
such that
F(e)\geq\alpha\cdot U(e)
. Recall that the forward projection in
Section

6.2.1
maps
P
to an
S^{\prime}_{i}
-
T^{\prime}_{i}
path
P^{\prime}
in
D^{(h,\lambda)}
. Because
F^{\prime}
is a
0.5
-blocking flow in
D^{(h,\lambda)}
, there exists an edge
e^{\prime}\in P^{\prime}
such that
F^{\prime}(e)\geq U^{\prime}(e)/2=U(e)/2
. By the definition of
F
, the original edge
e\in P
corresponding to
e^{\prime}
has
F(e)\geq F^{\prime}(e)/\kappa\geq U(e)/(2\kappa)=U(e)\cdot\Omega(\epsilon/h^{2})
as desired.

The Work and Depth.
The work and depth are dominated by applying
Lemma

6.7
. Because
D^{(h,\lambda)}
has size
O(mh^{2}/\epsilon)
, the final work and depth are
\tilde{O}(mkh^{5}/\epsilon)
and
\tilde{O}(h^{2})
.

6.3
(1+\epsilon)
-Approx
h
-Length Multi-Commodity Maxflows: Proof of
Theorem

6.1

The algorithm is described in
Algorithm

1
. To simplify the analysis,
Algorithm

1
only outputs a
(1+100\epsilon)
-approximate solution. To obtain a
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate solution required by
Theorem

6.1
, we can update
\epsilon\leftarrow\epsilon/100
at the very beginning.

Algorithm 1

Multi-Commodity Length-Constrained Flows and Moving Cuts

A directed graph
G
with edge lengths
\ell
, capacities
U
, and source-sink pairs
\{(S_{i},T_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
; length parameter
h
and constant
\epsilon\in(0,1/100)
.

(1\pm 100\epsilon)
-approximate
h
-length multi-commodity flow
F
and moving cuts
w
.

Let
\zeta=1/\epsilon
and
\eta=\epsilon^{2}/((1+10\epsilon)\ln m)
.

Initialize
w(e)=1/(m^{\zeta}\cdot U(e))
for all edges
e\in E(G)
.

Initialize
\lambda=1/m^{\zeta}
.

Initialize
F
to be a zero multi-commodity flow.

Initialize
w_{\mathrm{best}}\leftarrow w/\lambda
.

while

\lambda<1

do

for

\Theta(h^{3}\log n/\epsilon^{3})
iterations
do

Compute a
h
-length
(1+\epsilon)
-lightest path
\Omega(\epsilon/h^{2})
-blocker
\hat{F}
by applying
Theorem

6.6

on
G
with weights
w
and parameter
\lambda
.

F\leftarrow F+\eta\cdot\hat{F}
.

For each
e\in E(G)
,
w(e)\leftarrow w(e)\cdot(1+\epsilon)^{\hat{F}(e)/U(e)}
.

If
\sum_{e}w(e)U(e)/\lambda\leq\sum_{e}w_{\mathrm{best}}(e)U(e)
, update
w_{\mathrm{best}}\leftarrow w/\lambda
.

end

for

\lambda\leftarrow\lambda\cdot(1+\epsilon)
.

end

while

Return
(F,w_{\mathrm{best}})
.

Correctness.
The feasibility and approximation ratio are given by
Lemma

6.10
and
Lemma

6.11
respectively. Furthermore,
F
is
1/O(kh^{2}\log n/\epsilon^{3})
-fractional because each
\hat{F}
is
1/O(kh^{2}/\epsilon)
-fractional guaranteed by
Theorem

6.6
and
\eta=\Theta(\epsilon^{2}/\log n)
. For each commodity, the number of flow paths in
\mathrm{path}(F)
is at most
O(mh^{5}\log n/\epsilon^{4})
, because each
\hat{F}
has
O(mh^{2}\epsilon)
flow paths for this commodity and
F
is the summation of
\Theta(h^{3}\log n/\epsilon^{3})
such
\hat{F}
(after scaling).

6.9
.

It always hold that
\lambda\leq\min_{1\leq i\leq k}d^{(h)}_{w}(S_{i},T_{i})
.

Proof.

We will prove this by induction. Initially, the statement trivially holds. Now consider a phase of the outer while loop. Let
\lambda_{0}
be the
\lambda
at the beginning of this phase. Assume
\lambda_{0}\leq\min_{1\leq i\leq k}d^{(h)}_{w}(S_{i},T_{i})
at the beginning of this phase, we will show that
(1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0}\leq\min_{1\leq i\leq k}d^{(h)}_{w}(S_{i},T_{i})
holds at the end of this phase.

Assume for contradiction that some path
P\in\bigcup_{i}{\cal P}_{h}(S_{i},T_{i})
has
w(P)\leq(1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0}
during the whole phase. Then, in each of the
\Theta(h^{3}\log n/\epsilon^{3})
iterations of this phase, there is some edge
e\in P
such that
\hat{F}(e)\geq\Omega(\epsilon/h^{2})\cdot U(e)
. By averaging over at most
h
edges in
P
, there is a fixed edge
e\in P
such that
\hat{F}(e)\geq\Omega(\epsilon/h^{2})\cdot U(e)
in at least
\Theta(h^{2}\log n/\epsilon^{3})
iterations.
Therefore, at the end of this phase,

\displaystyle w(e)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{m^{\zeta}\cdot U(e)}\cdot(1+\epsilon)^{\Omega(\epsilon/h^{2})\cdot\Theta(h^{2}\log n/\epsilon^{3})}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{m^{\zeta}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(n)}\cdot(1+\epsilon)^{\Theta(\log n/\epsilon^{2})}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{n^{\Theta(1/\epsilon)}}{m^{1/\epsilon}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(n)}

\displaystyle\geq 1+\epsilon

where we use
U(e)\leq U_{\max}\leq\mathrm{poly}(n)
in the second inequality and
the last inequality is because we set the hidden constant behind
\Theta

to be sufficiently large. This contradicts
w(P)\leq(1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0}
since
\lambda_{0}<1
.
∎

6.10
.

The output
(F,w_{\mathrm{best}})
is feasible.

Proof.

First,
w_{\mathrm{best}}
is feasible because
w_{\mathrm{best}}
is
w/\lambda
at some moment, and
\min_{1\leq i\leq k}d^{(h)}_{w}(S_{i},T_{i})\geq\lambda
always holds by
Lemma

6.9
.

To see that
F
is feasible, for each edge
e\in E(G)
we prove
F(e)\leq U(e)
. By the update rule of
w
, we have
w(e)\geq 1/(m^{\zeta}\cdot U_{\max})\cdot(1+\epsilon)^{F(e)/(\eta\cdot U(e))}
. Also, observe that
w(e)\leq(1+2\epsilon)(1+\epsilon)
because right before the last time
w(e)
is updated,
w(e)\leq w(P)\leq(1+2\epsilon)\lambda\leq 1+2\epsilon
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m^{\zeta}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(n)}\cdot(1+\epsilon)^{F(e)/(\eta\cdot U(e))}

\displaystyle\leq(1+2\epsilon)(1+\epsilon)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m^{\zeta}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(n)}\cdot\exp(\frac{F(e)}{U(e)}\cdot\frac{(1+10\epsilon)\ln m}{\epsilon^{2}}\cdot\ln(1+\epsilon))

\displaystyle\leq O(1)

\displaystyle m^{((F(e)/U(e))(1+10\epsilon)-1)/\epsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{poly}(n)

Suppose
F(e)/U(e)>1
, then we can find small enough
\epsilon
to make the above inequality invalid.
Thus we have
F(e)\leq U(e)
.
∎

6.11
.

The output
(F,w_{\mathrm{best}})
satisfies
(1-100\epsilon)\sum_{e\in E(G)}w_{\mathrm{best}}(e)\cdot U(e)\leq\mathrm{value}(F)
.

Proof.

Let
\beta=\sum_{e}w_{\mathrm{best}}(e)U(e)
be the value of the best dual solution
w_{\mathrm{best}}
. Consider the
i
-th iteration of the inner loop (over the course of the whole algorithm), let
\lambda^{(i)}
and
w^{(i)}
denote the variable
\lambda
and function
w
at the beginning of this iteration. Let
D^{(i)}=\sum_{e\in E(G)}w^{(i)}(e)\cdot U(e)
. Note that
D^{(i)}/\lambda^{(i)}\geq\beta
by the definition of
w_{\mathrm{best}}
. Let
\hat{F}_{i}
denote the path blocker
\hat{F}
computed in this iteration. We have

\displaystyle D^{(i+1)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in E(G)}w^{(i)}(e)\cdot(1+\epsilon)^{\hat{F}_{i}(e)/U(e)}\cdot U(e)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{e\in E(G)}w^{(i)}(e)\cdot U(e)\cdot(1+\epsilon\cdot\hat{F}_{i}(e)/U(e))

\displaystyle=D^{(i)}+\epsilon\cdot\sum_{e\in E(G)}w^{(i)}(e)\cdot\hat{F}_{i}(e)

\displaystyle\leq D^{(i)}+\epsilon\cdot(1+2\epsilon)\lambda^{(i)}\cdot\mathrm{value}(\hat{F}_{i})

\displaystyle\leq D^{(i)}+\epsilon\cdot(1+2\epsilon)\cdot\mathrm{value}(\hat{F}_{i})\cdot\frac{D^{(i)}}{\beta}

\displaystyle\leq D^{(i)}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{\epsilon\cdot(1+2\epsilon)\cdot\mathrm{value}(\hat{F}_{i})}{\beta}\right)

Let
T-1
be the index of the last iteration. Because
F=\eta\sum_{i}\hat{F}_{i}
, we have

D^{(T)}\leq D^{(1)}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{\epsilon\cdot(1+2\epsilon)\cdot\mathrm{value}(F)}{\eta\cdot\beta}\right).

Note that
D^{(1)}=\sum_{e}w^{(1)}(e)U(e)=m^{1-\zeta}
, and
D^{(T)}\geq 1
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle m^{1-\zeta}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{\epsilon\cdot(1+2\epsilon)\cdot\mathrm{value}(F)}{\eta\cdot\beta}\right)

\displaystyle(\zeta-1)\ln m

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon\cdot(1+2\epsilon)\cdot\mathrm{value}(F)}{\eta\cdot\beta}

\displaystyle\frac{1-\epsilon}{(1+2\epsilon)(1+10\epsilon)}\cdot\beta

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{value}(F)

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{1+100\epsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{value}(F),

as desired.
∎

The Work and Depth.
Basically the algorithm just applies
Theorem

6.6

\Theta(h^{3}\log n/\epsilon^{3})
times, so the total work and depth are
\tilde{O}(mkh^{8}/\epsilon^{4})
and
\tilde{O}(h^{5}/\epsilon^{3})
.

7
Approximate Mincost Flows via Flow Boosting

In this section, we finally get parallel
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate mincost flows, for both the concurrent and non-concurrent versions. We first formally define the problems in
Definition

7.1
and
Definition

7.2
, which are from
[
HHL+24
]
. The main result is given by
Theorem

7.3
.

7.1
Concurrent Flow Problem.

Let
G
be a connected graph with edge capacities
u\geq 1
and
D:V\times V\mapsto\mathbb{N}
an integral demand. Let
k=|\mathrm{supp}(D)|
. The
k
-commodity concurrent flow problem
asks to find a capacity-respecting flow
F
routing
\lambda D
for maximum
\lambda
. An algorithm is a
C
-approximation for the concurrent flow problem if it always produces a capacity-respecting flow
F
routing
\lambda D
, where
\lambda\geq\lambda^{*}/C
and
\lambda^{*}
is the maximum value for which there exists a capacity-respecting
F^{*}
routing
\lambda^{*}D
.

In the concurrent flow problem with costs, each edge has a cost
b\geq 0
and there is a total cost budget
B
: the produced flow
F
must additionally have total cost
\sum_{P}F(P)\sum_{e\in P}b(e)\leq B
. An algorithm is a
C
-approximation for the concurrent flow problem with costs if it always produces a capacity-respecting flow
F
of total cost at most
B
routing
\lambda D
, where
\lambda\geq\lambda^{*}/C
and
\lambda^{*}
is the maximum value for which there exists a capacity-respecting
F^{*}
of total cost at most
B
routing
\lambda^{*}D
.

7.2
Non-Concurrent Flow Problem.

Let
G
be a connected graph with edge capacities
u\geq 1
and
S
a set of vertex pairs. Let
k=|S|
. The
k
-commodity non-concurrent flow problem
asks to find a capacity-respecting flow
F
routing flow between vertex pairs in
S
, i.e.
\mathrm{supp}(D_{F})\subseteq S
of maximum value. An algorithm is a
C
-approximation for the non-concurrent flow problem if it always produces a capacity-respecting flow
F
routing flow between vertex pairs in
S
of value
\operatorname{val}(F)\geq\operatorname{val}(F^{*})/C
, where
F^{*}
is the maximum-value capacity-respecting flow routing flow between vertex pairs in
S
.

In the non-concurrent flow problem with costs, each edge has a cost
b\geq 0
and there is a total cost budget
B
: the produced flow
F
must additionally have total cost
\sum_{P}F(P)\sum_{e\in P}b(e)\leq B
. An algorithm is a
C
-approximation for the concurrent flow problem with costs if it always produces a capacity-respecting flow
F
of total cost at most
B
routing flow between vertex pairs in
S
of value
\operatorname{val}(F)\geq\operatorname{val}(F^{*})/C
, where
F^{*}
is the maximum-value capacity-respecting flow of total cost at most
B
routing flow between vertex pairs in
S
.

7.3
.

Let
G
be an undirected graph with vertex costs and capacities. Given a precision parameter
1/\mathrm{poly}\log n\leq\epsilon\leq 1
, there are
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate algorithms for both the
k
-commodity concurrent and non-concurrent mincost flow problems with work
\hat{O}(mk)
and depth
\hat{O}(1)
. The flow solution is outputted explicitly in its edge representation.

7.4
.

We note that our algorithms are randomized and correct with high probability, but they have great potential to be deterministic. The only randomized components are the parallel neighborhood cover algorithm
Lemma

3.3
and the parallel length-constrained vertex expander decomposition algorithm
Theorem

4.1
. The latter is randomized only because the parallel neighborhood cover with separation
Theorem

3.2
. It seems that there have been deterministic results on parallel neighborhood covers
[
REGH22
,
RGH+22
]
, but we have not looked into them carefully.

The high-level approach is to use the flow boosting technique
[
GK07
,
HHL+24
]
. In
Section

7.2
, we state the flow boosting template. In
Section

7.3
, we show how to compute bi-criteria approximate low-step flows with
(1+\epsilon)
total-length slack and moderate congestion, using the approximate length-constrained maxflow algorithm
Theorem

6.1
. In
Section

7.4
, using the LC-flow shortcut
Theorem

5.2
, we can compute the bi-criteria approximate flow on the shortcut graph and map it back the original graph. Lastly, in
Section

5.5.1
we complete the proof of
Theorem

7.3
by plugging the subroutine of
Section

7.4
into the flow boosting template.

7.1
Section Preliminaries

7.5
Parallel Mincost Flow Rounding
[
Coh95
]
.

Let
G
be a directed graph with edge lengths and capacities. Let
S,T\subseteq V(G)
be source and sink vertices. Given the edge representation of a fractional single-commodity flow
\widetilde{F}
in
G
, there is an algorithm that computes the edge representation of an integral single-commodity flow
\hat{F}
in
G
satisfying that

1.

\mathrm{totlen}(\hat{F})\leq\mathrm{totlen}(\widetilde{F})+1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
,

2.

for each edge
e\in E(G)
,
\lfloor F(e)\rfloor\leq\hat{F}(e)\leq\lceil F(e)\rceil
.

The work is
\tilde{O}(m)
and the depth is
\tilde{O}(1)
.

7.6
.

Let
G
be a directed graph with edge lengths and capacities, and let
\mu\geq 1
be a given round parameter which is some power of
2
. Given a (multi-commodity) flow
\widetilde{F}
in
G
, there is an algorithm that computes the edge representation of an
(1/\mu)
-fractional flow
\hat{F}
satisfying the following.

1.

Let
\widetilde{D}
and
\hat{D}
denote
\mathrm{Dem}(\widetilde{F})
and
\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{F})
respectively. For each
(u,v)\in\widetilde{D}
,
\lfloor\widetilde{D}(u,v)\cdot\mu\rfloor/\mu\leq\hat{D}(u,v)\leq\lceil\widetilde{D}(u,v)\cdot\mu\rceil/\mu
.

2.

\mathrm{totlen}(\hat{F})\leq\mathrm{totlen}(\widetilde{F})+1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
.

3.

For each edge
e\in E(G)
,
\widetilde{F}(e)-|\mathrm{supp}(D)|/\mu\leq\hat{F}(e)\leq\widetilde{F}(e)+|\mathrm{supp}(D)|/\mu
.

The work is
\tilde{O}(m\cdot|\mathrm{supp}(D)|)
and the depth is
\tilde{O}(1)
.

Proof.

Roughly speaking, we just apply
Lemma

7.5
on different commodities in parallel. Concretely, let
\widetilde{F}^{(i)}
be the commodity-
i
flow of
\widetilde{F}^{(i)}
corresponding to the source-sink pair
(u,v)\in\mathrm{supp}(\widetilde{D})
. First, we create a graph
G^{(i)}
by adding artificial source
u^{\prime}
and sink
v^{\prime}
, and adding two edges
(u^{\prime},u),(v,v^{\prime})
with length
0
. Second, augmenting
\widetilde{F}^{(i)}
to be a
u^{\prime}
-to-
v^{\prime}
single-commodity flow on
G^{(i)}
by putting
\mathrm{value}(\widetilde{F}^{(i)})=\widetilde{D}(u,v)
flow units on edges
(u^{\prime},u)
and
(v,v^{\prime})
. Third, round
\widetilde{F}^{(i)}
to a
(1/\mu)
-fractional flow
\hat{F}^{(i)}
by applying
Lemma

7.5
on
\mu\cdot\widetilde{F}^{(i)}
and then scaling down the output by
\mu
.

Items

1
and
3
in
Corollary

7.6
come from
Item

1
in
Lemma

7.5
, and
Item

2
in
Corollary

7.6
comes from
Item

2
in
Lemma

7.5
. The work and depth are obvious.
∎

7.2
The Flow Boosting Template

The statement and the proof of the following flow boosting template are almost identical to those in
[
HHL+24
]
, except that we transform them into undirected graphs with vertex lengths, costs and capacities.

7.7
.

Let
G
be an undirected graph with vertex capacity function
U
and vertex cost function
b
, and let
B\geq 0
be the cost budget. Let
{\cal F}
be any convex set of flows in
G
containing at least one capacity-respecting flow, and let
\epsilon,s,\kappa\geq 0
be parameters. Suppose there is an algorithm
{\cal A}
that, given any integral vertex length function
\ell:V\to\{1,2,...,O(m^{1/\epsilon}N/\epsilon)\}
, computes the edge representation of a (non necessarily capacity-respecting) flow
F\in{\cal F}
such that

•

Length slack
s
:
\sum_{v\in V(G)}F(v)\cdot\ell(v)\leq s\cdot\sum_{v\in V(G)}F^{*}(v)\cdot\ell(v)
for any flow
F^{*}\in{\cal F}
that is also capacity-respecting, and

•

Congestion slack
\kappa
:
F(v)\leq\kappa\cdot U(v)
for all
v\in V(G)
, i.e.,
F(v)/\kappa
is capacity-respecting.

Then, there is an algorithm that makes
O(\kappa\epsilon^{-2}\log^{2}n)
calls to the algorithm
{\cal A}
and outputs the edge representation of a flow
\bar{F}\in{\cal F}
and a scalar
\lambda\geq 0
such that

1.

Feasibility: The flow
\lambda\bar{F}
is capacity-respecting with cost at most
b
, and

2.

Approximation factor
\approx s
: Let
\lambda^{*}
be the maximum value such that there exists flow
F^{*}\in{\cal F}
where
\lambda^{*}F^{*}
is capacity-respecting with cost at most
B
. Then
\lambda\geq\frac{1-O(\epsilon)}{s}\lambda^{*}
.

Moreover, the flow
\bar{F}
is a convex combination of the flows returned by the algorithm
{\cal A}
, and this convex combination can be outputted as well. The algorithm takes
\tilde{O}(\kappa\epsilon^{-2}m)
work and
\tilde{O}(\kappa\epsilon^{-2})
depth besides calling
{\cal A}
.

The proof is from
[
HHL+24
]
, and we append it here for completeness.
Let
{\mathcal{K}}
be the set of capacity-respecting flows in
G
, and consider the following flow LP of the graph
G
. We have a variable
x(F)\geq 0
for each
F\in\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{K}
indicating that we send flow
F
scaled by
x(F)
. To avoid clutter, we also define
b(F)=\sum_{v\in V}F(v)\cdot b(v)
as the cost of the flow
F
.

\displaystyle\max\qquad

\displaystyle\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{K}}x(F)

s.t.

\displaystyle\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{K}}F(v)\cdot x(F)\leq U(v)

\displaystyle\forall v\in V

\displaystyle\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{K}}b(F)\cdot x(F)\leq B

\displaystyle x\geq 0

Let
\beta
be the optimal value of this LP. Note that since
\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{K}
is convex, there is an optimal solution with
x(F^{*})=\beta
for some
F^{*}\in\mathcal{F}
and
x(F)=0
elsewhere. It follows that
\beta=\lambda^{*}
.

The dual LP has a variable
y(v)\geq 0
for each vertex
v\in V
as well as a variable
\phi\geq 0
of the cost constraint.

\displaystyle\min\qquad

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in V}U(v)\cdot y(v)+B\cdot\phi

\displaystyle=:D(y,\phi)

s.t.

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in V}F(v)\cdot(y(v)+b(v)\phi)\geq 1

\displaystyle\forall F\in\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{K}

\displaystyle y\geq 0,\,\phi\geq 0

Let
D(y,\phi)=\sum_{v\in V}U(v)\ell(v)+B\cdot\phi
be the objective value of the dual LP.
We note that for any vertex
v\in V
, we interpret the value
y(v)+b(v)\phi
as a length over
v
,
and we further define
\alpha(y,\phi)
as the minimum length taken over any flow
F\in\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{K}
under length function
y+\phi b
:

\alpha(\ell,\phi)=\min_{F\in\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{K}}\sum_{v\in V}F(v)\cdot(\ell(v)+b(v)\phi).

We note that for any feasible solution
\{y_{0},\phi_{0}\}
, we have
\{\frac{y_{0}}{\alpha(y_{0},\phi_{0})},\frac{\phi_{0}}{\alpha(y_{0},\phi_{0})}\}
is also a feasible solution.
Thus by scaling, we can restate the dual LP as finding a length function
y+\phi b
minimizing
D(y,\phi)/\alpha(y,\phi)
.
By LP duality, the minimum is at least
\beta
, the optimal value of the primal LP, which we recall also equals
\lambda^{*}\geq 1
.

Algorithm.

The algorithm initializes length functions
y^{(0)}(v)=\delta/U(v)
and
\phi^{(0)}=\delta/B
for parameter
\delta=n^{-1/\epsilon}
, and proceeds for a number of iterations. For each iteration
i
, the algorithm wishes to call the oracle
\mathcal{O}
on length function
y^{(i-1)}+\phi^{(i-1)}b
, but the length function
y^{(i-1)}+\phi^{(i-1)}b
is not integral. However, we will ensure that they are always in the range
[\delta/N,O(1)]
. So the algorithm first multiplies each length by
O(N/(\delta\epsilon))=O(n^{1/\epsilon}N/\epsilon)
and then rounds the weights to integers so that each length is increased by at most roughly the same factor up to
(1+\epsilon)
after scaled back.
The algorithm calls the oracle on these scaled, integral weights to obtain a flow
F^{(i)}
. On the original, unscaled graph, the flow satisfies the following two properties guaranteed by the oracle
{\mathcal{O}}
:

1.

Length slack
(1+\epsilon)s
:
\sum_{v\in V}F^{(i)}(v)\cdot(y^{(i-1)}(v)+b(v)\phi^{(i-1)})\leq(1+\epsilon)s\cdot\alpha(y^{(i-1)},\phi^{(i-1)})
, and

2.

Congestion slack
\kappa
:
F(v)\leq\kappa U(v)
for all
v\in V
, i.e.,
F/\kappa\in\mathcal{K}
.

Define
z^{(i)}=\min\{1,\,B/b(F^{(i)})\}
so that
b(z^{(i)}F^{(i)})\leq B
, i.e., the cost of the scaled flow
z^{(i)}F^{(i)}
is within the budget
B
.
The lengths are then updated as

y^{(i)}(v)=y^{(i-1)}(v)\bigg(1+\frac{\epsilon}{\kappa}\cdot\frac{z^{(i)}F^{(i)}(v)}{U(v)}\bigg)\quad\text{and}\quad\phi^{(i)}=\phi^{(i-1)}\bigg(1+\frac{\epsilon}{\kappa}\cdot\frac{b(z^{(i)}F^{(i)})}{B}\bigg).

This concludes the description of a single iteration. The algorithm terminates upon reaching the first iteration
t
for which
D(t)\geq 1
and outputs

\bar{F}=\frac{z^{(1)}F^{(1)}+z^{(2)}F^{(2)}+\cdots+z^{(t-1)}F^{(t-1)}}{z^{(1)}+z^{(2)}+\cdots+z^{(t-1)}}\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda=\frac{z^{(1)}+z^{(2)}+\cdots+z^{(t-1)}}{\kappa\log_{1+\epsilon}1/\delta}.

Correctness Analysis.

We first show the feasibility of the flow
\bar{F}
.

7.8
.

The scaled down flow
\lambda\cdot\bar{F}=\frac{1}{\kappa\log_{1+\epsilon}1/\delta}(z^{(1)}F^{(1)}+z^{(2)}F^{(2)}+\cdots+z^{(t-1)}F^{(t-1)})
is capacity-respecting with cost at most
B
.

Proof.

Consider any vertex
v
. On each iteration, we route
z^{(i)}F^{(i)}(v)\leq\kappa U(v)
units of flow through
v
and increase its corresponding variable
y(v)
by a factor
(1+\frac{\epsilon}{\kappa}\cdot\frac{z^{(i)}F^{(i)}(v)}{U(v)})\leq 1+\epsilon
. So for every
\kappa U(v)
units of flow routed through
v
over the iterations, we increase its variable by at least a factor
1+\epsilon
.
Initially, the value of its variable is
\delta/U(v)
, and after
t-1
iterations, it satisfies
y^{(t-1)}(v)\leq 1/U(v)
because otherwise
D(y^{(t-1)},\phi^{(t-1)})>1
which leads to an early termination. Therefore the total amount of flow through
v
in the first
t-1
phases is strictly less than
\kappa\log_{1+\epsilon}\frac{1/U(v)}{\delta/U(v)}\cdot U(v)=\kappa\log_{1+\epsilon}1/\delta\cdot U(v)
. Scaling the flow down by
\kappa\log_{1+\epsilon}1/\delta
, we obtain a capacity-respecting flow.

We can similarly show that the scaled down flow has cost at most
B
.
On each iteration, we route a flow of cost
b(z^{(i)}F^{(i)})\leq B
and increase the variable
\phi^{(i)}
by a factor
(1+\frac{\epsilon}{\kappa}\cdot\frac{b(z^{(i)}F^{(i)})}{B})\leq 1+\epsilon/\kappa
over the previous variable
\phi^{(i-1)}
. So for every
B
cost of flow routed, we increase the variable
\phi^{(i)}
by at least a factor
1+\epsilon/\kappa
. And for every
\kappa B
cost of flow routed, the length increases by at least a factor
(1+\epsilon/\kappa)^{\kappa}\geq 1+\epsilon
. Initially,
\phi^{(0)}=\delta/B
, and after
t-1
iterations, we similarly have
\phi^{(t-1)}\leq 1/B
. Therefore the total cost of flow in the first
t-1
phases is strictly less than
\kappa B\log_{1+\epsilon}\frac{1/B}{\delta/B}=\kappa\log_{1+\epsilon}(1/\delta)\cdot B
. Scaling the flow down by
\kappa\log_{1+\epsilon}1/\delta
, we obtain a flow with cost at most
B
.
∎

Then we argue about the approximation factor for
\bar{F}
.
We will analyze the values of
D(y^{(i)},\phi^{(i)})
and
\alpha(y^{(i)},\phi^{(i)})
for the variables
y^{(i)},\phi^{(i)}
. To avoid clutter, we denote
D(i)=D(y^{(i)},\phi^{(i)})
and
\alpha(i)=\alpha(y^{(i)},\phi^{(i)})
. For each iteration
i
, we have

\displaystyle D(i)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in V}U(v)\cdot y^{(i)}(v)+B\cdot\phi^{(i)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in V}U(v)\cdot y^{(i-1)}(v)\bigg(1+\frac{\epsilon}{\kappa}\cdot\frac{z^{(i)}F^{(i)}(v)}{U(v)}\bigg)+B\cdot\phi^{(i-1)}\bigg(1+\frac{\epsilon}{\kappa}\cdot\frac{b(z^{(i)}F^{(i)})}{B}\bigg)

\displaystyle=D(i-1)+\frac{\epsilon}{\kappa}\cdot\sum_{v\in V}z^{(i)}F^{(i)}(v)\cdot y^{(i-1)}(v)+\frac{\epsilon}{\kappa}\cdot z^{(i)}\cdot b(F^{(i)})\cdot\phi^{(i-1)}

\displaystyle=D(i-1)+\frac{\epsilon}{\kappa}\cdot z^{(i)}\bigg(\sum_{v\in V}F^{(i)}(v)\cdot(y^{(i-1)}(v)+b(v)\phi^{(i-1)})\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq D(i-1)+\frac{\epsilon}{\kappa}\cdot z^{(i)}\cdot(1+\epsilon)s\cdot\alpha(i-1),

where in the last inequality we use the length slack property of the flow from the oracle
{\mathcal{O}}
.
Recall that
\beta
is the optimal value of the primal LP problem, then we have
D(i-1)/\alpha(i-1)\geq\beta
.
Further

D(i)\leq D(i-1)+\frac{\epsilon}{\kappa}\cdot z^{(i)}\cdot(1+\epsilon)s\cdot\frac{D(i-1)}{\beta}.

Define
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon(1+\epsilon)s/\kappa
so that

D(i)\leq\bigg(1+\frac{\epsilon(1+\epsilon)sz^{(i)}}{\kappa\beta}\bigg)D(i-1)=\bigg(1+\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}z^{(i)}}{\beta}\bigg)D(i-1).

Since
D(0)=(n+1)\delta\leq m\delta
, we have for
i\geq 1

\displaystyle D(i)\leq\bigg(\prod_{j\leq i}(1+\epsilon^{\prime}z^{(j)}/\beta)\bigg)m\delta

\displaystyle=\bigg(1+\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}z^{(i)}}{\beta}\bigg)m\delta\prod_{j\leq i-1}\bigg(1+\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}z^{(j)}}{\beta}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\epsilon^{\prime})m\delta\exp\bigg(\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}\sum_{j\leq i-1}z^{(j)}}{\beta}\bigg),

where the last inequality uses our assumption that
\beta\geq 1
and the fact that
z^{(j)}\leq 1
by definition. To avoid clutter, define
z^{(\leq i)}=\sum_{j\leq i}z^{(j)}
for all
i
.

The procedure stops at the first iteration
t
for which
D(t)\geq 1
. Therefore,

1\leq D(t)\leq(1+\epsilon^{\prime})m\delta\exp\bigg(\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}z^{(\leq t-1)}}{\beta}\bigg),

which implies

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{z^{(\leq t-1)}}\leq\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{\ln\frac{1}{(1+\epsilon^{\prime})m\delta}}.

(1)

We further note the fact that
\delta=n^{-1/\epsilon}
to obtain

\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{*}}=\frac{z^{(\leq t-1)}}{\kappa\log_{1+\epsilon}1/\delta}\cdot\frac{1}{\beta}\geq\frac{\ln\frac{1}{(1+\epsilon^{\prime})m\delta}}{\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\kappa\log_{1+\epsilon}1/\delta}=\frac{\ln\frac{1}{(1+\epsilon^{\prime})m\delta}}{\epsilon(1+\epsilon)s\log_{1+\epsilon}1/\delta}\geq\frac{1-O(\epsilon)}{s}.

Running time.

Recall from above that
\lambda\bar{F}
is capacity-respecting with cost at most
B
, so
\lambda\leq\beta
. Since
\lambda=\frac{z^{(\leq t-1)}}{\kappa\log_{1+\epsilon}1/\delta}
, we obtain
z^{(\leq t-1)}\leq\beta\kappa\log_{1+\epsilon}1/\delta
. On each iteration
i\leq t-1
, either
z^{(i)}=1
or
z^{(i)}<1
, and the latter case implies that
b(z^{(i)}F^{(i)})=B
, which means
\phi^{(i)}=\phi^{(i-1)}(1+\epsilon/\kappa)
. Initially,
\phi^{(0)}=\delta/B
, and after
t-1
iterations, since
D(t-1)<1
, we have
\phi^{(t-1)}\leq D(t-1)/B<1/B
. So the event
\phi^{(i)}=\phi^{(i-1)}(1+\epsilon/\kappa)
can happen at most
\log_{1+\epsilon/\kappa}1/\delta
times. It follows that
z^{(i)}<1
for at most
\log_{1+\epsilon/\kappa}1/\delta
values of
i\leq t-1
. Since
z^{(\leq t-1)}\leq\beta\kappa\log_{1+\epsilon}1/\delta
, we have
z^{(i)}=1
for at most
\beta\kappa\log_{1+\epsilon}1/\delta
many values of
i\leq t-1
. Therefore, the number of iterations
t
is at most

\log_{1+\epsilon/\kappa}1/\delta+\beta\kappa\log_{1+\epsilon}1/\delta+1=O(\beta\kappa\epsilon^{-2}\log m).

By scaling all vertex capacities and costs by various powers of two, we can ensure that
\beta\in[1,2]
on at least one guess, so the number of iterations is
O(\kappa\log_{1+\epsilon}1/\delta)=O(\kappa\epsilon^{-2}\log m)
. Doing so also ensures that
\lambda^{*}=\beta\geq 1
as we had previously assumed. For incorrect guesses, we terminate the algorithm above after
O(\kappa\epsilon^{-2}\log m)
iterations to not waste further computation. Among all guesses, we take the one with maximum
\lambda
that satisfies feasibility. Since there are
O(\log n)
relevant powers of two, the running time picks up an overhead of
O(\log n)
.

7.3
Approximate Low-Step Min-Total-Length Multi-Commodity Flows

In this subsection, we want to show
Lemma

7.9
, but in fact we will prove a slightly more general version
Lemma

7.10
. The proof of
Lemma

7.9
is deferred to the end of this subsection after finishing proving
Lemma

7.10
. For better understanding, we note that in
Lemma

7.9
, choosing
\tau=1
means we are in the non-concurrent setting, while choosing
\tau=|D|
means the concurrent setting.

7.9
.

Let
G
be an undirected graph with positive integral vertex lengths
\ell
and integral vertex capacities
U
. Given an integral demand
D
, step bound
t\geq 1
, a flow value threshold
\tau
equal to either
1
or
|D|
, suppose there exists a feasible flow
F^{*}
partially routing
D
with
\mathrm{value}(F^{*})=\tau
and step
t
. Then further given a precision parameter
10/n\leq\epsilon<1
, there is an algorithm that computes the path representation of a flow
F
satisfying the following.

1.

\mathrm{Dem}(F)\leq D
and
\mathrm{value}(F)=\tau
.

2.

F
has congestion
O(\log^{2}n/\epsilon)
in
G
.

3.

\mathrm{totlen}(F)\leq(1+\epsilon)^{5}\cdot\mathrm{totlen}(F^{*})
for any feasible flow
F^{*}
partially routing
D
with
\mathrm{value}(F^{*})=\tau
and step
t
.

The work and depth are
\tilde{O}(mk\cdot t^{8}/\epsilon^{9})
and
\tilde{O}(t^{5}/\epsilon^{6})
respectively, where
k=|\mathrm{supp}(D)|
is the number of commodities.

The intuition behind the proof of
Lemma

7.10
is a simple greedy strategy, as mentioned in
Section

2.2
. But there is a small technical issue. When we pick
\tau=|D|
, we are actually in the concurrent setting, while
Theorem

6.1
we will use is an approximate algorithm in the non-concurrent setting. Roughly speaking, when we miss an
\epsilon
-fractional of total demands in the non-concurrent setting, we may miss some commodity entirely, which is not allowed in the concurrent setting. A simple fix is to iterate the non-concurrent approximate algorithm
\tilde{O}(\log n)
times to reduce the missing fraction to
1/n
. This is what the second For loop in
Algorithm

2
is doing.

7.10
.

Let
G
be a directed graph with positive integral edge lengths
\ell
and integral capacities
U
. Given an integral demand
D
, step bound
t\geq 1
, and a flow value threshold
1\leq\tau\leq|D|
, suppose there exists a feasible flow
F^{*}
partially routing
D
with
\mathrm{value}(F^{*})=\tau
and step
t
. Then further given a precision parameter
10/n\leq\epsilon<1
, there is an algorithm that computes the path representation of a flow
F
satisfying the following.

1.

\mathrm{Dem}(F)\leq D
and
\mathrm{value}(F)=\tau-1/n
,

2.

F
has congestion
O(\log^{2}n/\epsilon)
in
G
,

3.

\mathrm{totlen}(F)\leq(1+\epsilon)^{4}\cdot\mathrm{totlen}(F^{*})
for any feasible flow
F^{*}
partially routing
D
with
\mathrm{value}(F^{*})=\tau
and step
t
.

The work and depth are
\tilde{O}(mk\cdot t^{8}/\epsilon^{9})
and
\tilde{O}(t^{5}/\epsilon^{6})
respectively, where
k=|\mathrm{supp}(D)|
is the number of commodities.

Proof.

The algorithm is described in
Algorithm

2
. Here we discuss in details the main steps on
9
and
12
. Let
\mu
be the smallest power of
2
at least
n^{4}
.

Algorithm 2

Low-Step Multi-Commodity Flows:
LowStepFlows
(G,D,t,\epsilon)

1:

Graph
G
with integral edge lengths
\ell
and integral capacities
u
, integral demand
D
, step bound
t\geq 1
, and a tradeoff parameter
\epsilon
.

2:

A flow
F
partially routing
D
with
|D|-\mathrm{value}(F)\leq 1/n
and congestion
O(\log^{2}n/\epsilon)
, such that
\mathrm{totlen}(F)\leq(1+\epsilon)^{4}\cdot\mathrm{totlen}(F^{*})
for any feasible flow
F^{*}
routing
D
with step
t
.

3:

Let
\mu
be the smallest power of
2
such that
\mu\geq n^{4}
.

4:

Initialize
F\leftarrow 0
and
D^{\prime}\leftarrow D
.

5:

for

p
from
0
to
\bar{p}=\lceil\log_{1+\epsilon}(nN)\rceil

do

6:

h_{p}\leftarrow(1+\epsilon)^{p}
.

7:

Let
G^{\prime}
be
G
with edge lengths
\ell^{\prime}
such that
\ell^{\prime}(e)=\lceil\ell(e)/(\epsilon h_{p}/t)\rceil

8:

for

j
from
1
to
\bar{j}=\Theta(\log n)

do

9:

Let
\widetilde{F}_{p,j}
be a
1.5
-approximate
(t/\epsilon+t)
-length maxflow with
\mathrm{Dem}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})\leq D^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
.

10:

\triangleright
Apply
Theorem

6.1

11:

If
\mathrm{value}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})\leq 1/(2n)
, terminate the inner loop.

12:

Round
\widetilde{F}_{p,j}
to a
(1/\mu)
-fractional flow
\hat{F}_{p,j}
.
\triangleright
Apply
Corollary

7.6

13:

Let
\lambda
be the maximum value in
[0,1]
such that
\mathrm{value}(F+\lambda\hat{F}_{p,j})\leq\tau-1/n

14:

F_{p,j}\leftarrow\lambda\hat{F}_{p,j}
.

15:

F\leftarrow F+F_{p,j}
.

16:

If
\lambda<1
, terminate the algorithm and return
F
.

17:

D^{\prime}\leftarrow D^{\prime}-\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{F}_{p,j})
.

18:

end

for

19:

end

for

20:

Return
F
.

9
.
We will compute a feasible (fractional) flow
\widetilde{F}_{p,j}
in
G^{\prime}
with
\mathrm{Dem}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})\leq D^{\prime}
and length
t/\epsilon+t
satisfying that
\mathrm{value}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})\geq\mathrm{value}(\widetilde{F}^{*}_{p,j})/(1+1/2)
for any feasible flow
\widetilde{F}^{*}_{p,j}
in
G^{\prime}
with
\mathrm{Dem}(\widetilde{F}^{*}_{p,j})\leq D^{\prime}
and length
t/\epsilon+t
. To do this,

•

We first construct a graph
G^{\prime\prime}
by starting from
G^{\prime}
and adding, for each demand pair
(u_{i},v_{i})\in\mathrm{supp}(D^{\prime})
, two artificial vertices
s_{i},t_{i}
and two artificial directed edges
(s_{i},u_{i}),(v_{i},t_{i})
with capacity
D^{\prime}(u_{i},v_{i})
and length
1
.

•

We will see in a moment that
12
guarantees that
D^{\prime}
is always
(1/\mu)
-fractional.
Hence, we apply
Theorem

6.1
on
G^{\prime\prime}
with capacities scaled up by
\mu
(to ensure integral capacities), source-sink pairs
\{(s_{i},t_{i})\mid i\}
, length parameter
t/\epsilon+t+2
and approximation parameter
0.5
. Let
\widetilde{F}^{\prime\prime}_{p,j}
be the output.

•

\widetilde{F}_{p,j}
is obtained by scaling down
\widetilde{F}^{\prime\prime}_{p,j}
by
\mu
and removing the flow on the artificial edges.

12
.
We simply apply
Corollary

7.6
on
G^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{F}_{p,j}
with rounding parameter
\mu
. The output
\hat{F}_{p,j}
is a
(1/\mu)
-fractional flow on
G^{\prime}
satisfying that

1.

\lfloor\mathrm{Dem}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})\cdot\mu\rfloor/\mu\leq\mathrm{Dem}(\hat{F}_{p,j})\leq\lceil\mathrm{Dem}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})\mu\rceil/\mu\leq D^{\prime}
(since
D^{\prime}
is
(1/\mu)
-fractional), which implies
\mathrm{value}(\hat{F}_{p,j})\geq\mathrm{value}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})-n^{2}/\mu\geq\mathrm{value}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})-1/n^{2}

2.

\mathrm{totlen}(\hat{F}_{p,j})\leq\mathrm{totlen}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})+1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
.

3.

\hat{F}_{p,j}(e)\leq U(e)+n^{2}/\mu
for each
e\in E(G^{\prime})
, which implies that
\hat{F}_{p,j}
has congestion
1+1/n^{2}
.

Finally, because
\hat{F}_{p,j}
is
(1/\mu)
-fractional, the new
D^{\prime}
on
17
is also
(1/\mu)
-fractional inductively.

Correctness.
The congestion of
F
is obviously
O(\log^{2}n/\epsilon)
because there are
O(\log^{2}n/\epsilon)
different pairs of
p,j
, and each
\hat{F}_{p,j}
has congestion
O(1)
. Also, the algorithm directly gives
\mathrm{Dem}(F)\leq D
. It remains to show
\mathrm{value}(F)=\tau-1/n
, and
\mathrm{totlen}(F)\leq(1+\epsilon)\mathrm{totlen}(F^{*})
for any feasible flow
F^{*}
partially routing
D
in
G
with
\mathrm{value}(F^{*})=\tau
and step
t
.

We begin by setting up some notations. First, we emphasize that for each
0\leq p\leq\bar{p}
and
1\leq j\leq\bar{j}
, we will regard
\widetilde{F}_{p,j}
as a zero flow if it is not computed by the algorithm (same for
\hat{F}_{p,j}
and
F_{p,j}
).
Let
F_{p}=\sum_{p^{\prime}\leq p,j}F_{p^{\prime},j}
and similarly, let
F^{*}_{p}
be the maximum
h_{p}
-length subflow of
F^{*}
. For convenience, we refer to
F_{-1}
and
F^{*}_{-1}
as zero flows. We first prove
7.11
and
Lemma

7.12
, which is useful for our argument later.

7.11
.

For any
0\leq p\leq\bar{p}
, we have

1.

An
h_{p}
-length
t
-step flow
F
in
G
has length
t/\epsilon+t
in
G^{\prime}
,

2.

An
(t/\epsilon+t)
-length flow
F^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
has length
(1+\epsilon)h_{p}
in
G
.

Proof.

For
Item

1
, consider any flow path
P\in\mathrm{path}(F)
. We know
P
has step
t
and length
\sum_{e\in P}\ell(e)\leq h_{p}
. Therefore, the length of
P
in
G^{\prime}
is

\sum_{e\in P}\ell^{\prime}(e)=\sum_{e\in P}\lceil\ell(e)/(\epsilon h_{p}/t)\rceil\leq\sum_{e\in P}(\ell(e)/(\epsilon h_{p}/t)+1)\leq t/\epsilon+t.

For
Item

2
, consider any flow path
P^{\prime}\in\mathrm{path}(F^{\prime})
. The length of
P^{\prime}
in
G
is
\sum_{e\in P^{\prime}}\ell(e)\leq\sum_{e\in P^{\prime}}(\epsilon h_{p}/t)\cdot\ell^{\prime}(e)\leq(1+\epsilon)h_{p}
.
∎

7.12
.

For each
0\leq p\leq\bar{p}
, we have
\mathrm{value}(F_{p})\geq\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{p})-1/n
.

Proof.

Consider a fixed
p
. We set up some notations: for each
j\geq 0
, let
F_{p,\leq j}=F_{p-1}+\sum_{j^{\prime}\leq j}F_{p,j^{\prime}}
, and let
D^{\prime}_{p,j}
be the
D^{\prime}
at the beginning of iteration
j
.

Case 1: terminate on
16
.
Consider the case that the inner loop for some
p^{\prime}\leq p
terminates on
16
. Then we directly have
\mathrm{value}(F_{p})=\mathrm{value}(F_{p^{\prime}})=\tau-1/n\geq\mathrm{value}(F^{*})-1/n\geq\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{p})-1/n
as desired.

Case 2: terminate on
11
.
Next, we consider the case that the inner loop terminates on
11
in some iteration
j
. By the definition of
F^{*}_{p}
, there is an
h^{\prime}_{p}
-length
t
-step feasible flow in
G
routing
\min\{\mathrm{Dem}(F^{*}_{p}),D^{\prime}_{p,j}\}
. By
7.11
, this flow is an
(t/\epsilon+t)
-length feasible flow in
G^{\prime}
routing
\min\{\mathrm{Dem}(F^{*}_{p}),D^{\prime}_{p,j}\}
. Therefore,
\widetilde{F}_{p,j}
has

\mathrm{value}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})\geq|\min\{\mathrm{Dem}(F^{*}_{p}),D^{\prime}_{p,j}\}|/1.5\geq(\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{P})-\mathrm{value}(F_{p,\leq j-1}))/1.5.

where the second inequality is because

\displaystyle\min\{\mathrm{Dem}(F^{*}_{p}),D^{\prime}_{p,j}\}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Dem}(F^{*}_{p})+D^{\prime}_{p,j}-\max\{\mathrm{Dem}(F^{*}_{p}),D^{\prime}_{p,j}\}

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{Dem}(F^{*}_{p})+D^{\prime}_{p,j}-D

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Dem}(F^{*}_{p})-\mathrm{Dem}(F_{p,\leq j-1}).

Since
\mathrm{value}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})\leq 1/(2n)
from
11
, we have

\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{P})-\mathrm{value}(F_{p,\leq j-1})\leq 1/n

as desired.

Case 3: terminate after
\Theta(\log n)
iterations.
Finally, we consider the case that the inner loop has
\Theta(\log n)
iterations. In this case, we will show the following statement instead: for each
j\geq 0
,

\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{p})-\mathrm{value}(F_{p,\leq j})\leq\max\{\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{p})/2^{j},1/n\}.

Providing this statement, when
j=\Theta(\log n)\geq\log_{2}(\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{p})\cdot n)
(we choose a sufficiently large constant behind the
\Theta
on
9
), it gives
\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{p})-\mathrm{value}(F_{p})\leq 1/n
as desired.

Now we prove this statement by induction. In the base case
j=0
, the statement holds trivially. Consider an inductive step
j\geq 1
, and assume the statement holds for
j-1
. We also assume
\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{p})-\mathrm{value}(F_{p,\leq j-1})>1/n
, otherwise this inductive step is trivial.

On
9
, an argument similar to Case 2 gives

\mathrm{value}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})\geq(\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{P})-\mathrm{value}(F_{p,\leq j-1}))/1.5

On
12
, the flow
\hat{F}_{p,j}
has

\mathrm{value}(\hat{F}_{p,j})\geq\mathrm{value}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})-1/n^{2}\geq(\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{P})-\mathrm{value}(F_{p,\leq j-1}))/2

where we use
\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{P})-\mathrm{value}(F_{p,\leq j-1})\geq 1/n
. Finally,

\displaystyle\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{p})-\mathrm{value}(F_{p,\leq j})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{p})-\mathrm{value}(F_{p,\leq j-1})-\mathrm{value}(F_{p,j})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{p})-\mathrm{value}(F_{p,\leq j-1})-\mathrm{value}(\hat{F}_{p,j})

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot(\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{p})-\mathrm{value}(F_{p,\leq j-1}))

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{p})/2^{j},

where the second equality is because
F_{p,j}=\hat{F}_{p,j}
.
∎

To see
\mathrm{value}(F)=\tau-1/n
. Note that when
p=\lceil\log_{1+\epsilon}(nN)\rceil
,
h_{p}\geq nN
is sufficiently large and we have
F=F_{p}
and
F^{*}=F^{*}_{p}
. By
Lemma

7.12
,
\mathrm{value}(F)\geq\mathrm{value}(F^{*})-1/n=\tau-1/n
. On the other hand,
13
,
14
and
15
give
\mathrm{value}(F)\leq\tau-1/n
. Therefore, we can conclude
\mathrm{value}(F)=\tau-1/n
.

Lastly, we prove
\mathrm{totlen}(F)\leq(1+\epsilon)^{4}\cdot\mathrm{totlen}(F^{*})
. We note the notation
\mathrm{totlen}(\cdot)
is with respect to the length function
\ell
. We first show
7.13
below.

7.13
.

For each
0\leq p\leq\bar{p}
and
1\leq j\leq\bar{j}
,
\mathrm{totlen}(\hat{F}_{p,j})\leq\mathrm{value}(\hat{F}_{p,j})\cdot(1+\epsilon)^{3}\cdot h_{p}
.

Proof.

We assume that
\hat{F}_{p,j}
is computed by the algorithm, otherwise we view
\hat{F}_{p,j}
as a zero flow and the claim is trivial.

By
7.11
, we have

\mathrm{totlen}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})\leq\mathrm{value}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})\cdot(1+\epsilon)h_{p}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{totlen}(\hat{F}_{p,j})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{totlen}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})+1/\mathrm{poly}(n)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{value}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})\cdot(1+\epsilon)h_{p}+1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
