\displaystyle\leq(\mathrm{value}(\hat{F}_{p,j})+1/n^{2})\cdot(1+\epsilon)h_{p}+1/\mathrm{poly}(n)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{value}(\hat{F}_{p,j})\cdot(1+\epsilon)^{3}h_{p},

where the last inequality is because
\epsilon\geq 10/n
and
\mathrm{value}(\hat{F}_{p,j})\geq\mathrm{value}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})-1/n^{2}\geq 1/(2n)-1/n^{2}
(
11
guarantees that
\mathrm{value}(\widetilde{F}_{p,j})\geq 1/(2n)
).
∎

Providing
7.13
, for each
0\leq p\leq\bar{p}
, we have

\mathrm{totlen}(F_{p}-F_{p-1})\leq\mathrm{value}(F_{p}-F_{p-1})\cdot(1+\epsilon)^{3}h_{p},

because
F_{p}-F_{p-1}=\sum_{0\leq j\leq\bar{j}}F_{p,j}
is a linear combination of
\{\hat{F}_{p,j}\mid 1\leq j\leq\bar{j}\}
. On the other hand, we have

\mathrm{totlen}(F^{*}_{p}-F^{*}_{p-1})\geq\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{p}-F^{*}_{p-1})\cdot h_{p}/(1+\epsilon),

because when
p\geq 1
, each flow path in
F^{*}_{p}-F^{*}_{p-1}
has length at least
h_{p-1}=h_{p}/(1+\epsilon)
by definition. Moreover, when
p=0
, each flow path has length at least
1=h_{0}
because the edge length
\ell
are positive integers.

Next we rewrite
\mathrm{totlen}(F)
and
\mathrm{totlen}(F^{*})
as follows. For convenience, we let
h_{-1}=0
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{totlen}(F)

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq p\leq\bar{p}}\mathrm{totlen}(F_{p}-F_{p-1})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{0\leq p\leq\bar{p}}\mathrm{value}(F_{p}-F_{p-1})\cdot(1+\epsilon)^{3}\cdot h_{p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq p\leq\bar{p}}(\mathrm{value}(F-F_{p-1})-\mathrm{value}(F-F_{p}))\cdot(1+\epsilon)^{3}\cdot h_{p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq p\leq\bar{p}}\mathrm{value}(F-F_{p-1})\cdot(1+\epsilon)^{3}\cdot(h_{p}-h_{p-1}).

Similarly,

\mathrm{totlen}(F^{*})\geq\sum_{0\leq p\leq\bar{p}}\mathrm{value}(F^{*}-F^{*}_{p-1})\cdot(h_{p}-h_{p-1})/(1+\epsilon).

Furthermore, for each
0\leq p\leq\bar{p}
, we have

\mathrm{value}(F-F_{p-1})\leq\mathrm{value}(F^{*}-F^{*}_{p-1})

because

\displaystyle\mathrm{value}(F-F_{p-1})-\mathrm{value}(F^{*}-F^{*}_{p-1})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{value}(F)-\mathrm{value}(F^{*})+(\mathrm{value}(F^{*}_{p-1})-\mathrm{value}(F_{p-1}))

\displaystyle\leq(\tau-1/n)-\tau+1/n

\displaystyle\leq 0,

where the second inequality is by
\mathrm{value}(F)=\tau-1/n
,
\mathrm{value}(F^{*})=\tau
and
Lemma

7.12
. Combining all these above, we can conclude
\mathrm{totlen}(F)\leq(1+\epsilon)^{4}\cdot\mathrm{totlen}(F^{*})

Work and Depth.
Clearly the bottleneck is
9
. A single execution takes
\tilde{O}(m|\mathrm{supp}(D)|\cdot(t/\epsilon)^{8})
work and
\tilde{O}((t/\epsilon)^{5})
depth. We need to execute it
\bar{p}\cdot\bar{j}=\tilde{O}(1/\epsilon)
times sequentially. Therefore, the final work and depth are
\tilde{O}(m|\mathrm{supp}(D)|\cdot t^{8}/\epsilon^{9})
and
\tilde{O}(t^{5}/\epsilon^{6})
.

∎

Proof of
Lemma

7.9
.

We first construct a directed graph
G_{\mathrm{dir}}
(with positive integral edge lengths and capacities) as a counterpart of
G
by standard vertex splitting. Initially
G_{\mathrm{dir}}
is empty.

•

For each vertex
v\in V(G)
, we add two vertices
v_{\mathrm{in}}
and
v_{\mathrm{out}}
to
G_{\mathrm{dir}}
with a directed edge
e_{v}
from
v_{\mathrm{in}}
to
v_{\mathrm{out}}
. The edge
e_{v}
has length
\ell(e_{v})=\ell(v)
and capacity
U(e_{v})=U(v)
.

•

For each edge
e=\{u,v\}\in V(G)
, we add two edges, one from
u_{\mathrm{out}}
to
v_{\mathrm{in}}
and the other one from
v_{\mathrm{out}}
to
v_{\mathrm{in}}
. Both edges have length
0
and capacity infinity (to be precise, set the capacity to be
10mN
which is sufficiently large).

The following
7.14
basically says we can transfer flows between
G
and
G_{\mathrm{dir}}
.

7.14
.

We have the following:

•

(Forward mapping) Any flow
F
in
G
can be mapped to a flow
F_{\mathrm{dir}}
routing the same demand in
G_{\mathrm{dir}}
with
\mathrm{totlen}(F_{\mathrm{dir}})=\mathrm{totlen}(F)
,
\operatorname{cong}(F_{\mathrm{dir}})=\mathrm{cong}(F)
and
\mathrm{step}(F_{\mathrm{dir}})\leq 2\mathrm{step}(F)
.

•

(Backward mapping) Any feasible flow
F_{\mathrm{dir}}
in
G
can be mapped to a feasible flow
F
routing the same demand in
G
with
\mathrm{totlen}(F)=\mathrm{totlen}(F_{\mathrm{dir}})
and
\mathrm{cong}(F)=\mathrm{cong}(F_{\mathrm{dir}})
.

Moreover, the backward mapping can be done in
O(m)
work and
O(1)
depth.

We then compute the desired flow
F
as follows.

1.

Apply
Lemma

7.10
on
G_{\mathrm{dir}}
with the same
D,\tau,\epsilon
but a different step bound
2t
. Let
\widetilde{F}_{\mathrm{dir}}
be the output with guarantees stated in
Lemma

7.9
.

2.

For each demand pair
(u_{i},v_{i})\in\mathrm{supp}(D)
, let
F^{(i)}_{\mathrm{dir}}=\lambda_{i}\cdot\widetilde{F}^{(i)}_{\mathrm{dir}}
, where

\lambda_{i}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\tau/(\tau-1/n),\ \text{if}\ \tau=1\\
&D(u_{i},v_{i})/\mathrm{value}(\widetilde{F}^{(i)}_{\mathrm{dir}}),\ \text{if}\ \tau=|D|\end{aligned}\right.

Let
F_{\mathrm{dir}}
be the multi-commodity flow containing all
F_{\mathrm{dir},i}
.

3.

Compute
F
by mapping
F_{\mathrm{dir}}
back to
G
using
7.14
.

To see the correctness, first we clearly have
\mathrm{Dem}(F)\leq D
and
\mathrm{Dem}(F)=\tau
because of Step 2. Regarding the congestion and total length, note that the scaling factor in Step 2 is at most
1+\epsilon
no matter
\tau
equals to
1
or
|D|
. In particular, when
\tau=|D|
, we have
D(u_{i},v_{i})/(D(u_{i},v_{i})-1/n)\leq 1/(1-1/n)\leq 1+\epsilon
because
\mathrm{Dem}(\widetilde{F}_{\mathrm{dir}})\leq D
,
\mathrm{Dem}(\widetilde{F}_{\mathrm{dir}})=\tau-1/n
and
D
is an integral demand. Therefore, compared to
Lemma

7.10
, the congestion and total length slack in
Lemma

7.9
blow up by a factor of
(1+\epsilon)
.

The work and depth are the same as
Lemma

7.10

∎

7.4
Approximate Min-Total-Length Multi-Commodity Flows

7.15
.

Let
G
be an undirected graph with vertex lengths
\ell
and capacities
U
. Given an integral demand
D
, a flow value threshold
\tau
equal to either
1
or
|D|
, suppose there exists a feasible flow
F^{*}
partially routing
D
with
\mathrm{value}(F^{*})=\tau
. Given a precision parameter
1/\mathrm{poly}\log(n)\leq\epsilon\leq 1
, there is an algorithm that computes the edge representation of a flow
F
such that

•

\mathrm{Dem}(F)\leq D
and
\mathrm{value}(F)=\tau
,

•

F
has congestion
\hat{O}(1)
, and

•

\mathrm{totlen}(F)\leq(1+\epsilon)\mathrm{totlen}(F^{*})
for any feasible flow
F^{*}
partially routing
D
with
\mathrm{value}(F^{*})=\tau
.

The work and depth are
\hat{O}(mk)
and
\hat{O}(1)
respectively, where
k=|\mathrm{supp}(D)|
is the number of commodities.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon/100
. The algorithm is as follows.

1.

We apply
Corollary

5.3
on
G
to compute an LC-flow shortcut
H
of
G
with length slack
\lambda=1+\epsilon^{\prime}
, congestion slack
\kappa=\hat{O}(1)
and step
t=\hat{O}(1)
. Let
G_{\mathrm{sc}}=G\cup H
be the corresponding LC-flow shortcut graph.

2.

Next, apply
Lemma

7.9
on
G_{\mathrm{sc}}
with demand
D
, step bound
t
, flow value threshold
\tau
and precision parameter
\epsilon^{\prime}
. Let
F_{\mathrm{sc}}
be the output flow in
G_{\mathrm{sc}}
.

3.

Finally, use the algorithmic backward mapping from
Corollary

5.3
to map
F_{\mathrm{sc}}
back to a flow
F
in
G
, and
F
is the flow we desire.

To see the correctness, let
F^{*}
be an arbitrary feasible flow in
G
partially routing
D
with
\mathrm{value}(F^{*})=\tau
. In the first step, the forward mapping of
Corollary

5.3
shows that there exists a flow
F^{*}_{\mathrm{sc}}
in
G_{\mathrm{sc}}
routing
\mathrm{Dem}(F^{*})
with

\mathrm{cong}(F^{*}_{\mathrm{sc}})\leq 1,\ \mathrm{totlen}(F^{*}_{\mathrm{sc}})\leq(1+\epsilon^{\prime})\mathrm{totlen}(F^{*})\text{ and step }t=\hat{O}(1).

In the second step,
Lemma

7.9
guarantees that
\mathrm{Dem}(F_{\mathrm{sc}})\leq D
,
\mathrm{value}(F_{\mathrm{sc}})=\tau
, the flow
F_{\mathrm{sc}}
in
G_{\mathrm{sc}}
has

\mathrm{cong}(F_{\mathrm{sc}})\leq\tilde{O}(1/\epsilon)=\tilde{O}(1)\text{ and }\mathrm{totlen}(F_{\mathrm{sc}})\leq(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{5}\cdot\mathrm{totlen}(F_{\mathrm{sc}}^{*}).

Finally, in the third step, the backward mapping algorithm from
Corollary

5.3
guarantees that
F
routes
\mathrm{Dem}(F_{\mathrm{sc}})
and has

\mathrm{cong}(F)\leq\hat{O}(1)\cdot\mathrm{cong}(F_{\mathrm{sc}})\text{ and }\mathrm{totlen}(F)\leq\mathrm{totlen}(F_{\mathrm{sc}}).

Thus, we can conclude that
F
partially routing
D
,
\mathrm{value}(F)=\tau
and

\mathrm{cong}(F)\leq\hat{O}(1)\text{ and }\mathrm{totlen}(F)\leq(1+\epsilon^{\prime})^{6}\mathrm{totlen}(F^{*})\leq(1+\epsilon)\mathrm{totlen}(F^{*})

as desired.

Consider the work and depth. By
Corollary

5.3
, step 1 has work
\hat{O}(m)
and depth
\hat{O}(1)
. By
Lemma

7.9
and
t=\hat{O}(1)
, step 2 has work
\hat{O}(mk)
and depth
\hat{O}(1)
. By
Corollary

5.3
again, step 3 has work
\hat{O}(m+nk)
and depth
\hat{O}(1)
.

∎

7.5
Approximate Multi-Commodity Mincost Flows: Proof of
Theorem

7.3

See
7.3

Proof.

We first define the demand
D
and the flow value threshold
\tau
.

•

For the concurrent version, the demand
D
is given (with
|\mathrm{supp}(D)|=k
), and we set
\tau=|D|
.

•

For the non-concurrent version, we are given a set
S
of vertex pairs (with
|S|=k
), we define
D
by setting, for each
(u,v)
,
D(u,v)=1
if
(u,v)\in S
, otherwise
D(u,v)=0
. Set
\tau=1
.

We now use the flow boosting template
Lemma

7.7
with precision parameter

\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon/c

for some sufficiently large constant
c
. We let the convex set
{\cal F}
of flows collect all flows
F
in
G
routing
D
with
\mathrm{value}(F)\geq\tau
. To make sure that
{\cal F}
contains at least one feasible flow (so that it satisfies the condition of
Lemma

7.7
), we can simply work on the graph
G
with vertex capacities scaled up by
\tau
, and then scale down the output flow by
\tau
at the end.

Observe that we can use
Lemma

7.15
as the algorithm
{\cal A}
in the flow boosting template
Lemma

7.7
. Indeed, in graph
G
with the vertex length function
\ell
given by
Lemma

7.7
,
Lemma

7.15
will compute the edge representation of a flow
F\in{\cal F}
such that
\mathrm{totlen}(F)\leq(1+\epsilon^{\prime})\mathrm{totlen}(F^{*})
for any feasible flow
F^{*}
partially routing
D
with
\mathrm{value}(F^{*})=\tau
(so also for all such
F^{*}
with
\mathrm{value}(F^{*})\geq\tau
), i.e. for any capacity-respecting
F^{*}\in{\cal F}
. Furthermore, we have
\mathrm{cong}(F)\leq\hat{O}(1)
. Therefore, we set the length slack
s
and congestion slack
\kappa
of
Lemma

7.7
to be

s=1+\epsilon^{\prime}\text{ and }\kappa=\hat{O}(1)

The output of the flow boosting template
Lemma

7.7
is the edge representation of a flow
\bar{F}\in{\cal F}
(so
\mathrm{value}(F)\geq\tau
) and a scalar
\lambda\geq 0
satisfying the following.

1.

The flow
\lambda\bar{F}
is capacity-respecting and has cost at most
B
.

2.

Let
\lambda^{*}
be the maximum value such that there exists flow
F^{*}\in{\cal F}
where
\lambda^{*}F^{*}
is capacity-respecting with cost at most
B
(greedily we can assume
\mathrm{value}(F^{*})=\tau
). Then
\lambda\geq\frac{1-O(\epsilon^{\prime})}{s}\lambda^{*}=\frac{1-O(\epsilon^{\prime})}{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\lambda^{*}
.

Let
F=\lambda\bar{F}
be our final output.

Observe that in the concurrent version, the
\lambda
and
\lambda^{*}
above are exactly those in
Definition

7.1
, so the approximation factor is
\frac{1-O(\epsilon^{\prime})}{1+\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq 1+\epsilon
. In the non-concurrent version, the
\lambda
and
\lambda^{*}
above are the
\mathrm{value}(F)
and
\mathrm{value}(F^{*})
in
Definition

7.1
, so the approximation factor is also
1+\epsilon
.

The work and depth are
\hat{O}(mk)
and
\hat{O}(1)
by
Lemma

7.7
,
\kappa=\hat{O}(1)
and
Lemma

7.15
.
∎

8
Conclusions and Open Problems

In this work, we present a parallel
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate multi-commodity mincost flow algorithm with almost optimal work and depth, which applies to undirected graphs with capacities and costs on both edges and vertices. Our key technical contribution is devising an LC-flow shortcut with
1+\epsilon
length slack,
\hat{O}(1)
congestion slack, and
\hat{O}(1)
step, which has size
\hat{O}(n)
and can be computed in
\hat{O}(m)
work and
\hat{O}(1)
depth. Our work also leaves several interesting open problems.

Polylogarithmic Depth.

A natural and important open problem is to further reduce the depth from subpolynomial to polylogarithmic while retaining almost-optimal work or improving toward nearly-optimal work, even in the single-commodity setting. As we discussed in
Section

1
,
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate parallel algorithms with polylogarithmic depth and nearly-linear work are known for
shortest paths

[
Li20
,
ASZ20
,
RGH+22
]
and
(edge-capacitated) maxflows

[
AKL+24
]
. However, it remains unclear whether similar results (particularly polylogarithmic depth) can be achieved for the (edge-capacitated) mincost flow problem or vertex-capacitated maxflow problem.

Open Problem 8.1
.

Are there parallel
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate algorithms for either (edge-capacitated) mincost flows or vertex-capacitated maximum flows, with nearly-linear work and polylogarithmic depth?

Better LC-flow Shortcuts.

The second open problem is about the quality of the LC-flow shortcuts. Recall that LC-flow shortcuts have the following quality measures: length slack, congestion slack, step, and size. In our approach to obtaining approximate maximum/mincost flows using LC-flow shortcuts, the approximation depends on the length slack, the depth overhead depends on congestion slack and step, and the work overhead depends on congestion slack, step and size.

Therefore, it is interesting to optimize the trade-offs between these measures, particularly between congestion slack and step, even for existential constructions without efficient algorithms. Note that our construction shows the existence
8
8

8

Use existential length-constrained expander decompositions with optimal tradeoffs.
of LC-flow shortcuts with
1+\epsilon
length slack,
n^{O(1/k)}
congestion slack,
(1/\epsilon)^{\mathrm{poly}(k)}
step and
n^{1+O(1/k)}
size, for
\epsilon\leq 1
and
k\geq 1
. It would be interesting to reduce the dependence on
k
in the step bound to subexponential or even polynomial. We point out that such better step bounds are known for some weaker notions of shortcuts:

•

Shortcuts with only length control
[
Coh00
]
:
1+\epsilon
length slack,
(\frac{\log n}{\epsilon})^{O(\log k)}
step and
n^{1+O(1/k)}
size.

•

Flow shortcuts with only (edge-)congestion control (e.g., tree flow sparsifiers
[
HHR03
,
RST14
]
):
\tilde{O}(1)
congestion slack,
\tilde{O}(1)
step and
\tilde{O}(n)
size.

•

LC-flow shortcuts with edge-congestion control and large length slack
[
HHL+24
]
:
\mathrm{poly}(k)
length slack,
n^{O(1/k)}
congestion slack,
\mathrm{poly}(k)
step and
n^{1+O(1/k)}
size.

As stepping stones, the following simpler notions of flow shortcuts are also open to improved trade-offs. The first is LC-flow shortcuts with only
edge
-congestion control and
1+\epsilon
length slack, for which the best known trade-off is still achieved by our work. The second is flow shortcuts with vertex-congestion control (without length control), for which the best known tradeoff is
n^{O(1/k)}
congestion slack,
2^{O(k)}
step, and
\tilde{O}(n)
size, as shown implicitly
9
9

9

In fact,
[
HLSW25
]
presents LC-flow shortcuts with vertex-congestion control and large length slack.
in
[
HLSW25
]
.

Distributed Mincost Flows.

Parallel and distributed algorithms are closely related, and there have been many technical transfers between the two settings. For example,
[
RGH+22
]
studies the
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate shortest path problem in parallel and distributed settings simultaneously.

As we give the almost-optimal parallel algorithm for
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate mincost flow problem, a natural next step is to solve it almost-optimally in the distributed setting. We note that there has been distributed
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate (single-commodity) maxflow algorithms with almost-optimal round complexity in the
CONGEST
model
[
GKK+15
]
. However, analogous results for the min-cost flow problem remain unclear, though there have been some efforts on distributed exact min-cost flow in directed graphs
[
FGL+22
,
dV23
]
.

Open Problem 8.2
.

Is there distributed
(1+\epsilon)
-approximate mincost flow algorithm with almost-optimal round complexity in the
CONGEST
model?

We note that our approach may potentially transfer to the distributed setting, since the main building blocks, including length-constrained expander decompositions, sparse neighborhood cover, and approximate length-constrained maxflows, all admit efficient distributed algorithms.

Appendix A
Notations for Expander Decomposition Algorithm

We will prove
Theorem

4.1
in the appendix.
Theorem

4.1
involves an expander routing algorithm, which will be achieved by an expander witness. We define it as follows.

Routers and expander witness.

We will need the definition of routers as follows. Notice that the congestion is defined on the edges.

A.1
Routers.

Given an edge-capacitated graph
G=(V,E)
and a node-weighting
A
on
V
, we say that
G
is a
t
-step
\kappa
-router for
A
if every
A
-respecting demand can be routed in
G
by a
t
-step flow
f
such that for every edge
e\in E
we have
\sum_{e\in p}f(p)\leq\kappa\cdot U(e)
flow paths in
f
. In this case, we say
f
has
edge-congestion

\kappa
on
G
.

The following definition shows how to embed a router into a graph.

A.2
Graph Embedding.

An
h
-length edge-to-vertex embedding with congestion
\kappa
from an edge-capacitated graph
H
to
G
with vertex lengths and capacities where
V(H)=V(G)
, is an assignment of each edge
(u,v)\in E(H)
a flow
F_{u,v}
on
G
routing the demand
D(u,v)=U(u,v)
, such that
\sum_{e\in E(H)}F_{e}
has length at most
h
and congestion at most
\kappa
.

Length-Constrained Expansion Witnesses

Below, for node-weighting
A
and nodes
S
, we let
A_{S}
be
A
restricted to nodes in
S
.

A.3
Length-Constrained Expansion Witness.

A
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expansion witness for graph
G
with vertex capacities and lengths and node-weighting
A
consists of four numbers
s_{0},s_{1},\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1}
such that
s_{0}\cdot s_{1}\leq s,\kappa_{0}\cdot\kappa_{1}\leq 1/\phi
and the following:

•

Neighborhood Cover:
a neighborhood cover
\mathcal{N}
of
G
with covering radius
h
;

•

Routers:
an
s_{0}
-step
\kappa_{0}
-router
R_{S}
of
A_{S}
for each
S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathcal{N}
;

•

Embedding of Routers:
an
(h\cdot s_{1})
-length edge-to-vertex embedding of
\cup_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}}R_{S}
into
G
with congestion
\kappa_{1}
.

We can define a witnessed expander decomposition as follows.

A.4
Witnessed Length-Constrained (Vertex) Expander Decomposition.

Given a graph
G=(V,E)
, a
witnessed
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition
for a node-weighting
A
with length slack
s
and cut slack
\kappa
is an
h\cdot s
-length cut
C
of size at most
\kappa\cdot\phi|A|
such that
A
is
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expanding in
G-C
along with a
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expansion witness for
G-C
.

The following theorem is the main focus of this appendix. It shows an algorithm computing a length-constrained expander decomposition with a witness after applying the decomposition.

A.5
Witnessed Length-Constrained Expander Decompositions.

There is a randomized algorithm
\textsc{VertexLC-ED}(G,A,h,\phi,\epsilon)
that takes inputs an undirected graph
G
with vertex capacities and lengths, a node-weighting
A
, length bound
h\geq 1
, conductance
\phi\in(0,1)
and a parameter
\epsilon
satisfying
0<\epsilon<1
, outputs a witnessed
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition
C
for
G,A
with cut slackness
\kappa
where

\displaystyle\kappa=n^{O(\epsilon)}\qquad\qquad s=\exp(\exp(\frac{O(\log(1/\epsilon))}{\epsilon}))

The algorithm takes
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}
work and
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}
depth.

We will show that
Theorem

A.5
implies
Theorem

4.1
, so it suffices to prove
Theorem

A.5
in the appendix.

Proof of
Theorem

4.1
.

The first part (output an expander decomposition) directly follows from
Theorem

A.5
. We only need to prove an expander routing algorithm.

Suppose we are given a
h
-length
A
-respecting demand
D
on
G-C
, we will show how to route
D
with length
O(\exp(\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon))\cdot hs)
and congestion
n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}/\phi
. We assume
\epsilon=\omega(1/\log\log n)
as otherwise
s
becomes
\mathrm{poly}(n)
and there is no length bound, which makes this problem converge to normal expander routing.

Notice that from
Theorem

A.5
, we also get a witness defined in
Definition

A.3
. For each pair
(u,v)
such that
D(u,v)>0
, since
D
is
h
-length, the neighborhood cover
{\mathcal{N}}
contains a cluster
S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}
such that
u,v\in S
, and we build the demand on
R_{S}
by putting the same demand
D(u,v)
on
R_{S}
. We do this for every pair
(u,v)
, and get a demand
D_{S}
on
R_{S}
for every
S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}
so that
\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}}D_{S}=D
, and
D_{S}
is trivially
A_{S}
-respecting on
S
because
D
is
A
-respecting.

Now we can apply Theorem 7.1
[
HHL+24
]
for each
R_{S}
and
D_{S}
. For clarity, we restate Theorem 7.1 of
[
HHL+24
]
as follows.

A.6
Theorem 7.1 of
[
HHL+24
]
.

Given a
s_{0}
-step
\kappa_{0}
-router
R_{S}
for a node-weithting
A
, an
A
-respecting demand
D_{S}
and
\epsilon\in(\log^{-c}N,1)
for some sufficiently small constant
c
, one can compute a path-representation of flow
F
routing
D
on
R_{S}
with

•

edge congestion
n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}\cdot\kappa_{0}
and step
\exp(\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon))\cdot s_{0}
, and

•

|\mathrm{path}(F)|\leq(|E(R)|+|\mathrm{supp}(D)|)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}

in
(|E(R)|+|\mathrm{supp}(D)|)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(s_{0})\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}
work and
\mathrm{poly}(s_{0})\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}
depth.

We apply
Theorem

A.6
on
R_{S}
for every
S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}
to get a flow
F^{R}_{S}
in
R
routing
D_{S}
. To get a flow
F_{S}
in
G-C
routing
D_{S}
, we use the
embedding of routers
: for every flow path
P\in F^{R}_{S}
and edge
e\in E(P)
, we embed this edge into
G-C
with flow
F_{e}
of the same value as
P
, and concatenate all the flows
F_{e}
to get the flow
F_{P}
, and take the union of all
F_{P}
to get the flow
F_{S}
.

Quality of the returned flow.

We first analyze the length. According to
Theorem

A.6
, the flow
F^{R}_{S}
for every
S
has step
\exp(\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon))\cdot s_{0}
. Thus, each flow path in
F_{S}
is concatenated by
\exp(\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon))\cdot s_{0}
many flows of length
h\cdot s_{1}
. Thus,
F_{S}
has length at most
O(\exp(\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon))\cdot s_{0}\cdot h\cdot s_{1})=O(\exp(\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon))\cdot hs)
. To get the length
hs
stated in
Theorem

4.1
, it suffices to relax
s
and
\phi
a bit to
s=\exp\exp(\frac{O(\log(1/\epsilon))}{\epsilon})\cdot O(\exp(\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon))=\exp\exp(\frac{O(\log(1/\epsilon))}{\epsilon})
. Notice that
C
is still a valid
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition after relaxing (increasing)
s
and decreasing
\phi
by a constant according to the monotonicity
Lemma

A.7
.

Then, we analyze the congestion. Notice that

\mathrm{cong}(\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}}F_{S})\leq 2\mathrm{cong}(\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{P\in\mathrm{path}F^{R}_{S}}\sum_{e\in E(P)}F_{e})

notice that
F_{e}
is routing
U(e)
many flow from one end point of
e
to another. Moreover, according to
Theorem

A.6
, the value of the total amount of flow from one end point of
e
to another is at most
n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}\cdot\kappa_{0}\cdot U(e)
. Thus, after scaling the flow
\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}}F_{S}
down by
n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}\cdot\kappa_{0}
, it becomes a subflow of
\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{e\in E(R_{S})}F_{e}
, which according to the definition of witness, has congestion at most
\kappa_{1}
. The congestion of the original flow
\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}}F_{S}
is thus at most
n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}\cdot\kappa_{0}\cdot\kappa_{1}\leq n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}/\phi
.

Complexity.

Theorem

A.5
takes
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}
work and
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}
depth.

After getting the witness, we apply
Theorem

A.6
on each router
R_{S}
. Notice that
R_{S}
for all
S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}
are explicit outputs of algorithm
Theorem

A.5
, so the size of
\cup_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}}R_{S}
is at most the work, i.e.,
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}
. Each pair of demand in
D
contributes to at most one demand pair in
D_{S}
, which implies
\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}}\mathrm{supp}(D_{S})\leq\mathrm{supp}(D)
. Thus, According to
Theorem

A.6
, the routing takes in total

(\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}+|\mathrm{supp}(D)|)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(s_{0})\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}

work and
\mathrm{poly}(s_{0})\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}
depth.

For each flow path of
F^{R}_{S}
, we map the (at most
\exp(\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon))\cdot s_{0}
many) edges of the path to
G-C
of (at most
hs
many edges). According to the support flow size specified in
Theorem

A.6
, the total work of this step is

\exp(\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon))\cdot s_{0}\cdot hs\cdot(\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}+|\mathrm{supp}(D)|)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}

and depth
\widetilde{O}\left(1\right)
. By accumulate all the work above, we get the total work (remember that
\epsilon=\omega(1/\log\log n)
so that
n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}\geq\mathrm{poly}(s)

(m+|\mathrm{supp}(D)|)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}

and depth

\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon)}

∎

Organization.

The algorithm is similar to classical expander decomposition and is based on repeatedly cutting the most balanced sparse cut. We will define this concept in the length constraint setting in this section. In
Appendix

B
, we will show some useful properties of sparse cut that will be used in the analysis of the algorithm. In
Appendix

C
, we will give an algorithm for finding the (approximately) ‘most balanced’ sparse cut. In
Appendix

D
, we will apply the algorithm in
Appendix

C
repeatedly to finally prove
Theorem

4.1
.

We first state the basic fact about the monotonicity of length constraint cuts when
h
increases,
s
decreases, and
\phi
increases.

A.7
.

Fix a node-weighting
A
and a (moving cut)
C
on the vertex set
V
, for every two graphs
G,G^{\prime}
where length function of
G^{\prime}
is at most length function of
G
, and every
h^{\prime}=ch,2\leq s^{\prime}\leq s,\phi^{\prime}\geq 2\phi
where
c\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}
, we have the following monotonicity.

1.

If
C
is an
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut in
G
, then
2C
is an
(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})
-length
\phi^{\prime}
-sparse cut in
G^{\prime}
.

2.

DLSC_{G^{\prime},A}(\phi^{\prime},h^{\prime},s^{\prime})\geq DLSC_{G,A}(\phi,h,s)
.

3.

G^{\prime}
is
(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})
-length
\phi^{\prime}
-expanding implies
G
is
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expanding.

Proof.

Given
C
is an
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut, denote by
D
its witnessing demand that has the demand-size of
C
, we have
A_{(h,s)}(C)=|D|\geq|C|/\phi
and

\mathrm{leng}(P_{u,v},G+\ell_{C,hs})>hs

for any demand pairs
D(u,v)>0
.
We show in the following that
2C
also
h^{\prime}s^{\prime}
-separates the
h^{\prime}
-length demand
D
since
h^{\prime}\geq h
, which yields a sparsity upper bound of at most
2\phi
.
Namely, from
\mathrm{leng}(P_{u,v},G+\ell_{C,hs})>hs
, we know that the length increase along path
P_{u,v}
is at least
hs-h
.
With the assumption of
s\geq 2
, the length increase from
2C
is larger than
hs
.
We can further assume that
h^{\prime}s^{\prime}=c^{\prime}hs
where
c^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}
, and view
C
as an
(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})
-length moving cut with the same cut value but larger length increase on the graph.
Then for any graph
G^{\prime}
is a copy of
G
but with length function at most the one of
G
, we ahve

\mathrm{leng}(P_{u,v},G^{\prime}+\ell_{2C,h^{\prime}s^{\prime}})>h^{\prime}s^{\prime}
.
Thus the sparisty of the
(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})
-length moving cut
2C
in
G^{\prime}
is upper bounded by
2|C|/|D|\leq 2\phi
.
This immediately concludes the
item

1
, and implies the following ones.

Proof of
item

2
: For the second point, since
|D|=DLSC_{G,A}(\phi,h,s)
, and we have an
(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})
-length
\phi^{\prime}
-sparse cut
2C
also fully separates
D
, it shows that
DLSC_{G^{\prime},A}(\phi^{\prime},h^{\prime},s^{\prime})\geq|D|
.

Proof of
item

3
: For the last one, we show it by contraposition, suppose
G
is not
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expanding, then there exists an
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut
C
in
G
.
As shown above, we can construct an
(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})
-length
\phi^{\prime}
-sparse cut
2C
in
G^{\prime}
, which implies that
G^{\prime}
is not
(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})
-length
\phi^{\prime}
-expanding.
∎

Concepts of demand sizes.

We will define demand-size-largest sparse cut, which corresponds to the concept of ‘most balanced sparse cut’ in the classical setting, i.e., demand-size corresponds to the balance, as defined below.

A.8
(h,s)
-Separated Demand-Size.

For a graph
G
, an
h
-length cut
C
, and a node-weighting
A
, we define the
(h,s)
-length demand-size of
C
(with respect to
A
on
G
) as the size of the largest
A
-respecting
h
-length demand which is
(hs)
-separated by
C
. We denote this “demand-size” by
A_{G}(h,s,C)
. When
G
is clear from the context, we simply write
A(h,s,C)
.

A.9
Demand-Size-Largest Sparse Cut,
DLSC
.

For a graph
G
, a node weighting
A
, and an
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut
C
, we call
C
the demand-size-largest
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut if its demand-size is maximum among all
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cuts. We notate the size of this cut as

\displaystyle\text{DLSC}_{G,A}(\phi,h,s):=A_{G}(h,s,C)

When
G,A
are clear from the context, we omit them and simply write
\text{DLSC}(\phi,h,s)
.

A.10
Approximately Demand-Size-Largest Sparse Cut.

C
is an
\alpha
-approximate demand-size-largest
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut for a node-weighting
A
with length approximation
\alpha_{h}
, length slackness approximation
\alpha_{s}
and sparsity approximation
\alpha_{\phi}
if it is an
(\alpha_{h}\cdot h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut and we have

\displaystyle A_{(h,s)}(C)\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}\cdot\text{DLSC}(\phi/\alpha_{\phi},h/\alpha_{s},s\cdot\alpha_{s}).

Appendix B
Union of Sparse Vertex Moving Cut Sequence is Sparse

Formally, we consider a sequence of moving cuts, defined as follows.

B.1
Sequence of Moving Cuts.

Given graph
G=(V,E)
and node-weighting
A
, a sequence of moving cuts is

(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots)
.
Let
G-\sum_{j<i}C_{j}
denote the graph that is applied with cuts from
C_{1}
to
C_{i-1}
sequentially.
We refer to the
(h,s)
-length sparsity of
C_{i}
with respect to
A
in
G-\sum_{j<i}C_{j}
as its
(h,s)
-length sparsity in the sequence.
We say the sequence
(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots)
is
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse if each
C_{i}
is
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse in the sequence.

B.2
Sequence of Witnessing Demands.

Given graph
G=(V,E)
, a node-weighting
A
, and an
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse sequence of moving cuts
(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots)
, we define the sequence of witnessing demands as
\mathcal{D}=(D_{1},D_{2},\ldots)
such that
D_{i}
is an
h
-length
A
-respecting demand in
G-\sum_{j<i}C_{j}
and
\operatorname{spars}_{hs}(C_{i},D_{i})\leq\phi
.
Furthermore, we assume
D_{i}
is fully separated, i.e.,
\operatorname{sep}_{hs}(C_{i},D_{i})=|D_{i}|
.

Intuitively, witnessing demand
D_{i}
indicates close vertex pairs
hs
-separated by
C_{i}
.
In particular, if
D_{i}(u,v)>0
, then we have
\mathrm{dist}_{G-\sum_{j<i}C_{j}}(u,v)\leq h
but
\mathrm{dist}_{G-\sum_{j\leq i}C_{j}}(u,v)\geq hs
.

The main result we would like to show in this section is that the union of sparse length-constrained vertex moving cuts is still sparse:

B.3
Union of Sparse Vertex Moving Cuts is a Sparse Vertex Moving Cut.

Let
(C_{1},\ldots,C_{k})
be a sequence of moving cuts where
C_{i}
is an
(h,s)
-length
\phi_{i}
-sparse cut in
G-\sum_{j<i}C_{j}
w.r.t. node-weighting
A
. Then the moving cut
\sum_{i}C_{i}
is an
(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})
-length
\phi^{\prime}
-sparse cut w.r.t.
A
where
h^{\prime}=2h
,
s^{\prime}=\frac{(s-2)}{2}
and
\phi^{\prime}=s^{3}\cdot\log^{3}n\cdot n^{O(1/s)}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i}|C_{i}|}{\sum_{i}|C_{i}|/\phi_{i}}
.

B.1
Low Arboricity Demand Matching Graph via Parallel Greedy Spanners

We follow the idea by
[
HHT24
]
to construct a graph called demand matching graph.
It helps to construct an overall demand that witnesses the sparsity of the union of moving cuts in the sequence of moving cuts.
Given node-weighting
A
and demand
D
, we can scale them (arbitrarily close to being) integral.
We can thus assume, w.l.o.g., that
A
and
D
are integral.
Informally, the graph creates
A(v)
copies for each vertex
v
.

B.4
Demand Matching Graph
[
HHT24
]
.

Given a graph
G=(V,E)
with vertex capacity and length, a node-weighting
A
and
A
-respecting demands
\mathcal{D}=(D_{1},D_{2},\ldots)
, we denote
2A(v)
unique “copies” of
v
by
\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(v):=\{v_{i}:i\in\{1,2,\ldots,2A(v)\}\}
.
We further define the demand matching (multi)-graph
G(\mathcal{D})=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
with unit edge length as follows:

•

Vertices:

G(\mathcal{D})
has vertices
V^{\prime}=\bigsqcup_{v}\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(v)
.

•

Edges:
For each demand
D_{i}
, let
M_{i}
be any matching where the number of edges between
\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(u)
and
\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(v)
for each
u,v\in V
is
D_{i}(u,v)
. Then
E^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i}M_{i}
.

We note there always exists a matching
M_{i}
defined as above for each demand
D_{i}
since
D_{i}
is
A
-respecting and we create
2A(v)
unique “copies” of each vertex
v
.
Given a sequence of witnessing demands,
we can construct a corresponding demand matching graph as defined above.
The key property is that it has a low arboricity.

B.5
Bounded Arboricity of Demands of Sequence of Cuts
[
HHT24
]
.

Let
(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots)
be a sequence of
(h,s)
-length cuts with witnessing demands
\mathcal{D}=(D_{1},D_{2},\ldots)
. Then
G(\mathcal{D})
has arboricity at most
s^{3}\cdot\log^{3}N\cdot N^{O(1/s)}
.

Proof.

We say that a sequence
(E_{1},E_{2},\ldots,E_{k})
of edge sets on
V
is
s
-
pg
(abbreviation for
s
-parallel greedy) for some integer
s\geq 2
, iff for each
1\leq i\leq k
,

•

set
E_{i}
is a matching on
V
; and

•

if we denote by
G_{i-1}
the graph on
V
induced by edges in
\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq i-1}E_{j}
, then for every edge
(u,v)\in E_{i}
,
d_{G_{i-1}}(u,v)>s
.

Equivalently, a sequence
(E_{1},\ldots,E_{k})
is
s
-
pg
iff for each
1\leq i\leq k
, every cycle in
G_{i}
of length at most
s+1
contains at least two edges in
E_{i}
.
We say that a graph
G
is
s
-
pg
iff its edge set
E(G)
is the union of some
s
-
pg
sequence on
V(G)
.
[
HHT23
]
proves that every
s
-
pg
graph on
n
vertices has arboricity
s^{3}\cdot\log^{3}n\cdot n^{O(1/s)}
.

We now show that this fact implies
Lemma

B.5
by showing that
G(\mathcal{D})
is a
s
-
pg
graph.
Specifically, given witnessing demands
\mathcal{D}=(D_{1},D_{2},\ldots,D_{k})
, we would like to show that the reversed sequence
(M_{k},M_{k-1},\ldots,M_{1})
is
s
-
pg
.

For each
i
, let
G_{i}(\mathcal{D})
be the graph induced by edges in
\bigcup_{k-i+1\leq j\leq k}M_{k}
.
Consider an edge in
M_{k-i}
, the matching corresponding to demand
D_{k-i}
.
By definition, it suffices to show that for each
(u,v)\in M_{k-i}
, there is no length-at-most-
s
path in
G_{i}(\mathcal{D})
containing
u,v
.
Assume for contradiction that there exists a path
P=(u,x_{1},\ldots,x_{s-1},v)
in
G_{i}(\mathcal{D})
.
This means that in graph
G-\sum_{1\leq j\leq k-i}C_{j}
, every pair in
(u,x_{1}),(x_{1},x_{2}),\ldots,(x_{s-1},v)
is at distance at most
h
. By triangle inequality, this implies that the distance between
u,v
in
G-\sum_{1\leq j\leq k-i}C_{j}
is less than
hs
. However, as the moving cut
C_{k-i}
separates all pairs in
D_{k-i}
to distance more than
hs
, as an edge in
M_{k-i}
,
u
and
v
should be at distance more than
hs
in
G-\sum_{1\leq j\leq k-i}C_{j}
, a contradiction.
∎

B.2
Matching-Dispersed Demand

We describe how to construct the witness demand for the union of moving cuts from the demand matching graph constructed previously.

B.6
Tree Matching Demand
[
HHT24
]
.

Given tree
T=(V,E)
we define the tree-matching demand on
T
as follows. Root
T
arbitrarily. For each vertex
v
with children
C_{v}
do the following. If
|C_{v}|
is odd let
U_{v}=C_{v}\cup\{v\}
, otherwise let
U_{v}=C_{v}
. Let
M_{v}
be an arbitrary perfect matching on
U_{v}
and define the demand associated with
v
as

\displaystyle D_{v}(u_{1},u_{2})=D_{v}(u_{2},u_{1}):=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $\{u_{1},u_{2}\}\in M_{v}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

where each edge in
M_{v}
has an arbitrary canonical
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
. Then, the tree matching demand for
T
is defined as

\displaystyle D_{T}:=\sum_{v\text{ internal in }T}D_{v}

We observe that a tree matching demand has size equal to the input size (up to constants).

B.7
.

Let
T
be a tree with
n
edges. Then
|D_{T}|\geq n
.

We can construct a matching-dispersed demand from a forest cover by applying the tree matching demand to each tree and scaling down by the arboricity.

B.8
Matching-Dispersed Demand
[
HHT24
]
.

Given graph
G
, node-weighting
A
and demands
\mathcal{D}=(D_{1},D_{2},\ldots)
, let
G(\mathcal{D})
be the demand matching graph (
Definition

B.4
), let
T_{1},T_{2},\ldots
be the trees of a minimum size forest cover with
\alpha
forests of
G(\mathcal{D})
(
Definition

B.4
) and let
D_{T_{1}},D_{T_{2}},\ldots
be the corresponding tree matching demands (
Definition

B.6
). Then, the matching-dispersed demand on nodes
u,v\in V
is

\displaystyle\mathrm{MD}_{\mathcal{D},A}(u,v):=\frac{1}{4\alpha}\cdot\sum_{i}\sum_{u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(u)}\sum_{v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(v)}D_{T_{i}}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})

We begin with a simple helper lemma that observes that the matching-dispersed demand has size essentially equal to the input demands (up to the arboricity).

B.9
[
HHT24
]
.

Given graph
G
, node-weighting
A
and and demands
\mathcal{D}=(D_{1},D_{2},\ldots)
where
G(\mathcal{D})
has arboricity
\alpha
, we have that the matching-dispersed demand
\mathrm{MD}_{\mathcal{D},A}
satisfies
|\mathrm{MD}_{\mathcal{D},A}|\geq\frac{1}{4\alpha}\sum_{i}|D_{i}|
.

Proof.

Observe that the number of edges in
G(\mathcal{D})
is exactly
\sum_{i}|D_{i}|
and so summing over each tree
T_{j}
in our forest cover and applying
Lemma

B.7
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{j}|D_{T_{j}}|\geq\sum_{i}|D_{i}|

Combining this with the definition of
\mathrm{MD}_{\mathcal{D},A}
(
Definition

B.8
) gives the claim.
∎

B.3
Sparsity Proof for Union of Sparse Vertex Moving Cut

We now argue the key properties of the matching-dispersed demand which will allow us to argue that it can be used as a witnessing demand for
\sum_{i}D_{i}
.

B.10
Properties of Matching-Dispersed Demand
[
HHT24
]
.

Given graph
G=(V,E)
and node-weighting
A
, let
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
be a sequence of moving cuts where
C_{i}
is
(h,s)
-length
\phi_{i}
-sparse in
G-\sum_{j<i}C_{i}
w.r.t.
A
with witnessing demands
\mathcal{D}=(D_{1},D_{2},\ldots)
. Then the matching dispersed demand
\mathrm{MD}_{\mathcal{D},A}
is:

1.

a
2h
-length
A
-respecting demand;

2.

h\cdot(s-2)
-separated by
\sum_{i}C_{i}
and;

3.

of size
|\mathrm{MD}_{\mathcal{D},A}|\geq\frac{1}{s^{3}\cdot\log^{3}N\cdot N^{O(1/s)}}\sum_{i}\frac{|C_{i}|}{\phi_{i}}
.

Proof.

We show the each property separatedly.

Proof of
Item

1
.
For a pair of vertices
u
and
v
, we note
\mathrm{MD}_{\mathcal{D},A}(u,v)>0
only if there is a path consisting of at most two edges between a node in
\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(u)
and a node in
\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(v)
in the demand matching graph
G(\mathcal{D})
.
Furthermore,
u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(u)
and
v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(v)
is connected by an edge in
G(\mathcal{D})
only if there is some witness demand
D_{i}
such that
D_{i}(u,v)>0
.
Since each
D_{i}
is
h
-length, we have
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)\leq h
, and thus by triangle inequality
\mathrm{MD}_{\mathcal{D},A}(u,v)
is
2h
-length in
G
.

To see that
\mathrm{MD}_{\mathcal{D},A}(u,v)
is
A
-respecting, we observe that each vertex in
G(\mathcal{D})
is incident to at most
2\alpha
matchings across all of the tree matching demands.
Thus for any
u\in V
since
|\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(u)|=2A(u)
we have

\sum_{u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(u)}\sum_{j}\sum_{v}\sum_{v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(v)}D_{T_{j}}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\leq\sum_{u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(u)}2\alpha\leq 4\alpha\cdot A(u).

Then for any
u\in V
in matching-dispersed demand we have,

\sum_{v}\mathrm{MD}_{\mathcal{D},A}(u,v)=\sum_{v}\frac{1}{4\alpha}\sum_{j}\sum_{u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(u)}\sum_{v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(v)}D_{T_{j}}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\leq A(u)

A symmetric argument shows that
\sum_{v}\mathrm{MD}_{\mathcal{D},A}(v,u)\leq A(u)
, and so we have that
\mathrm{MD}_{\mathcal{D},A}
is
A
-respecting.

Proof of
Item

2
.
Consider an arbitrary pair of vertices
u
and
v
such that
\mathrm{MD}_{\mathcal{D},A}(u,v)>0
; it suffices to argue that
\sum_{i}C_{i}
indeed
h(s-2)
-separates
u
and
v
.
Given
\mathrm{MD}_{\mathcal{D},A}(u,v)>0
,
if
u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(u)
,
v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(v)
and
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
is an edge in
G(\mathcal{D})
, then we know some
D_{i}
has value between
u
and
v
and
C_{i}

hs
-separates them.

Otherwise, there is a path
(u^{\prime},w^{\prime},v^{\prime})
in
G(\mathcal{D})
where
u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(u)
,
v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(v)
and for some
w\in V
we have
w^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Copies}_{A}(w)
.
It shows that there exists some demand
D_{i}
and
D_{j}
that has value between
\{u,w\}
and
\{w,v\}
respectively.
By definition of
G(\mathcal{D})
(
Definition

B.4
), each
D_{i}
corresponds to a different matching in
G(\mathcal{D})
and so since
\{u^{\prime},w^{\prime}\}
and
\{w^{\prime},v^{\prime}\}
share the vertex
w^{\prime}
, we may assume
i\neq j
and without loss of generality that
i<j
.
Let
G_{\leq i}
be
G
with
\sum_{l\leq i}C_{l}
applied.

Since
D_{i}
is
hs
-separated by
C_{\leq i}
, we know that

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{G_{\leq i}}(u,w)\geq hs.

(2)

On the other hand, since
D_{j}
is an
h
-length demand, and
j>i
, we know that the distance between
w
and
v
in
G_{\leq i}
is

\displaystyle d_{G_{\leq i}}(w,v)\leq h.

(3)

Thus, it follows that
C_{\leq i}
must
h(s-2)
separate
u
and
v
since otherwise we would know that
d_{G_{\leq i}}(u,w)\leq h(s-2)
and so combining this with
Equation

3
and the triangle inequality we get
d_{G_{\leq i}}(u,w)\leq hs-h
, contradicting
Equation

2
. Thus,
\sum_{i}C_{i}
must
h(s-2)
vertices
u
and
v
.

Proof of
Item

3
.
By
Lemma

B.9
, we know that
|\mathrm{MD}_{\mathcal{D},A}|\geq\frac{1}{4\alpha}\sum_{i}|D_{i}|
where
\alpha
is the arboricity of
G(\mathcal{D})
.
From
Lemma

B.5
, we have a bound of
s^{3}\cdot\log^{3}N\cdot N^{O(1/s)}
on the arboricity of
G(\mathcal{D})
.
Further, each
D_{i}
is a witnessing demand for a
\phi_{i}
-sparse cut
C_{i}
such that
|D_{i}|\geq\frac{|C_{i}|}{\phi_{i}}
,
we conclude

|\mathrm{MD}_{\mathcal{D},A}|\geq\frac{1}{4\cdot s^{3}\cdot\log^{3}N\cdot N^{O(1/s)}}\sum_{i}\frac{|C_{i}|}{\phi_{i}}

∎

We conclude with the proof of
Theorem

B.3
:
See
B.3

Proof.

Let
\widehat{C}
be the union of sparse cuts and defined as
\widehat{C}=\sum_{i}C_{i}
.
Recall that to demonstrate that
\widehat{C}
is a
\phi^{\prime}
-sparse
(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})
-length sparse cut, it suffices to argue that there exists an
h^{\prime}
-length
A
-respecting demand
D
that is
h^{\prime}s^{\prime}
-separated by
\widehat{C}
where
|D|\geq\frac{|\widehat{C}|}{\phi^{\prime}}
.

Lemma

B.10
demonstrates the existence of exactly such a demand—namely the matching dispersed demand denoted by
D
as defined in
Definition

B.8
—for
h^{\prime}=2h
,
s^{\prime}=\frac{(s-2)}{2}
and
\phi^{\prime}=s^{3}\cdot\log^{3}N\cdot N^{O(1/s)}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i}|C_{i}|}{\sum_{i}|C_{i}|/\phi_{i}}
.
∎

B.11
.

We note that when taking the union of sparse cuts, we scale the cut value of
\widehat{C}
to be the multiple of
1/h^{\prime}s^{\prime}
such that the length increase remains the same as
\sum_{i}C_{i}
.
This gives
|\widehat{C}|=\frac{s}{s-2}\cdot\sum_{i}|C_{i}|
.
For simplicity of presentation, we take
|\widehat{C}|=\sum_{i}|C_{i}|
whenever the
\frac{s}{s-2}
factor can be hidden in the
O(\cdot)
notation.

B.4
Application: Demand-Size-Largest Sequence of Sparse Cuts

B.12
DLSCS
.

Fix a graph
G
, node-weighting
A
and parameters
h
,
s
and
\phi
.
\operatorname{DLSCS}
is the demand-size of the demand-size-largest sequence of
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse moving cuts. Specifically,

\displaystyle\operatorname{DLSCS}(\phi,h,s):=\sum_{i}A_{(h,s)}(C_{i})

where above
A_{(h,s)}(C_{i})
is computed after applying all
C_{j}
for
j<i
and
(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots)
is the
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expanding moving cut sequence maximizing
\sum_{i}A_{(h,s)}(C_{i})
.

B.13
.

Given graph
G
and node-weighting
A
, we have that

\displaystyle\operatorname{DLSCS}(\phi,h,s)\leq s^{3}\cdot\log^{3}n\cdot n^{O(1/s)}\cdot\operatorname{DLSC}(\phi^{\prime},h^{\prime},s^{\prime})

where
h^{\prime}=2h
,
s^{\prime}=\frac{(s-2)}{2}
and
\phi^{\prime}=s^{3}\cdot\log^{3}n\cdot n^{O(1/s)}\cdot\phi
.

Proof.

Let
(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots)
be a sequence of
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cuts such that
\sum_{i}A_{(h,s)}(C_{i})=\text{DLSCS}(\phi,h,s)
where
\phi_{i}\leq\phi
is the minimum value for which
C_{i}
is
\phi_{i}
-sparse. Let
D_{1},D_{2},\ldots
be the demands witnessing these cuts so that for all
i
we have

\displaystyle|C_{i}|/\phi_{i}=|D_{i}|.

Let
\widehat{C}=\sum_{i}C_{i}
where
|\widehat{C}|=\sum_{i}|C_{i}|
.
By
Theorem

B.3
we know that
\widehat{C}
is an
(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})
-length
\phi^{\prime}
-sparse cut for
A
.
What is more, we can further tighten the sparsity of
\widehat{C}
by each
\phi_{i}
, and we have

\operatorname{spars}_{(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})}(\widehat{C},A)=s^{3}\cdot\log^{3}n\cdot n^{O(1/s)}\cdot\frac{\sum_{i}|C_{i}|}{\sum_{i}|C_{i}|/\phi_{i}},

and so there must be some
h^{\prime}
-length demand
D
which is
h^{\prime}s^{\prime}
-separated by
\widehat{C}
, and we further have

\displaystyle s^{3}\cdot\log^{3}n\cdot n^{O(1/s)}\cdot\frac{|\widehat{C}|}{\operatorname{spars}_{(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})}(\widehat{C},A)}=|\widehat{C}|\cdot\frac{\sum_{i}|C_{i}|/\phi_{i}}{\sum_{i}|C_{i}|}=\sum_{i}|D_{i}|.

In other words, the demand-size of
\widehat{C}
multiplied by a sparsity slack is at least
\sum_{i}|D_{i}|
, as required.
∎

B.5
Application: Demand-Size Largest Sparse Cut at Most Largest Expanders’ Complement

B.14
Largest Expander’s Complement Size.

\text{LEC}(\phi,h,s)
is
\phi
times the size of the complement of the largest
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expanding subset of
A
. That is, let
\hat{A}
be the
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expanding node-weighting on
G
satisfying
\hat{A}\preceq A
with largest size and let
\bar{A}=A-\hat{A}
be its complement. Then

\displaystyle\text{LEC}(\phi,h,s):=\phi\cdot|\bar{A}|.

Observe that applying our previous relations we can get a simple lower bound on the demand-size of the largest-demand-size length-constrained sparse cut.

B.15
.

Given graph
G
and node-weighting
A
and parameters
h,s,\phi
, we have that

\displaystyle\text{DLSC}(\phi,h,s)\leq(1/\phi)\cdot\text{LEC}(\phi^{\prime},h^{\prime},s)

where
\phi^{\prime}=\tilde{O}(\phi\cdot s^{3}\cdot n^{O(1/s)})
and
h^{\prime}=2h
.

B.5.1
Largest Weighted Sparse Cut Sequence At Most Largest Cut

B.16
Largest Weighted Sparse Cut Sequence Size.

\text{LWSC}(\phi,h,s)
is the largest weighted size of a sparse cut sequence, namely

\displaystyle\text{LWSC}(\phi,h,s):=\sum_{i}\frac{\phi}{\operatorname{spars}_{(h,s)}(C_{i},A)}\cdot|C_{i}|

where
(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots)
is the
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expanding moving cut sequence maximizing
\sum_{i}\frac{\phi}{\operatorname{spars}_{(h,s)}(C_{i},A)}\cdot|C_{i}|
and each
\operatorname{spars}_{(h,s)}(C_{i},A)
is computed after applying
C_{j}
for
j<i
.

B.17
Largest Sparse Cut Size.

\text{LC}(\phi,h,s)
is the size of the largest
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut in
G
w.r.t.
A
. That is

\displaystyle\text{LC}(\phi,h,s):=|C_{0}|

where
C_{0}
is the moving cut of largest size in the set
\{C:\operatorname{spars}_{(h,s)}(C,A)\leq\phi\}
.

B.18
.

Given graph
G
and node-weighting
A
, we have that

\displaystyle\text{LWSC}(\phi,h,s)\leq\text{LC}(\phi^{\prime},h^{\prime},s^{\prime})

where
\phi^{\prime}=\phi\cdot s^{3}\cdot\log^{3}n\cdot n^{O(1/s)}
,
h^{\prime}=2h
and
s^{\prime}=\frac{(s-2)}{2}
.

Proof.

Let
(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots)
be the
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse moving cut sequence w.r.t
A
in
G
maximizing the weighted cut sequence size as defined in
Definition

B.16
.
Similarily take
\widehat{C}=\sum_{i}C_{i}
where
|\widehat{C}|=\sum_{i}|C_{i}|
.
By
Theorem

B.3
we know that
\widehat{C}
is an
(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})
-length
\phi^{\prime}
-sparse cut for
A
.
Naturally, the weighted cut sequence size is related to the size of the union of cuts.
We note that,

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\frac{\phi}{\operatorname{spars}_{(h,s)}(C_{i},A)}\cdot|C_{i}|

\displaystyle=\phi\cdot\sum_{i}|C_{i}|/\operatorname{spars}_{(h,s)}(C_{i},A)

\displaystyle=\phi\cdot\frac{\sum_{i}|C_{i}|/\operatorname{spars}_{(h,s)}(C_{i},A)}{\sum_{i}|C_{i}|}\cdot|\widehat{C}|

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi^{\prime}}{\operatorname{spars}_{(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})}(\widehat{C},A)}\cdot|\widehat{C}|.

Then it suffices to argue that there exists some
(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})
-length
\phi^{\prime}
-sparse moving cut
C^{\prime}
can achieve the above size.
The basic idea to get
C^{\prime}
is to simply add arbitrary length increases to
\widehat{C}
such that the size increases while
C^{\prime}
remains to be
\phi^{\prime}
-sparse.
Namely, based on
\widehat{C}
, we arbitrarily increase the cut value to get an
(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})
-length moving cut
C^{\prime}
where
|C^{\prime}|=\frac{\phi^{\prime}}{\operatorname{spars}_{(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})}(\widehat{C},A)}\cdot|\widehat{C}|
.
We note that
C^{\prime}
also
h^{\prime}s^{\prime}
-separates the witness demand
D
of cut
\widehat{C}
, thus

\operatorname{spars}_{(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})}(C^{\prime},A)\leq\frac{|C^{\prime}|}{|D|}=\frac{\phi^{\prime}\cdot|\widehat{C}|}{|D|\cdot\operatorname{spars}_{(h^{\prime},s^{\prime})}(\widehat{C},A)}=\phi^{\prime}

.
This concludes the lemma.
∎

B.5.2
Largest Cut At Most Largest Expander’s Complement

B.19
.

Given graph
G
and node-weighting
A
and parameters
h,s,\phi
, we have that

\displaystyle\text{LC}(\phi,h,s)\leq\text{LEC}(\phi^{\prime},h,s)

where
\phi^{\prime}=3\phi
.

Intuitively, if there exists a large expanding sub-node-weighting, then any sparse cut would have relatively small size, otherwise it contradicts the fact that no sparse cut can cut too much into an expanding sub-node-weighting.
To formally show the proof of above lemma, we first formalize the idea with projected down demand
[
HHT24
]
.

Projected Down Demands.

B.20
Projected Down Demand
[
HHT24
]
.

Suppose we are given graph
G
, node-weighting
A
,
A
-respecting demand
D
and
\hat{A}\preceq A
where
\bar{A}:=A-\hat{A}
is the complement of
\hat{A}
. Then, let
D^{+}
be any demand such that
\sum_{v}D^{+}(u,v)=\min(\bar{A}(u),\sum_{v}D(u,v))
for every
u
and
D^{+}\preceq D
. Symmetrically, let
D^{-}
be any demand such that
\sum_{v}D(v,u)=\min(\bar{A}(u),\sum_{v}D(v,u))
and
D^{-}\preceq D
. Then we define the demand
D
projected down to
\hat{A}
on
(u,v)
as

\displaystyle D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}(u,v):=\max(0,D(u,v)-D^{+}(u,v)-D^{-}(u,v)).

The following establishes the basic properties of
D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}
.

B.21
.

Given graph
G
, node-weighting
A
,
\hat{A}\preceq A
where
\bar{A}:=A-\hat{A}
, we have that
D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}
is
\hat{A}
-respecting,
|D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}|\geq|D|-2|\bar{A}|
and
D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}\preceq D
.

Proof.

We do a case analysis by the
minimizer of
\min(\bar{A}(u),\sum_{v}D(u,v))
.

•

If
\sum_{v}D^{+}(u,v)=\bar{A}(u)
(where
D^{+}
is defined in
Definition

B.20
) then by the fact that
D
is
A
-respecting we have

\displaystyle\sum_{v}D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{v}D(u,v)-\sum_{v}D^{+}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq A(v)-\bar{A}(u)

\displaystyle=\hat{A}(u).

•

On the other hand, if
\sum_{v}D^{+}(u,v)=\sum_{v}D(u,v)
then by the non-negativity of node-weightings we have

\displaystyle\sum_{v}D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{v}D(u,v)-\sum_{v}D^{+}(u,v)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle=\hat{A}(u).

In either case we have
\sum_{v}D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}(u,v)\leq\hat{A}(u)
. A symmetric argument using
D^{-}
(where
D^{-}
is defined in
Definition

B.20
) shows that
\sum_{v}D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}(v,u)\leq\hat{A}(u)
and so
D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}(u,v)
is
\hat{A}
-respecting.

To see that
|D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}|\geq|D|-2|\bar{A}|
, observe that by definition,
|D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}|\geq|D|-|D^{+}|-|D^{-}|
. But, also by definition,
|D^{+}|,|D^{-}|\leq|\bar{A}|
, giving the claim. Lastly, observe that
D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}\preceq D
trivially by construction.
∎

We can now formally prove the main result of this section.
See
B.19

Proof.

Let
C_{0}
be the
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut of largest size w.r.t.
A
in
G
and let
\bar{A}
be the complement of the largest
(h,s)
-length
\phi^{\prime}
-expanding subset
\hat{A}\preceq A
as in
Definition

B.14
.

Let
D
be the demand that witnesses the
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparsity of
C_{0}
; that is, it is the minimizing
A
-respecting demand in
Definition

3.8
. We may assume, without loss of generality, that
C_{0}

hs
-separates all of
D
; that is,
\operatorname{sep}_{hs}(C_{0},D)=|D|
. Let
D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}
be the projected down demand (as in
Definition

B.20
). Recall that by
Lemma

B.21
we know that
D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}
is
\hat{A}
-respecting,
|D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}|\geq|D|-2|\bar{A}|
and
D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}\preceq D
.

However, since
C_{0}

hs
-separates all of
D
and
D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}\preceq D
we know that
C_{0}
must
hs
-separate all of
D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}
and so applying this and
|D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}|\geq|D|-2|\bar{A}|
we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{spars}_{s\cdot h}(C_{0},D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}})=\frac{|C_{0}|}{\operatorname{sep}(C_{0},D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}})}=\frac{|C_{0}|}{|D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}|}\leq\frac{|C_{0}|}{|D|-2|\bar{A}|}

(4)

where, as a reminder,
\operatorname{spars}
is defined in
Definition

3.7
and
Definition

3.8
.

On the other hand, since
D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}}
is
\hat{A}
-respecting and
\hat{A}
is
(h,s)
-length
\phi^{\prime}
-expanding by definition, we know that no cut can be too sparse w.r.t.
\hat{A}
and, in particular, we know that

\displaystyle 3\phi=\phi^{\prime}\leq\operatorname{spars}_{(h,s)}(C_{0},\hat{A})\leq\operatorname{spars}_{s\cdot h}(C_{0},D^{\downharpoonright\hat{A}})

(5)

Combining
Equation

4
and
Equation

5
and solving for
\phi^{\prime}\cdot|\bar{A}|
we have

\displaystyle\frac{3\phi\cdot|D|-|C_{0}|}{2}\leq\phi^{\prime}\cdot|\bar{A}|.

However, recall that
C_{0}
is an
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut witnessed by
D
and, in particular, this means that
\frac{1}{\phi}|C_{0}|\geq|D|
. Applying this we conclude that

\displaystyle|C_{0}|\leq\phi^{\prime}\cdot|\bar{A}|.

as required.
∎

B.5.3
Proof of
Lemma

B.15
.

With
Lemmas

B.18
and
B.19
as shown above, it is rather straight forward to prove
Lemma

B.15
.
See
B.15

Proof.

Let
C
be the
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut of largest demand-size.
Observe that
C
is also a candidate for the largest weighted sparse cut sequence, and thus

\frac{\phi}{\operatorname{spars}_{(h,s)}(C,A)}\cdot|C|=\phi\cdot\text{DLSC}(\phi,h,s)\leq\text{LWSC}(\phi,h,s)

Further, by
Lemma

B.18
we have

\displaystyle\text{LWSC}(\phi,h,s)\leq\text{LC}(\phi^{\prime},h^{\prime},s^{\prime})

where
\phi^{\prime}=\tilde{O}(\phi\cdot s^{3}\cdot n^{O(1/s)})
,
h^{\prime}=2h
and
s^{\prime}=(s-2)/2
. Lastly, by
Lemma

B.19
we have

\displaystyle\text{LC}(\phi^{\prime},h^{\prime},s^{\prime})\leq\text{LEC}(3\phi^{\prime},h^{\prime},s^{\prime}).

Combining the above and observing that
\text{LEC}(3\phi^{\prime},h^{\prime},s^{\prime})\leq\text{LEC}(3\phi^{\prime},h^{\prime},s)
gives the lemma.
∎

Appendix C
Approximating Demand-Size-Largest Sparse Cuts (DLSC)

In this section, we will prove the following lemma.

C.1
.

There exists an algorithm
\textsc{ApxDLSC}{}(G,A,h,s,\phi,\epsilon)
that is given a
vertex-capacitated graph

G=(V,E,\ell,u)
, a length slackness
s>2
, a length bound
h>s^{c/\epsilon}
for some sufficiently large constant
c
, a conductance parameter
\phi\in(0,1)
, a parameter
\epsilon\in(1/\log^{0.1}n,1)
, either output an empty cut, or computes an
\alpha
-approximate demand-size-largest
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut with sparsity approximation
\alpha_{\phi}
, length slackness approximation
\alpha_{s}
where

\alpha=s^{3}\cdot n^{O(\epsilon+\frac{1}{s})}\qquad\qquad\alpha_{\phi}=s^{3}\cdot n^{O(\epsilon+\frac{1}{s})}\qquad\qquad\alpha_{s}=s^{O(1/\epsilon)}

In the case when the algorithm outputs an empty cut, it also outputs a
(h/s^{O(1/\epsilon)},s/\epsilon)
-length
\phi/n^{O(\epsilon)}
-expansion witness for
G
.

The algorithm uses
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot m\cdot n^{f(\epsilon)}
work and
\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{f(\epsilon)}
depth, where
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}f(\epsilon)\to 0
.

To recall the definition of approximately demand-size-largest sparse cut, we restate
Definition

A.10
as follows.

See
A.10

The idea to prove
lemma

C.1
is to use the constant-hop cut-matching game introduced by
[
HHG25
]
, which we will explain in the next section.

C.1
Constant Steps Cut Matching Games

The cut-matching game in the literature was used to find sparse cuts in the classical setting where the cut strategy simply returns a bi-partition of the vertex set. This classical cut-matching game will generate an expander with polylogarithmic hops, which is too much for our case where the hop corresponds to the length slackness, and we want the length slackness to be a constant. Thus,
[
HHG25
]
proposed a generalized cut-matching game where instead of simply bi-partitioning the vertex set, they do multiple partitions (in our case partition the node-weighting). This generalized version allows them to achieve constant hops, We define them formally as follows.

Definition.

In this section, only for the definition of a cut-matching game, we will forget about the vertex capacity and length and only consider edge-capacitated graphs
G=(V,E)
where each edge
e\in E
is represented as
(\text{id}_{e},\text{capacity}_{e})
, i.e., associated with a capacity. We define
U:E\to{\mathbb{N}}^{+}
as the edge capacity function. This is because the cut-matching game was only defined with congestion on edges, which also suffices for our case (this should be clear in the next section).

Cut strategies.

A cut strategy is an algorithm that is given an undirected graph
G=(V,E)
and a node-weighting
A
, outputs a set of node-weightings pairs
\{(A^{(j)},B^{(j)})\}_{j}
satisfying

1.

for every
j
we have
|A^{(j)}|=|B^{(j)}|
, and
A^{(j)}+B^{(j)}\preceq A
,

2.

A\preceq\sum_{j}(A^{(j)}+B^{(j)})
.

Matching strategies.

A matching strategy is an algorithm that is given a graph
G
and node-weighting pairs
\{(A^{(j)},B^{(j)})\}_{j}
, outputs for each
j
a set of capacitated edges
M^{(j)}\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(A^{(j)})\times\mathrm{supp}(B^{(j)})
such that for every vertex
u
we have
U(\delta_{M^{(j)}}(u))\leq A^{(j)}(u)
and
U(\delta_{M^{(j)}}(u))\leq B^{(j)}(u)
.

Cut-matching games.

A cut-matching game is an algorithm that is given a cut strategy and matching strategy, starts from an empty edge-capacitated graph
G_{0}=(V,E_{0}=\emptyset)
and runs in a certain number of rounds (denoted by
r
) to update
G_{0}
. In the
i
-the round (starting from
i=1
), the algorithm first applies the cut strategy on the graph
G_{i-1}
and gets a set of node-weighting pairs
\{(A^{(j)}_{i},B^{(j)}_{i})\}_{j}
. Then the algorithm applies the matching strategy on
\{(A^{(j)}_{i},B^{(j)}_{i})\}_{j}
to get
\{M^{(j)}_{i}\}_{j}
and get the graph
G_{i}=(V,E_{i})
where
E_{i}=E_{i-1}\bigcup\left(\cup_{j}M^{(j)}_{i}\right)
. The resulting graph of a cut-matching game is
G_{r}
. We care about the following parameters of a cut-matching game.

•

Rounds of Interaction:
The number of rounds
r
.

•

Cut Batch Size:
The maximum number of pairs the cut strategy plays in each round of interaction, i.e.,
\max_{i}|\{(A_{i}^{(j)},B_{i}^{(j)})\}_{j}|
. The classical cut matching games can be viewed as the special case when the cut batch size is
1
. We need larger cut batch size in order to achieve constant hop.

•

Matching Perfectness:
If each set of edges the matching player plays for a batch always has a total capacity of at least a
1-\alpha
fraction of the total node-weighting then we say that the cut matching game is
(1-\alpha)
-perfect. That is, a cut matching game is
(1-\alpha)
-perfect if for every
i
we have

\sum_{j}U\left(M_{i}^{(j)}\right)\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot\sum_{j}|A_{i}^{(j)}|=(1-\alpha)\cdot\sum_{j}|B_{i}^{(j)}|

In
[
HHT24
]
, they proved a high-quality cut strategy. By combining Theorem 1.1 and Theorem 13.6 of
[
HHT24
]
, we get the following lemma.

C.2
Cut strategy,
[
HHT24
]
.

For every
\epsilon\in\left(0,1\right)
(possibly a function of
n
), there is a cut strategy with cut batch size
n^{O(\epsilon)}
which when used in a cut matching game with
1/\epsilon
rounds of interaction and an arbitrary
(1-\alpha)
-perfect matching strategy results in a
G_{r}
that is a
1/\epsilon
-step and
n^{O(\epsilon)}
-router (see
Definition

A.1
) for some
A^{\prime}\preceq A
of size
|A^{\prime}|\geq(1-O(\frac{\alpha}{\epsilon}))\cdot|A|
.
This cut-strategy costs work
m\cdot n^{f(\epsilon)}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)
and depth
n^{f(\epsilon)}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)
where
f(\epsilon)\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

In order to prove
Lemma

C.1
, i.e., find an approximately demand-size-largest sparse cut of
G
, we will use cut-matching games to construct a graph
H
on the same vertex set but with a different edge set. Specifically, we will use the cut strategy provided by
Lemma

C.2
to construct
H
. The high-level idea is to find a
(1-\alpha)
-perfect matching after each round of cut strategy and add them to
H
so that we can use
Lemma

C.2
to argue that
H
becomes a good router in the end; moreover, we will make sure that each edge in
H
(i.e., in each matching) corresponds to a path in the original graph
G
, such that these path are vertex length bounded with low vertex congestion. For that purpose, the matching player can be viewed as routing as many as possible demands in the original graph with length bound and low congestion, and each successfully routed demand corresponds to an edge in
H
. The following lemma implied by
[
HHS23
]
is for that purpose.

C.3
Application of Theorem 16.1
[
HHS23
]
.

There is an algorithm that is given a graph
G=(V,E)
with vertex capacities and length, two node-weightings
A,B\subseteq V
, length bound
h\geq 1
and sparsity
0<\phi<1
, outputs a pair of flow and moving cut
(F,C)
such that

1.

F
has vertex congestion
\widetilde{O}\left(1/\phi\right)
, vertex length
h
and routes some
\hat{A}\preceq A
to
\tilde{B}\preceq B
.

2.

C
is a
h
-length vertex moving cut,
\{v\mid\hat{A}(v)<A(v)\}
and
\{v\mid\tilde{B}(v)<B(v)\}
are
h
-far on
G-C
, and
C
has size at most

|C|\leq\phi\cdot\left(|A|-\operatorname{val}(F)\right)

The algorithm runs in
\widetilde{O}\left(m\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\right)
work and
\widetilde{O}\left(\mathrm{poly}(h)\right)
depth.

Proof.

We first apply standard transformation from vertex capacity to directed edge capacity in order to apply Theorem 16.1 of
[
HHS23
]
. We construct a
directed graph
with edge capacities and length
G^{\prime}=(V_{in}\cup V_{out}\cup\{s,t\},E^{\prime})
defined as follows.

•

V_{in},V_{out}
are both copies of
V
, we write
v^{in},v^{out}
for the corresponding copies for
v\in V
.

•

E^{\prime}=\{(v^{in},v^{out})\mid v\in V\}\cup\{(u^{out},v^{in}\mid(u,v)\in E\}\cup\{(s,u^{in}),(v^{out},t)\mid u\in\mathrm{supp}(A),v\in\mathrm{supp}(B)\}
.

•

Each edge
(v^{in},v^{out})
get capacity
U(v)
and length
\ell(v)
; each edge
(u^{out},v^{in})
get sufficiently large capacity, for example,
10\max_{v\in V}U(v)
, and length
1
10
10

10

Actually we want the length to be
0
here, but in
[
HHS23
]
, they have the assumption that lengths are at least 1
; each edge
(s,u^{in})
gets capacity
A(u)
and length
1
, each edge
(v^{out},t)
gets capacity
B(v)
and length
1
.

We now apply Theorem 16.1 of
[
HHS23
]
with parameters
S=\{s\},T=\{t\}
and
h,\phi
to be
3\cdot\text{(our length bound h)},(\text{our sparsity }\phi)/3
in
Lemma

C.3
. We will get
(\hat{f},\hat{w})
in
\widetilde{O}\left(m\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\right)
work and
\widetilde{O}\left(\mathrm{poly}(h)\right)
depth.

Getting
F
.

We transfer
\hat{f}
into a flow in
F
in
G
in the following way. Every flow path
p\in\hat{f}
must be like
(s,v_{1}^{in},v_{1}^{out},v_{2}^{in},v_{2}^{out},...,v^{in}_{k},v_{k}^{out},t)
for some sequence
(v_{1},...,v_{k})
due to the definition of
G^{\prime}
. We let
(v_{1},...,v_{k})
be the corresponding flow in
F
if
(s,v_{1}^{in})
is saturated by
\hat{f}
. In this way, as long as
\hat{f}
has edge-congestion
\widetilde{O}\left(1/\phi\right)
(as implied by Theorem 16.1 of
[
HHS23
]
), each vertex
v
cannot have vertex congestion more than
\widetilde{O}\left(1/\phi\right)\cdot U(v)
in
F
since each of them corresponds to an edge
v^{in},v^{out}
used in
\hat{f}
. Thus,
F
has vertex-congestion at most
\widetilde{O}\left(1/\phi\right)
. Moreover,
F
routes some
\hat{A}\preceq A
to
\tilde{B}\preceq B
. So
F
is a valid output.

Getting
C
.

For each
v\in V
, we assign the moving cut
C(v)
as

C(v)/3=\hat{w}(v^{in},v^{out})+\left(\sum_{(v,u)\in E}\hat{w}(v^{out},u^{in})\right)+\hat{w}(s,v^{in})

The last term only exists when
v\in\mathrm{supp}(A)
. Clearly, we have

|C|\leq 3\cdot\sum_{v\in V}C(v)\cdot U(v)\leq 3\cdot\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}}\hat{w}(e)\cdot U(e)\leq\phi\cdot\left(\left(\sum_{(s,v^{in})\in E^{\prime}}U(s,v^{in})\right)-\operatorname{val}(\hat{f})\right)

Notice that the last inequality is from Theorem 16.1 of
[
HHS23
]
. According to the definition of
G^{\prime}
, we have
\sum_{(s,v^{in})\in E^{\prime}}U(s,v^{in})=|A|
, and
\operatorname{val}(\hat{f})=\operatorname{val}(F)
, so
|C|\leq\phi\cdot\left(|A|-\operatorname{val}(F)\right)
.

It remains to verify
\{v\mid\hat{A}(v)<A(v)\}
and
\{v\mid\tilde{B}(v)<B(v)\}
are
h
-far on
G-C
. According to Theorem 16.1 of
[
HHS23
]
, the distance of
s,t
in
G^{\prime}
is at least
3h
under the distance function where (i) all edges
(s,v^{in})
or
(t,v^{out})
that are saturated by
\hat{f}
has distance
3h+1
, (ii) all other edges
e
has distance in
G^{\prime}
increased by
\hat{w}(e)\cdot 3h
. Suppose there is a path
p_{G}
in
G
from
a\in\{v\mid\hat{A}(v)<A(v)\}
to
b\in\{v\mid\tilde{B}(v)<B(v)\}
with distance less than
h
in
G-C
, this corresponds to a path in
G^{\prime}
denoted as
p^{\prime}_{G}=(s,a^{in},a^{out},...,b^{in},b^{out},t)
where
(s,a^{in}),(b^{out},t)
are both not saturated by
\hat{f}
. The edge length of
p
, according to the definition of
C
, is at most

\ell(p^{\prime}_{G})\leq(|P|+1)+\ell_{C,h}(P_{G})<3h

The first term
(|P|+1)
is from the fact that we set the initial length of every edge to be at least
1
. The second term is because every length increases on edges by
\hat{w}
is absorbed by the same length increase on its adjacent vertex by
C
. This is a contradiction that
s,t
in
G^{\prime}
is at least
3h
-far.
∎

C.2
Approximating DLSC from Cut-Matching Games (Proof of
Lemma

C.1
)

This section is adapted from Section 13.2 of
[
HHT24
]
, with the simplification that we do not need to guarantee the cut strategy to work on a smaller node-weighting (for their paper they need this property to do recursion while gradually reducing the node-weighting size), and we do not do
(\leq h,s)
-length decomposition as they do.

In this section, we show how to combine the cut and matching strategy in
Lemmas

C.2
and
C.3
to get a cut-matching game that can prove
Lemma

C.1
. Intuitively, since a length-constraint expander is a low-step expander under every low-diameter cluster, we are going to apply our cut-matching game on every low-diameter cluster. Thus,
Theorem

3.2
will be used to get a collection of low-diameter clusters to apply cut-matching games. We describe our algorithm formally as follows.

Step 1: Create Clusters for Cut Matching Games.

Apply
Theorem

3.2
to
G
to compute a neighborhood cover
{\mathcal{N}}
with separation factor
2s
, covering radius
h_{\mathrm{cov}}=h/\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\cdot(2s)^{O(1/\epsilon)}\right)=h/s^{O(1/\epsilon)}
(because
s>2
), cluster diameter
h_{\mathrm{diam}}=h
and width
\omega=n^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot\log n
.

Step 2: Run Cut Matching Games.

First, let

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}:=\phi/n^{O(\epsilon)}

(6)

be the (relaxed) sparsity with respect to which we will run our cut-matching game.

Next, we do the following for each
{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}
. Intuitively, we wish to run a cut-matching game on every cluster in
{\mathcal{S}}
which is a low-diameter cluster. We do so by running the cut player for each of them simultaneously and running the matching player by combining the source and sink node-weighting from all of them: difference clusters will not interfere with each other because they are far away from each other. Formally, we initialize empty graph
H_{S}=(S,\emptyset)
for every
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
and we repeat the following from
i=1
to
1/\epsilon
.

1.

Run cut strategies:
For each
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
, apply the the cut strategy (from
Lemma

C.2
) to
H_{S}
. Let
\{A^{(j)}_{i,S},B^{(j)}_{i,S}\}_{j\in[b]}
be the output pairs of node-weightings from the cut strategy for cluster
S
. Here we fix
b
to be an upper bound for the cut batch size for all cut strategies over
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
, which satisfies
b=n^{O(\epsilon)}
according to
Lemma

C.2
. For the cut strategy that returns less than
b
pairs of node weightings, we simply augment it to
b
pairs by appending empty node weightings to them.

2.

Compute a cutmatch:
For each
j\in[b]
, we apply
Lemma

C.3
on the graph
G
, two node-weightings
A^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}:=\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}A^{(j)}_{i,S},B^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}:=\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}B^{(j)}_{i,S}
, length bound
s\cdot h_{\mathrm{diam}}
and sparsity
\phi^{\prime}
, to get a flow and moving cut pair
(F^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}},C^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}})
. Define
F_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}=\cup_{j}F^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}

3.

Update graphs:
For each
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
and each
j\in[b]
, add a matching
M^{(j)}_{i,S}\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(A_{i,S}^{(j)})\times\mathrm{supp}(B_{i,S}^{(j)})
to
H_{S}
defined as follows:
(a,b)\in M^{(j)}_{i,S}
if there is a flow path in
F_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
from
a
to
b
, and the capacity of this edge
(a,b)
is the summation of
F_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}(p)
among all flow path
p
from
a
to
b
.

Algorithm Output.

We return as our cut
C^{*}
the union of all cuts among all
C^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
for
j\in[b],i\in[1/\epsilon]
and
{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}
. If
C^{*}
is empty, then we return a witness:

•

Neighborhood Cover:
we return the neighborhood cover
{\mathcal{N}}
constructed in Step 1, it has covering radius
h/s^{O(1/\epsilon)}
;

•

Routers:
an
s_{0}
-step
\kappa_{0}
-router of
A_{S}
for each
S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathcal{N}
, for which we return the final graph
H_{S}
in the cut-matching game. Notice that
s_{0}=1/\epsilon
and
\kappa_{0}=n^{O(\epsilon)}
according to
Lemma

C.4
.

•

Embedding of Routers:
an
(h_{\mathrm{cov}}\cdot s_{1})
-length edge-to-vertex embedding of
\cup_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}}R_{S}
into
G
with congestion
\kappa_{1}
. We take the embedding as in Step 2 (3): every edge in
H_{S}=R_{S}
corresponds to a flow path in
F_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
for some
i,{\mathcal{S}}
. According to
Lemma

C.3
, these flow path has length at most
h_{\mathrm{diam}}\cdot s
. For the congestion, notice that for each
j\in[b]
and
i\in[1/\epsilon]
, the flow
F^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
has congestion
\widetilde{O}\left(1/\phi^{\prime}\right)
according to
Lemma

C.3
. Thus, the total congestion is at most
n^{O(\epsilon)}/\phi
.
Notice that
s_{1}=s
and
\kappa_{1}=n^{O(\epsilon)}/\phi
follows from the argument above.

C.4
.

If
C^{*}
is an empty cut, then
H_{S}
is a
1/\epsilon
-step
n^{O(\epsilon)}
-router.

Proof.

As
C^{*}
is an empty cut, every cut
C^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
is an empty cu. According to
Lemma

C.2
, that means
\hat{A}^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}},\hat{B}^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
must be equal to
A^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}},B^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
(here
\hat{A},\hat{B}
are the vertices saturated by the flow), otherwise there exists
a\in\mathrm{supp}(\hat{A}^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}),b\in\mathrm{supp}(\hat{B}^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}})
such that
a,b
are
h_{\mathrm{diam}}\cdot s
-far. In this case, we must have
a,b
in different clusters
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
. In other words, if we look at the cluster
S
that contains
a
, we have that all vertices in
B^{(j)}_{i,S}
are saturated. Notice that
|A^{(j)}_{i,S}|=|B^{(j)}_{i,S}|
, so there must be a flow from
A^{(j)}_{i,S}
to
B^{(j)}_{i,S^{\prime}}
for some
S^{\prime}\not=S
. This is a contradiction as
S,S^{\prime}
are at least
h_{\mathrm{diam}}\cdot s
far, but the flow has length at most
h_{\mathrm{diam}}\cdot s
.

Thus, we must have
\hat{A}^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}},\hat{B}^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
equal to
A^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}},B^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
for every
i,j,{\mathcal{S}}
. This means the matching perfectness is
1
. According to
Lemma

C.2
,
H_{S}
finally is a router for
A_{S}
with
1/\epsilon
step and
n^{O(\epsilon)}
congestion.

∎

Witness.

Suppose
C^{*}
is empty, then the algorithm does not output
C^{*}
, but an expander witness. The correctness follows from
Lemma

C.4
and the above arguments.

Proof of
Lemma

C.1
.

Now we assume
C^{*}
is not empty and we will prove that the algorithm returns an approximately demand-size-largest sparse cut.

We first show the complexity argument.

Complexity.

Step 1 uses
Theorem

3.2
, which costs
h\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
depth and
h\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot m
work.

Step 2 consists of
1/\epsilon
iterations. For each iteration, there are three steps.

Firstly, it simultaneously runs cut strategies for all
S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}
. Notice that according to
Theorem

3.2
the total number of vertices and edges for all
S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}
are at most
n^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot n
and
n^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot m
because each vertex in
V
is included in at most
n^{O(\epsilon)}
many different
S
. Thus, according to
Lemma

C.2
, all the cut-strategies in total uses depth
n^{f(\epsilon)}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)
and work
m\cdot n^{f(\epsilon)}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)
where
f
is a function that goes to
0
as
\epsilon
goes to
0
.

Secondly, it simultaneously compute a cutmatch for each
j\in[b]
and
{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}
by applying
Lemma

C.3
, in total costs
\widetilde{O}\left(m\cdot\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}\right)
work and
\widetilde{O}\left(\mathrm{poly}(h)\right)
depth.

Thirdly, it updates the graphs, which is straightforward by using the computed flow, so the work and depth are subsumed by other operations. The output is by union of all the computed cut, so the cost is subsumed by other costs.

Correctness.

We now proceed to prove the correctness.

The intuition for the correctness are as follows. According to
Lemma

B.13
, i.e., demand-size largest sparse cut (DLSC) is roughly at most the largest expander’s complement size (LEC). Recall from
Definition

B.14
that largest expander’s complement size (LED) is roughly less than
A-\hat{A}
for every expanding node-weighting
\hat{A}\preceq A
. Thus, our plan is to construct a large expanding node-weighting to serve as an upper bound for DLSC, and then the demand-size of the returned cut
C^{*}
will not be too smaller than that value because of the guarantee from
Lemma

C.3
.

For every
S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}
, let the matching perfectness of the cut-matching game on
H_{S}
be
(1-\alpha_{S})
. In other words, for every iteration
i
, we have
\sum_{j}U\left(M^{(j)}_{i,S}\right)\geq(1-\alpha_{S})\cdot\sum_{j}|A^{(j)}_{i,S}|
. Moreover, there exists an iteration
i^{*}_{S}
such that
\sum_{j}U\left(M^{(j)}_{i^{*}_{S},S}\right)=(1-\alpha_{S})\cdot\sum_{j}|A^{(j)}_{i^{*}_{S},S}|
.

Correctness: Constructing an expanding node-weighting.

According to
Lemma

C.2
, for every
S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}
, there exists a node-weighting
A^{\prime}_{S}\preceq A_{S}
(here
A_{S}
is the node-weighting
A
restricting to
S
) with
|A^{\prime}_{S}|\geq(1-O(\frac{\alpha_{S}}{\epsilon}))\cdot|A|
such that
H_{S}
in the end becomes a
1/\epsilon
-step and
n^{O(\epsilon)}
-router for
A^{\prime}_{S}
. We define
\hat{A}
in the following way. For every node
v\in V
, if there exists
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
A^{\prime}_{S}(v)\leq A_{S}(v)/2
, let
\hat{A}(v)=0
, otherwise let
\hat{A}(v)=A_{S}(v)
. Clearly, we have
\hat{A}\preceq A
. We will show that
\hat{A}
is expanding by using
Theorem

3.11
.

C.5
.

\hat{A}
is
(h_{\mathrm{cov}},s^{O(1/\epsilon)})
-length
\phi/n^{O(\epsilon)}
-expanding on
G
.

Proof.

According to the second point of
Theorem

3.11
, we only need to show that, for every
h_{cov}
-length demand
D
that is
\hat{A}
respecting, we can route
D
on
G
with congestion
n^{O(\epsilon)}/\phi
and length (dilation) at most
h_{\mathrm{cov}}\cdot s^{O(1/\epsilon)}
.

For every positive demand
D(u,v)>0
, since
\mathrm{dist}_{G}(u,v)\leq h_{\mathrm{cov}}
, according to the definition of neighborhood cover, there exists
S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}
such that both
u,v
are in
S
. Moreover, since
D
is
\hat{A}
respecting, we have
A^{\prime}_{S}(v)\geq A_{S}(v)/2
. We route the demand
D(u,v)
by using the
1/\epsilon
-step
n^{O(\epsilon)}
-router
H_{S}
with an additional congestion factor of at most
2
(because
D(u,v)
can be as large as
A_{S}(v)
but in the router we can only have
A^{\prime}_{S}(v)
). We will get a routing path on
H_{S}
from
u
to
v
and we need to map it back to the original graph
G
, this can be done by mapping each edge in
H_{S}
to a path in
G
: remember that each edge in
H_{S}
corresponds to a flow in step (update graphs), which according to the step (compute a cutmatch), has length bound
s\cdot h_{\mathrm{diam}}=s^{O(1/\epsilon)}\cdot h
. This gives the length bound.

To see the total congestion, notice that every edge in
H_{S}
is used at most
n^{O(\epsilon)}
times, where edges in
H_{S}
can be decomposed into at most
(1/\epsilon)\cdot b=(1/\epsilon)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}=n^{O(\epsilon)}
(remember that
\epsilon>\log^{0.1}n
) matchings, each matching can be mapped to flow paths of the original graph
G
with vertex congestion
\widetilde{O}\left(1/\phi^{\prime}\right)=n^{O(\epsilon)}/\phi
according to the usage of
Lemma

C.3
. Thus, routing in a single
S
cause a total vertex congestion of
n^{O(\epsilon)}/\phi
in
G
. For a single
{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}
, it is easy to see that different routings in difference
S\in{\mathcal{S}}
do not share vertices with each other: every two clusters in
{\mathcal{S}}
are
2s\cdot h_{\mathrm{diam}}
-far, and remember that every flow path for the routing is
s\cdot h_{diam}
-length bounded. So we can route all the demands contributed by
{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}
in vertex congestion of
n^{O(\epsilon)}/\phi
. Notice that
|{\mathcal{N}}|
is the width of the neighborhood cover, bounded by
n^{O(\epsilon)}
, so the total vertex congestion is
n^{O(\epsilon)}/\phi
.
∎

According to the definition of LEC
Definition

B.14
, we get the following inequality from
Lemma

C.5
.

LEC(\phi/n^{O(\epsilon)},h_{\mathrm{cov}},s^{O(1/\epsilon)})\leq\left(\phi/n^{O(\epsilon)}\right)\cdot(|A|-|\hat{A}|)

By using
Lemma

B.15
, we can get an upper bound for DLSC.
