DLSC\left(\phi/\left(s^{3}\cdot n^{O(\epsilon+1/s)}\right),h_{\mathrm{cov}}/2,s^{O(1/\epsilon)}\right)\leq\left(s^{3}\cdot n^{O(\epsilon+1/s)}\right)\cdot(|A|-|\hat{A}|)

(7)

Next, we will show the cut returned has demand-size close to
(|A|-|\hat{A}|)
.

Correctness: Returned cut is approximately demand-size-largest sparse.

We first show that the returned cut
C^{*}
is sparse. In the proof we will define a demand
D
to show the sparsity of
C^{*}
.
D
will also be used later to bound the demand size of
C^{*}
.

C.6
.

If
C^{*}
is not empty,
C^{*}
is a
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut with respect to the node-weighting
A
.

Proof.

Recall that
C^{*}
is the union of cuts
C^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
for
j\in[b],i\in[1/\epsilon]
and
{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}
, and
C^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
is computed from
Lemma

C.3
which is guaranteed to satisfy

|C^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}|\leq\phi^{\prime}\cdot\left(|A^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}|-\operatorname{val}(F^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}})\right)

Moreover, suppose
F^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
routes the demand
\tilde{A}^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}\preceq A^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
to
\tilde{B}^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}\preceq B^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
. Then, the two vertex sets which are not saturated by
F^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
, i.e.,
V_{1}=\{v\mid\tilde{A}^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}(v)<A^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}(v)\}
and
V_{2}=\{v\mid\tilde{B}^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}(v)<B^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}(v)\}
, they are
s\cdot h_{\mathrm{diam}}
-far. Thus, we can define a demand
D^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
from
V_{1}
to
V_{2}
that when combined with the node-weighting saturated by
F^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
, becomes the full node-weighting
A^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
.
D^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
is an
h_{\mathrm{diam}}
-length demand separated by
C^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
and thus
C^{*}
, with separation length
s\cdot h_{\mathrm{diam}}=s\cdot h
and demand size
\left(|A^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}|-\operatorname{val}(F^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}})\right)
. We define the demand
D
as the union of all demands
D^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}
scale down by
n^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot(1/\epsilon)=n^{O(\epsilon)}
(to make sure it is
A
respecting, because
i
can iterate over
1/\epsilon
choices and
j,{\mathcal{S}}
can iterate over
n^{O(\epsilon)}
choices). According to the definition of sparse cut
Definition

3.8
, we can give the sparsity bound of
C^{*}
as (remember
h_{\mathrm{diam}}=h
)

\displaystyle\operatorname{spars}_{(h,s)}(C^{*},A)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|C^{*}|}{|D|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sum_{i,j,{\mathcal{S}}}|C^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}|}{\sum_{i,j,{\mathcal{S}}}|D^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}|}\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\leq\phi^{\prime}\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\leq\phi

The third inequality comes from the fact that
|C^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}|/|D^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}|=|C^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}|/\left(|A^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}|-\operatorname{val}(F^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}})\right)\leq\phi^{\prime}
for all
i,j,{\mathcal{S}}
from
Lemma

C.3
.
∎

Then we bound the demand-size of
C^{*}
. From the proof of
Lemma

C.6
, we have already shown that
D
is separated by
C^{*}
by length
h\cdot s
.
D
is
A
-respecting
h
-length, and has size

|D|=\frac{1}{n^{O(\epsilon)}}\cdot\sum_{i,j,{\mathcal{S}}}|D^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}|=\frac{1}{n^{O(\epsilon)}}\cdot\sum_{i,j,{\mathcal{S}}}\left(\left(\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}|A^{(j)}_{i,S}|\right)-\operatorname{val}(F^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}})\right)

(8)

Recall the definition of
\hat{A}
: a vertex
v
has
\hat{A}(v)<A(v)
only happens when
\hat{A}(v)=0
and there exists some
S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}
such that
A^{\prime}_{S}(v)\leq A_{S}(v)/2
. The definition of
A^{\prime}_{S}
is from
Lemma

C.2
, i.e., an expanding sub-node-weighting of
A_{S}
which has the size guarantee to be at least
(1-\frac{O(\alpha_{S})}{\epsilon})|A_{S}|
where
\alpha_{S}
is the matching perfectness on the cut-matching game of
S
. Thus, every
v
with
\hat{A}(v)<A(v)
contributes to the difference
\frac{O(\alpha_{S})}{\epsilon}|A_{S}|
between
|A_{S}|
and
|A^{\prime}_{S}|
for some
S
, so we have

|A|-|\hat{A}|\leq\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}}\frac{O(\alpha_{S})}{\epsilon}|A_{S}|

According to the definition of matching perfectness, there exists an iteration
i^{*}_{S}
for every
S
such that
\sum_{j}U\left(M^{(j)}_{i^{*}_{S},S}\right)=(1-\alpha_{S})\cdot\sum_{j}|A^{(j)}_{i^{*}_{S},S}|
, i.e., in that iteration we get

\alpha_{S}\cdot\sum_{j}|A^{(j)}_{i^{*}_{S},S}|=\sum_{j}|A^{(j)}_{i^{*}_{S},S}|-\sum_{j}U\left(M^{(j)}_{i^{*}_{S},S}\right)

(9)

Recall from the definition of cut strategy that
\sum_{j}|A^{(j)}_{i^{*}_{S},S}|\geq|A_{S}|/2
. We can take the sum over all
S
and all iterations
i
, which gives us

\displaystyle|A|-|\hat{A}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}}\frac{O(\alpha_{S})}{\epsilon}|A_{S}|

\displaystyle\leq O(\frac{1}{\epsilon})\cdot\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}}\alpha_{S}\cdot\sum_{j}|A^{(j)}_{i^{*}_{S},S}|

\displaystyle\leq^{\ref{eq:alphas}}O(\frac{1}{\epsilon})\cdot\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}}\left(\sum_{j}|A^{(j)}_{i^{*}_{S},S}|-\sum_{j}U\left(M^{(j)}_{i^{*}_{S},S}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq O(\frac{1}{\epsilon})\cdot\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{i}\left(\sum_{j}|A^{(j)}_{i,S}|-\sum_{j}U\left(M^{(j)}_{i,S}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq O(\frac{1}{\epsilon})\cdot\left(\sum_{i,j,S}|A^{(j)}_{i,S}|-\sum_{i,j,S}U\left(M^{(j)}_{i,S}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=O(\frac{1}{\epsilon})\cdot\left(\sum_{i,j,S}|A^{(j)}_{i,S}|-\sum_{i,j,{\mathcal{S}}}\operatorname{val}\left(F^{(j)}_{i,{\mathcal{S}}}\right)\right)

∎

We can compare the last line with the size of
D
as shown in
Equation

8
, and get

|A|-|\hat{A}|\leq\frac{n^{O(\epsilon)}}{\epsilon}|D|\leq n^{O(\epsilon)}\cdot|D|

Combine with
Equation

7
, we get

DLSC\left(\phi/\left(s^{3}\cdot n^{O(\epsilon+1/s)}\right),h_{\mathrm{cov}}/2,s^{O(1/\epsilon)}\right)\leq\left(s^{3}\cdot n^{O(\epsilon+1/s)}\right)\cdot|D|

Recall that
D
is
h
-length which is
hs
-far in
G-C^{*}
. Thus, we have that

A_{(h,s)}(C^{*})\geq|D|\geq\frac{1}{s^{3}\cdot n^{O(\epsilon+1/s)}}\cdot DLSC\left(\phi/\left(s^{3}\cdot n^{O(\epsilon+1/s)}\right),h/s^{O(1/\epsilon)},s^{O(1/\epsilon)}\right)

This proves the correctness of
Lemma

C.1
(remember that both
A_{(h,s)}(C)
and
DLSC
increases as
h
increases or
s
decreases by
Lemma

A.7
).

Appendix D
Expander Decomposition from Approximately DLSC

In this section, we will show how to get length-constraint expander decomposition by repeatedly calling and cutting approximately demand-size-largest sparse cut. We will prove the following theorem.

See
4.1

Notice that if
\epsilon
is too small, say less than
o(1/\log\log n)
, then the length slackness becomes an arbitrary large polynomial on
n
. In this case,
Theorem

4.1
is trivial since every cut cannot separate any demand as
h\cdot s
is too large, so
G
is an expander anyway. Thus, in what follows we assume
\epsilon=\Omega(1/\log\log n)
.

Recall that from
Lemma

C.1
and the definition
Definition

A.10
, we can use
\textsc{ApxDLSC}(G,A,h,s,\phi,\epsilon)
to get a
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut
C
such that the demand size is at least
\frac{1}{\alpha}
fraction of the largest demand size among all
(h/\alpha_{s},s\cdot\alpha_{s})
-length
\phi/\alpha_{\phi}
-sparse cut. Here the parameters
\alpha,\alpha_{s},\phi_{\phi}
depend on
n,\epsilon,s
can be found in
Lemma

C.1
, we state them here for convenience.

See
C.1

To recall the definition of approximate sparse cut, we restate the definition as follows.

See
A.10

Intuition.

The algorithm repeatedly finds and cuts the approximately demand-size-largest
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut until the demand size decreases by a factor of
1/n^{O(\epsilon)}
. We can prove that there will be not too many such cuts because of
Lemma

B.13
. For convenience, we restate the lemma as below.

See
B.13

The demand-size of a cut sequence is not too large than the demand-size of one cut. We call the above procedure an ‘epoch’. One can see that after this epoch, the demand-size-largest
(h/\alpha_{s},s\cdot\alpha_{s})
-length
\phi/\alpha_{\phi}
-sparse cut get reduced by a factor of
1/n^{O(\epsilon)}
. We update
h,s,\phi
to the corresponding new parameters and continue to the next epoch to further reduce the demand-size. After
O(1/\epsilon)
epochs, the graph will have no sparse cut and it becomes a good expander. The detailed algorithm is stated as follows.

Lemma

C.1
did not specify the constant hidden inside
O
for
\alpha,\alpha_{\phi},\alpha_{s}
. We use
c
in the algorithm to denote a sufficiently large constant that is larger than every constant hidden inside these
O
. The same holds for the constants hidden inside
Lemma

B.13
. This is to prevent potential confusion.

Algorithm 3

C\leftarrow\textsc{VertexLC-ED}(G,A,h,\phi)

1:

z\leftarrow n^{2c\sqrt{\epsilon}},\quad\ell_{0,z}\leftarrow 0

2:

h_{0}\leftarrow h\cdot(1/\epsilon)^{(3c/\epsilon)^{1/\sqrt{\epsilon}}},\quad s_{0}\leftarrow 1/\epsilon,\quad\phi_{0}\leftarrow\phi\cdot n^{5c\sqrt{\epsilon}}

3:

for

\mathsf{epoch}=1,\ldots,1/\sqrt{\epsilon}

do

4:

\ell_{\mathsf{epoch},0}\leftarrow\ell_{\mathsf{epoch}-1,z}

5:

for

j=1,\ldots,z

do

6:

C_{\mathsf{epoch},j}\leftarrow\textsc{ApxDLSC}(G_{\mathsf{epoch},j-1},A,h_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}},\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}},\epsilon)

7:

\ell_{\mathsf{epoch},j}\leftarrow\ell_{\mathsf{epoch},j-1}+h_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot C_{\mathsf{epoch},j}

8:

G_{\mathsf{epoch},j}\leftarrow G+\ell_{\mathsf{epoch},j}

9:

end

for

10:

h_{\mathsf{epoch}+1}\leftarrow h_{\mathsf{epoch}}/s_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{2c/\epsilon}

11:

s_{\mathsf{epoch}+1}\leftarrow s_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot s_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{2c/\epsilon}

12:

\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}+1}\leftarrow\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}/n^{5c\epsilon}

13:

end

for

14:

return

\ell_{1/\sqrt{\epsilon},z}/h

Proof of
Theorem

4.1
.

We use the algorithm as described in
Algorithm

3
. We first show the correctness. The correctness follows from the following lemma, describing the invariance throughout the algorithm.

D.1
.

At the end of the
\mathsf{epoch}
iteration, we have

DLSC_{G_{\mathsf{epoch},z}}\left(\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}+1}\cdot n^{2c\epsilon},2h_{\mathsf{epoch}+1},\frac{s_{\mathsf{epoch}+1}-2}{2}\right)\leq|A|\cdot\left(\frac{1}{n^{c\sqrt{\epsilon}}}\right)^{\mathsf{epoch}}

Proof.

We prove it by induction on
\mathsf{epoch}
. When
\mathsf{epoch}=0
, we view ‘the end of the
0
iteration’ as ‘the start of the
1
iteration’. The induction hypothesis holds because the demand-size of every cut is at most
|A|
according to the definition of demand-size of a cut
Definition

A.8
.

Suppose
\mathsf{epoch}\geq 1
. Suppose to the contrary, at the end of iteration
\mathsf{epoch}
, we still have
DLSC_{G_{\mathsf{epoch},z}}(\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}+1}\cdot n^{2c\epsilon},2h_{\mathsf{epoch}+1},(s_{\mathsf{epoch}+1}-2)/2)>|A|\cdot\left(1/n^{c\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)^{\mathsf{epoch}}
. Consider the
\mathsf{epoch}
iteration. Notice that according to the monotonicity
Lemma

A.7
, we have
DLSC_{G_{\mathsf{epoch},j}}(2\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}+1}\cdot n^{2c\epsilon},2h_{\mathsf{epoch}+1},(s_{\mathsf{epoch}+1}-2)/2)>|A|\cdot\left(1/n^{c\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)^{\mathsf{epoch}}
throughout the whole
\mathsf{epoch}
iteration since
G_{\mathsf{epoch},j}
only increases its length as
j
increases.
According to the output guarantee of
ApxDLSC

Lemma

C.1
, the demand size of
C_{\mathsf{epoch},j}
for every
j\in[z]
is at least (notice that
s_{\mathsf{epoch}}>1/\epsilon
and
s_{\mathsf{epoch}}<(1/\epsilon)^{(3c/\epsilon)^{1/\sqrt{\epsilon}}}<n^{\epsilon}
for sufficiently large
n
because of
\epsilon>\Omega(1/\log\log n)
)

\displaystyle A_{G_{\mathsf{epoch},j-1}}(h_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}},C_{\mathsf{epoch},j})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{s^{3}_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot n^{c\left(\epsilon+\frac{1}{s_{\mathsf{epoch}}}\right)}}\cdot DLSC_{G_{\mathsf{epoch},j-1}}\left(\frac{\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}}{s^{3}_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot n^{c\left(\epsilon+\frac{1}{s_{\mathsf{epoch}}}\right)}},\frac{h_{\mathsf{epoch}}}{s_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{c/\epsilon}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot s_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{c/\epsilon}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{3c\epsilon}}\cdot DLSC_{G_{\mathsf{epoch},j-1}}\left(\frac{\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}}{n^{3c\epsilon}},\frac{h_{\mathsf{epoch}}}{s_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{c/\epsilon}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot s_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{c/\epsilon}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{3c\epsilon}}\cdot DLSC_{G_{\mathsf{epoch},j-1}}\left(2\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}+1}\cdot n^{2c\epsilon},2h_{\mathsf{epoch}+1},\frac{s_{\mathsf{epoch}+1}-2}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{|A|}{n^{3c\epsilon}}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{n^{c\sqrt{\epsilon}}}\right)^{\mathsf{epoch}}

We have that
(C_{\mathsf{epoch},0},C_{\mathsf{epoch},1},...)
is a sequence of
(h_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}})
-length
\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}
-sparse moving cut of graph
G_{\mathsf{epoch}-1,z}
. Thus, according to
Lemma

B.13
, we get that (remember that
s_{\mathsf{epoch}}>1/\epsilon
and
s_{\mathsf{epoch}}<(1/\epsilon)^{(3c/\epsilon)^{1/\sqrt{\epsilon}}}<n^{\epsilon}
for sufficiently large
n
because of
\epsilon>\Omega(1/\log\log n)
)

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[z]}A_{G_{\mathsf{epoch},j-1}}(h_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}},C_{\mathsf{epoch},j})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle s_{\mathsf{epoch}}^{3}\cdot\log^{3}n\cdot n^{c/s_{\mathsf{epoch}}}\cdot DLSC_{G_{\mathsf{epoch}-1,z}}\left(s^{3}\cdot\log^{3}n\cdot n^{c/s_{\mathsf{epoch}}}\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)},2h_{\mathsf{epoch}},\frac{s_{\mathsf{epoch}}-2}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle n^{2c\epsilon}\cdot DLSC_{G_{\mathsf{epoch}-1,z}}\left(\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot n^{2c\epsilon},2h_{\mathsf{epoch}},\frac{s_{\mathsf{epoch}}-2}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle n^{2c\epsilon}\cdot|A|\cdot\left(\frac{1}{n^{c\sqrt{\epsilon}}}\right)^{\mathsf{epoch}-1}

The last inequality is from the induction hypothesis. Combining the two inequalities, we get

z\cdot\frac{|A|}{n^{3c\epsilon}}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{n^{c\sqrt{\epsilon}}}\right)^{\mathsf{epoch}}\leq n^{2c\epsilon}\cdot|A|\cdot\left(\frac{1}{n^{c\sqrt{\epsilon}}}\right)^{\mathsf{epoch}-1}

This is a contradiction as
z
is as large as
n^{2c\sqrt{\epsilon}}
.
∎

Lemma

D.1
shows that at the end of the algorithm, when
\mathsf{epoch}=1/\sqrt{\epsilon}
, we get (remember that size of
A
scaling up to a integer is polynomial on
n
according to our assumption)

DLSC_{G_{\mathsf{epoch},z}}\left(\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}+1}\cdot n^{2c\epsilon},2h_{\mathsf{epoch}+1},\frac{s_{\mathsf{epoch}+1}-2}{2}\right)<\min_{v}A(v)

Also, notice that according to the update rule of
h_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}},\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}
, we have

s_{\mathsf{epoch}}\leq(s_{0})^{(2c/\epsilon+1)^{\mathsf{epoch}}}\leq(1/\epsilon)^{(2c/\epsilon+1)^{1/\sqrt{\epsilon}}}=O(\exp(\exp(O(\log(1/\epsilon)/\sqrt{\epsilon})))

h_{\mathsf{epoch}}\geq h_{0}/\prod_{\mathsf{epoch}\leq 1/\epsilon}(1/\epsilon)^{(2c/\epsilon+1)^{\mathsf{epoch}}}\geq h_{0}/(1/\epsilon)^{2\cdot(2c/\epsilon+1)^{1/\sqrt{\epsilon}}}\geq h

\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}\geq\phi_{0}/\left(n^{5c\epsilon}\right)^{1/\sqrt{\epsilon}}\geq\phi

Thus, according to the monotonicity
Lemma

A.7
, by setting
s=\exp(\exp(O(\log(1/\epsilon)/\sqrt{\epsilon}))
, we get

DLSC_{G_{1/\sqrt{\epsilon},z}}(\phi,h,s)<\min_{v}A(v)

In other words, on the graph
G_{1/\sqrt{\epsilon},z}
, there is no
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut (since demand size of a sparse cut should be at least
\min_{v}A(v)
). Thus,
G_{1/\sqrt{\epsilon},z}
is a
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expander for
A
. Moreover,
G_{1/\sqrt{\epsilon},z}=G+\ell_{1/\sqrt{\epsilon},z}
. If we interpreted the returned cut
C:=\ell_{1/\sqrt{\epsilon},z}/h
as a
h\cdot s
-length cut, then
G_{1/\sqrt{\epsilon},z}=G-C
is
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expanding for
A
(here we again use the monotonicity
Lemma

A.7
). Thus, the returned cut
C
is a
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expander decomposition.

Now analyze the size of the returned cut. Notice that
\ell_{1/\sqrt{\epsilon,z}}
is the summation of
h_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot C_{\mathsf{epoch},j}
for
\mathsf{epoch}\in[1/\sqrt{\epsilon}],j\in[z]
, where
C_{\mathsf{epoch},j}
is a
(h_{\mathsf{epoch}},s_{\mathsf{epoch}})
-length
\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}
-sparse cut according to
Lemma

C.1
. Thus, we have that
|C_{\mathsf{epoch},j}|\leq|A|\cdot\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}
according to
Definition

3.8
. Now we can give an upper bound for the returned cut
C
through the following calculations.

\displaystyle|C|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{h}\cdot\sum_{\mathsf{epoch}\in[1/\sqrt{\epsilon}],j\in[z]}|C_{\mathsf{epoch},j}|\cdot h_{\mathsf{epoch}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{h}\cdot\sum_{\mathsf{epoch}\in[1/\sqrt{\epsilon}],j\in[z]}|A|\cdot\phi_{\mathsf{epoch}}\cdot h_{\mathsf{epoch}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|A|}{h}\cdot\sum_{\mathsf{epoch}\in[1/\sqrt{\epsilon}],j\in[z]}\phi\cdot n^{5c\sqrt{\epsilon}}\cdot h\cdot(1/\epsilon)^{(3c/\epsilon)^{1/\sqrt{\epsilon}}}

\displaystyle\leq|A|\cdot\phi\cdot\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\cdot n^{2c\sqrt{\epsilon}}\cdot n^{5c\sqrt{\epsilon}}\cdot(1/\epsilon)^{(3c/\epsilon)^{1/\sqrt{\epsilon}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq|A|\cdot\phi\cdot n^{O(\sqrt{\epsilon})}

So the cut slack
\kappa=n^{O(\sqrt{\epsilon})}
.

Witness.

We just argued that at the end of the algorithm, the graph
G_{1/\sqrt{\epsilon},z}
contains no
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-sparse cut. According to
Lemma

C.1
, the algorithm must return a witness with length

h_{1/\sqrt{\epsilon}}/s^{O(1/\epsilon)}\geq h

length slackness

s_{1/\sqrt{\epsilon}}/\epsilon\leq s

and expansion

\phi_{1/\sqrt{\epsilon}}/n^{O(\epsilon)}\geq\phi

So it is a
(h,s)
-length
\phi
-expansion witness for
G_{1/\sqrt{\epsilon}}=G-C
.

Complexity.

It remains to argue the complexity of
Algorithm

3
. The algorithm consists of
1/\sqrt{\epsilon}=O(\log\log n)
interactions, each iteration consists of
z=n^{2c\sqrt{\epsilon}}
iterations where each of them contains a call to
ApxDLSC
. According to
Lemma

C.1
, the work for one call is
\mathrm{poly}(h_{\mathsf{epoch}})\cdot m\cdot n^{f(\epsilon)}
and depth is
\mathrm{poly}(h_{\mathsf{epoch}})\cdot n^{f(\epsilon)}
where
f
is a function that goes to
0
as
\epsilon
goes to
0
. Notice that the number of iterations is subsumed by
n^{f(\epsilon)}
. Moreover, since we have
h_{\mathsf{epoch}}\leq h\cdot(1/\epsilon)^{(3c/\epsilon)^{1/\sqrt{\epsilon}}}
and
\epsilon>1/\sqrt{\log\log n}
, we get that
\mathrm{poly}(h_{\mathsf{epoch}})=\mathrm{poly}(h)\cdot n^{O(\epsilon)}
.

The theorem follows by setting
\epsilon=\sqrt{\epsilon}
.
∎
